# Talavakaropanisat

## Talavakaropanisat

A discourse in Kannada by Bannanje Govindacharya

Translation into English by Arya

## Tree Vedas

The Vedas have four schools: Rk school, Yajus school, Sama school & Atharva school. However, essentially the Vedas are in three varieties. That is the reason the Vedas are called Trayi.

What is this Trayi? These three?

Veda in verse form, Veda in prose form, and Veda woven in music.

This verily is Rk, Yajus and Sama. Its verse portion is Rk: prose portion is Yajus and that woven in music is Sama.

However, essentially, Veda scripture has only two forms: prose and verse.

When music joins with prose or poetry, it turns into Sama. Besides these three, the Scripture doesn't have any other form.

The three syllables of “om” speak about these three aspects: “A+U+M”.

In the beginning the “om” existed in seed form. The very “om” sprouted into three Vyahrtis: Bhuh, Bhuvah, Suvah.

The three words of Vyahrti sprouted out and turned into the three lines of Gayatri. The root grew up as branch, turning into three sections of Purusasukta.

The same expanded, growing into a massive tree, became three Vedas. This is the way the ancients perceived the growth of the Vedakalpavrksa, the heavenly Veda-tree.

All the core essences of the grown tree are within the seed, in a subtle form. The essences of Rg-Veda is syllable “A’, syllable “U’ is the essence of Yajur-Veda, syllable ‘M’ is the essence of Sama-Veda.

Combining together, these three core syllables became ‘OM’.

Expanding out, these three syllables became the three Vedas.

A doubt might now arise: why especially ‘A’ is the syllable of Rg- Veda? Why ‘U’ is the syllable of Yajur-Veda? Why ‘M’ is the syllable of Sama-Veda?

All the syllables are employed in all the Vedas. What is so special about these syllables?

The basic clue about the option of these three syllables can be seen essentially within the Vedas themselves.

‘A’ is the syllable of Rg-Veda. That is the reason Rg-Veda begins with ‘A’.

Agnimile purohitam… (1.1.1)

Beginning with syllable ‘A’ ends with syllable ‘I’;

...Yatha vah susahasati (10.191.4)

Thus Rg-Veda traverses from ‘A’ to “I”. This is Rk-Samhita’s ‘Atha’, beginning and ‘Iti’ end.

With ‘I’, the very last syllable of Rg-Veda, Yajur-Veda begins.

Ise tvorje tva… (1.1.1.)

Beginning with ‘I’ ends with ‘U’ syllable.

Samudro va asvasya yonih samudro bandhuh (7.5.25)

Thus from ‘A’ to ‘I’ is Rg-Veda; ‘I’ to ‘U’ is Yajur-Veda.

The next syllable is ‘M’. However, Sama-Veda doesn’t begin with 'M'; doesn’t end with ‘M’ too.

Sama-Veda begins with ‘A’ again:

Agna a yahi vitaye... (1)

Sama-Veda ends also with ‘U’.

Swastika no bespatter-dadhatu (1875).

Since the same mantras of Rg-Veda and Yajur-Veda appear in Sama-Veda too, the appearance of ‘A’ and ‘U’ syllables at the beginning and ending of Sama-Veda is appropriate.

How then is ‘M’ the syllable of Sama-Veda?

‘M’ (mmm...) is a representation of musical note, therefore that is the symbol of Sama-Veda.

Therefore ‘A’ to ‘I’ is Rg-Veda. ‘I’ to ‘U’ is Yajur-Veda. When ‘M’ joins in these ‘A’ and ‘U’ that is, music joins in, it becomes Sama-Veda.

## It was four: became 1137

They divided these three Vedas into four schools.

Three are already there: Rg-Veda in verse form; Yajur-Veda in prose form and Sama-Veda in musical form. Harvard-Veda, compiled by Atharva RSI joined in as the fourth school.

In Rk, Yajus, Sana-Vedas, the name of the Rsi, the seer, is not present. They are adjectival names. Only Atharva-Veda came to be known under the name of the Rsi who compiled it. Atharva Veda is a compilation by Atharva Rsi; of selected mantras from Rk and Yajus.

Vedavyasa again edited these four schools and preached his students in four volumes. The tradition of students continued this tradition and the schools too proliferated into numerous schools and sub-schools.

Rg-Veda has 24 sub-schools. Yajur-Veda has 101. Sama-Veda has 1000. Atharva-Veda has 12. Thus (24+101+1000+12), it became 1137 schools and sub-schools in all.

This number 1137 is also a significant Vedic number. The total of these (1+1+3+7=12; 1+2=3) is again 3.

This verily is the original number of the Vedas. This verily is TrayL Not only this, 1137 can be divided only by number 3. Therefore too it is a Vedic number.

All these schools and sub-schools are not available now. Patanjali mentions in his Mahabhasya, ‘eka-vimsati-dha bahvrc-am’ Rg Veda has 21 sub-schools.

Even at his time 3 had been lost out of 24; 21 had remained. Now 21 are lost and 3 remain; Sakala-samhita, Baskala-samhita and Sangkhyayana-samhita.

Yajur-Veda is of two types; Krsna Yajur-Veda and Sukla Yajur-Veda. One is black Yajur-Veda: another is white.

They called the samhita, in which Mantra and Brahmana sections are mixed, Krsna Yajur-Veda. The unmixed pure samhita text came to be called Sukla Yajur-Veda.

The total sub-schools of these two schools are 101, of these 86 belong to Krsna Yajur-Veda; 15 to Sukla Yajur-Veda.

Of the 86 sub-schools of Krsna Yajur-Veda, 3 are available now. Taittinya-Samhita, Katha-Samhita and Maitrayamya-Samhita. Amongst these 3, only Taittinya-Samhita is more in Practice.

Amongst the 15 sub-schools of Sukla Yajur-Veda, 2 are prevailing: Kanva-Samhita and Madhyandina-Samhita. Thus in the Yajur-Veda school 5 samhitas in total are available now, 96 have been lost.

There is an interesting thing in mentioning that Sama-Veda has 1000 sub-schools. In these 1000 samhitas, there is not much difference in their basic texts. Perhaps some, one or two Mantras may not be in proper order. That is all. The essential difference is in the chanting pattern: one thousand types of chanting; Melody differences.

Among these one thousand chanting styles, 997 styles have been lost. The remaining are the 3 sub-schools of singing styles: Ranayaniya school, Jaiminiya school and Kauthuma school.

Out of the 12 sub-schools of Atharva-Veda, 11 are lost and only one has remained. Thus, 3 in Rg-Veda, 5 in Yajur-Veda, 3 in Sama- Veda and l in Atharva-Veda are the available Samhitas. Out of 1137 Samhitas, presently the available are (3+5+3+1). Only twelve Samhitas.

## Veda: the four faced

Veda emerged four-faced. Samhita, Brahmana, Aranyaka and Upanisat.

Samhita is the core mantra portion of the Veda. Brahmana is the appendix of the Veda. Similar to Harivamsa, the appendix of Mahabharata.

The Brahmana section explicates the Veda and explains its application. It is the very meaning of the word ‘Brahmana’: ‘Brahma’ means colossal; greater than everything. In the cosmos, two are the greater than all: Veda, in the world of words and God, in the world of categories.

God, comprehended by the Veda, and Veda explicating God, both these are Brahmas. While Veda is greatest among words, God is the greatest among the connotations of the Veda.

Now, Brahma means Veda. That which explains its meaning is Brahmana. Brahma+Ana = Brahmana. The very Brahmana, with a change in syllable became Brahmana: brahma anyate jnayate anena iti brahmanam

While the Brahmana section stresses an involvement in life, for those worshippers desiring release from life, Aranyaka, Brahmanas further section, extends help.

Aranyaka means, that which extends assistance for learning and meditating in the forest.

Earlier they were opting forest-life after the householder-life. It was household life, till the children were adults. An aspect of involvement is more present in it. After that, the handing over the home-responsibility to children and leaving for the forest; moving into forest for self-meditation. That verily is Vanaprastha. Over there, the section beneficial to meditation is Aranyaka.

Aranyaka doesn’t mean that it should be studied only in forest. While being a house holder even amidst home life, it needs to be studied. However, Vanaprastha is more conducive to practice it. Thus, Aranyaka does the job of cajoling one away from attachment to life. The essence of Brahmana is Aranyaka and the essence of Aranyaka is Upanisat. It is oriented towards absolute detachment and spiritual practice.

There is a subtle connecting thread between these four faces of Veda and the four Asramas, orders of life:

During Brahmacarya, celibacy; it is the studying of samhita; during house-holder phase, studying of Brahmana; during Vanaprastha, studying of Aranyaka; during sannyasa, studying Upanisats, which is the complete spiritual path.

These are not exclusive to specific persons. All these should be learnt right from the young age. The stages of practice are according to the mental states.

Now let us watch the unfolding patterns of the Veda:

Superficially, an interpretation of the mundane life-practice is mainly seen in Veda.

One example: ‘prajaya pasubhir-yasasa...’

This is a mantra, speaking about the fruits of the Vedic learning. This is its superficial meaning: home, full of children; shed, full of cows; and fame, spreading all around. For this, Is the learning of the Veda needed? Even without studying Veda, children are born.

Thus this is an external meaning. It has another face: In that Praja means abundance of students.

Going still deeper inside, Praja means Prajna, awareness of God. Praja means - prakrstah jayate anaya, that one with which we can climb high. So that the real ‘Praja’ is indeed ‘Prajna’- awareness, isn’t it?

At this level, the word Pasu doesn’t mean Cow. The first prayer of Yajur-Veda is Protect the ‘Pasus animals-of the family patron.

If so, what is the meaning of this?

The one that renders happiness and protects us is Pasu: patni sam-sadhanas-ceti vedah pasava iritah.

Pa = that which protects. Su = that which gives happiness. Therefore the Veda scripture, which opens up the joy of the inner self and protects us ever through the awareness of Atma, is the real Pasu.

This too is like the word Gau, Cow. In everyday life Gau is cow. At the spiritual level Gau is Vedic word. In this way spiritual scripture is Pasu. Spiritual knowledge is Praja. The spiritual joy and the bliss of the inner self, emanating from it is Yasas, the fame.

Upanisat is the one that elevates us to such a height of awareness. Thus, Upanisat is the highest peak in the Veda-mountain, among its core hymns, Samhita, Brahmana and Aranyakas. In the Himalaya of Vedic scripture, Gourisankara is Upanisat.

## Ten to four: heaven’s door

Presently hundreds of works carrying the title of Upanisat are available. But all of them are not Upanisats. A small work named ‘Vajrasuci’ written by a Buddhist monk, Dingnaga, published along with other Upanisats, has become an Upanisat. Some unfamiliar sections of Tantric texts also are published as Upanisats. It has become difficult to decide as to which is an ancient Upanisat and which is a later interpolation.

Among the Upanisats, established as part of Veda, ancients have exclusively considered ten major Upanisats as related to four Vedas. Previously, to propound Vedic doctrine, there was a need to work out commentaries on 10 Upanisats, Brahmasutra and Bhagavadgita. These three form the foundation of Indian philosophy. These are called Prasthanatrayi-three systems.

The essences of all the Vedas are these ten Upanisats. The essences of Mahabharata, all the puranas and finally the essence of all the Upanisats is Bhagavadgita. As these ten Upanisats belong to four Vedas. Bhagavadgita is the Upanisat to Mahabharata. Bhagavad-gitasu upanisatsu.

Brahmasutras are essential to derive the right meaning of all the treatises. Which Upanisat is related to which Veda? How to distribute ten among four?

Related to Rg-Veda is one: mahaitareyopanisat.

In the books published these days, there is a small text called Aitareyopanisat, merely which is a small portion of the. entire Upanisat. In reality it is a combination of two Upanisats.

In Aitareya Aranyaka, there are two Upanisats: Atmopanisat and Samhitopanisat. Both combine together and become a larger Upanisat having nine chapters. This verily is Mah Aitareyopanisat. It has a very significant status among Upanisats. In sixteen Samskaras-sanctifications - there is an exclusive sanctification regarding this: upanisad-vrata.

In Yajur-Veda, related to KrsnaYajur-Veda, there are two Upanisats: related to SuklaYajur-Veda also there are two. Kathakopanisat and Taittiriyopanisat are related to Krsna Yajur-Veda.

In SuklaYajur-Veda one is a small upanisat: Yajniya-mantropanisat. The same is now called Isavasyopanisat or Isopanisat. Another Upanisat is a larger one Brhad-Arnyakopanisat. Both these are related to Kanva samhita.

Related to Sama-Veda are two Upanisats. The small one in it is Talavakaropanisat. The same is called now as Kenopanisat. The larger one is Chandogopanisat or Chandogyopanisat.

Related to Atharva-Veda are three: Prasnopanisat or Satprasnopanisat. Atharvanopanisat, which is now called as Mundakopanisat. Mundakopanisat which is called now as Mundakyopanisat, erroneously.

This way, one in Rg-Veda, two in Sama-Veda, three in Atharva-Veda and four in Yajur-Veda (1+2+3+4=10), altogether there are ten major Upanisats. These ten are the full essence of the four Vedas.

## Singers in rhythm

Now we are to examine Talavakaropanisad, Kenopanisat in common usage, which is related to Sama-Veda.

What does it denote? How did this title come? What is its meaning? when it is called Kenopanisat this problem doesn’t arise. The Upanisat beginning with the word ‘Kena’ is Kenopanisat: kenesitam patati presitam manah.

But this is not its ancient title. Talavakaropanisat is very much traditional, proper title. Talavakara school was probably one of the 997 sub-schools of Sama-Veda, which have been lost. The sages who nurtured that tradition were called Talavakaras.

The Upanisat handed down by Talavakaras is Talavakaropanisat. Like Kathopanisat handed down by Katha sages; like Atharvanopanisat gained by sage Atharva; like Taittiriyopanisat gained by Tittiris.

Why were those sages named as Talavakaras? This word is defined thus: tala-vadya-karan satas-talavakaranacaksate.

The Talavadyakaras are Talavakaras.

Talavadya means, counting of rhythmic unit of verses through palm beats. Presently Sama has tunes: but no rhythms. Sama can’t be sung in beats. Sama can be played on flute; but can’t be played set to mrdanga-drum. Earlier Sama was sung set in beats. Talavakaras brought up such a song tradition.

That tradition is lost now. What a great loss!

But this name provides the proof that among the thousand song traditions such a unique style existed in earlier times. It is not known whether there existed a samhita text for this tradition. But Talavakara Brahmana is available. It is also called Jaiminiya Brahmana. For, the initiator of this Talavakara tradition was sage Jaimini.

The presently available Talavakara Brahmana text has four chapters. The last chapter has twelve Anuvakas - sub chapters. Among them, the entire tenth sub chapter is the Talavakaropanisat.

According to Acarya Sankara, this Upanisat belongs to the ninth chapter of Talavakara Brahmana of Jaimini. Accordingly, this Brahmana must have been very large. Presently, with the rest of the chapters being lost, only four chapters might be remaining.

## Nearness and height

All that is fine. But Upanisat means what? Why did they call the essence of the Vedas as Upanisat? Let us examine the meaning of the definitions of this word.

A number of meanings have come up for this word. Some say this: In it Brahma principle is Upanisanna-staying in. Therefore it is Upanisat: upanisannam brahma-tatvam atrety-upanisat.

It means, Upanisat is that through which we can comprehend Brahman. Some other says: Upa = near, Nisadana = sitting. Knowledge learnt by sitting close to the Guru, is Upanisat.

All knowledge is learnt by sitting close to the teachers. But among such learnings, this one is something special. Without the compassion of the teacher it can’t be learnt. Therefore it is a real Upanisat.

Acarya Sankara says: upanisadayatiti upanisat.

Nisada = end, annihilation. That one which annihilates our pain of life, ignorance is Upanisat. There is another beautiful interpretation. Ancients mention two types of texts: Nisats and Upanisats. Unless the meaning of the word Nisat is understood, the meaning of the word upanisat can’t be understood.

There is a saying in Mahabharata:

yam vakesvanuvakesu nisatsupnisatsu ca

vadanti satyakarmanam satyam satyesu samasu

This sloka tells that Vakas, Anuvakas, Nisats, Upanisats and the music of Sama also sing and praise God.

What is Vaka? What is Anuvaka? What is Nisat? If this is understood, the meaning of Upanisat is certainly understood.

Vaka is Yajur-Veda in prose form. A sentence, appropriate to the metrical form is Anuvaka. Therefore, Rg-Veda set in meters in poetical form and is Anuvaka. The sloka clearly mentions about Sama-Veda set in music. That is about the three Samhitas. Now, Nisat means Brahmanas and Aranyakas. Its excellent core portions are the Upanisats. In this way, this sloka of Mahabharata incorporates the whole of the Vedic literature. Spiritual learning is inherent in Brahmanas and Aranyakas. Therefore they are Nisats.

‘Upa’ means above, excelling, elevated. Therefore, the excellent core of the Brahmanas and Aranyakas is Upanisat. In this interpretation, ‘Upa’ doesn’t mean, near: but elevated. This is the right meaning of this word. Nearness is a subsequent meaning.

The base-word of 'Upa' is ‘Upari’ or ‘Upara'.

‘Upari’ means above, elevated. ‘Upara’ means one who is at the top, one who is elevated. This very ‘Upa’, ‘Upara’ became ‘Up’ ‘Upper’ in English. ‘Upar’ in Hindi. ‘Ober’ in German. ‘Upparige’ in Kannada.

Everywhere the meaning is same: the top.

‘upanisadayati’ - The scripture that elevates us is Upanisat. Similarly the elevated section, good portion in Nisats, Brahmanas and Aranyakas is Upanisat. On the whole, that which rises us intellectually is Upanisat. If we don’t grasp this meaning, we can’t understand the full fineness of the titles.

Upanayana and Upanisats are related. Upanayana-initiation - means a process of elevating a person, a boy. Until then the boy is a carefree, playful, eating, sleeping kid. Through Upanayana the boy climbs up the height of vow of knowledge. This rising process is Upanayana. Elevating scripture is Upanisat.

The person imparting such training is Upadhyaya, the teacher.

Upadhyaya doesn’t mean a person teaching from close quarters.

But there is some interesting thing here: A height is closeness also. One who rises high also moves in closer. The person, who has risen to the height of spiritual awareness, remains close to God. This verily is called Upavasa.

Remaining on the peaks of God-awareness is Upavasa. Staying mentally close to God is Upavasa. To climb that height, easing the belly weight and fasting also is called Upavasa. To fill in the mind, the belly should go empty.

Thus, the Upakhyana - commentary of ‘Upa’ is quite lengthy. ‘Upa’ is also a cryptic form of ‘Upahvara’.

‘Upahvara’ means solitude: place of secrecy.

Upanisat is a discreet learning to be achieved by contemplating in solitude. The most discreet message of the Vedas, the message that has to be protected with high vigilance and care, is Upanisat.

This Upanisat is a section of Talavakara Brahmana, belonging to the Jaimini tradition of Sama-Veda. This small Upanisat is in a form of commentary to Gayatri, which beautifully captures the massive in the small, the cosmos in the atom.

## None of that

Vedic literature emerged from the syllable ‘om’. Thus Veda-scripture expanded and its essence, the Upanisat emerged. Essentially, what kind of awareness does this ‘om’ give us?

We have a tradition: study of Veda or chanting of Mantras should commence with the chanting of ‘om’. And at the end too.

The power of mantra won’t dissipate and gets consolidated, by closing it with ‘om’.

By rule, study of Veda commences with ‘Harih om’. All the further sections of Veda we recite are the expansion of this ‘om’. What is told by this ‘om’, the same is told by the other Vedas too.

Who is propounded by this ‘om’? He is Sri Hari. That is the reason ‘Harih om’ is chanted. Proclaiming the attributes of ‘Hari’ addressed by ‘om’ verily is the entire Veda-scripture. In purana, there is a reference, as an evidence of this:

*vedaksarani yavanti pathitani dvuatibhih*

*tavanti harinamani pathitani na samsayah*

The number of syllables of Veda we read in, is equal to chanting so many names of God. That is the reason it commences with ‘Harih om’. Here is an awareness that, through each syllable, we chant each name of God.

To know a tree, the seed should be known. For the understanding of Veda, understanding of ‘OM’ syllable is essential. When the meaning of ‘om’ is grasped, we get qualified to enter into the Vedas.

Then, what does ‘om’ tell?

‘Om’ propounds the seed-formula of meditation of God: a-u-m.

‘A’ means ‘not’: neti netyatma.

God is ‘not’, what is He not?

He is not what we know, none of that. Whatever our mind can imagine, none of that. For, He is beyond our imagination.

*yato vaco navartante apraya manasa saha*

Speech and mind, they turn back, failing to reach Him.

This is the initial stage of recognizing God.

Why it is so?

Why does ‘om’ say what is not God, instead of telling what is He? Because, that indeed is the method of introducing thorough language.

We can’t familiarize the unfamiliar through words.

First of all, a thing should be familiar. The name of the thing should be known. Then, when that word is heard, that thing is recollected. Therefore, words bring out only the memory of the familiar thing and not the recognition of the unfamiliar.

This being so, how to explain God, unfamiliar to us, through words?

To this, ‘om’ tells: God is ‘A’: ah iti brahma

That Brahman is not the perceived soil, water, fire or unseen air, ether. That is not this. That is not that. Neither Ksara the mortal world, nor Aksara immortal nature is That. Beyond these two the Ksara and Aksara is That.

Our mind is limited. Unlimited is That.

Our mind is created. Uncreated is That.

Our mind is small and imperfect. Infinite and perfect is That. Therefore, our mind can’t grasp It.

This is the first step of Dhyana: first, you understand, that God can’t be fully understood.

This verily is the first stage of God-awareness.

Why God is thus this ‘A’?

As the answer to this, the second syllable is there: Because God is ‘U’.

‘U’ is an abbreviation of Uttama, utkarsa; meaning great, excellent.

It is an abbreviation in Sanskrit language.

Brahma is greater and more enormous than all the phenomena we can comprehend.

It is such an enormous thing, that our mind cannot guess It.

If it is so, why should we care about It?

Isn’t it better to give up worrying about It, instead of wasteful effort to grasp It, which is ungraspable?

Why this funny act of punching the air?

To answer this question, came the third syllable, ‘M’.

‘M’ means Mana, awareness: manam jnanam.

Our understanding doesn’t reach It. It is above our measures of comprehension. Yet we can understand It!

What is this contradiction? Incomprehensible, yet comprehensible.

The very fact that It is incomprehensible, isn’t that also a comprehension of It? The very fact that is above all the phenomena that we understand, isn’t that also an understanding of It?

Therefore ‘om’ tells: Understand that Brahman as ‘A’. Understand it is as ‘U’. Understand it as ‘M’. At least that much is understood, It is above everything; our understanding can’t reach It; this much we have realized.

Therefore, it is true, it is incomprehensible and comprehensible too. Essentially the meaning of both the statements is one and the same. The total gist is this: The entire water of the ocean can not be filled in our small bowl. Whatever is the size of our bowl, that much can be filled up, no problem. The bowl we bring along can be filled up; pitcher or drum can be filled up.

Even after our bowl is filled up, still the ocean remains full!

Ocean didn’t get emptied. But our bowl got filled up. With that our job is complete. We gained the experience of the ocean. However, the idea that one can completely comprehend Brahman is as meaningless, as the effort that we can fill up the entire waters of the ocean in our bowl.

‘Completely filling up is not possible. But no need not to fill up too. As much it can fill up your bowl, so much you fill it up with’ assures "om’ and then rests. Thus, it is an ocean of infinite knowledge and bliss, that our bowls can’t fully contain, But the very search regarding how much our bowl can fill itself with those drops of knowledge and bliss, is the Talavakaropanisat.

## Peak of knowledge and bliss

Each Veda-school has its own Santimantra, recited at the beginning of the studies. Reciting Upanisats after chanting the Santimantra is the tradition.

The following is the Santimantra of Sama-school:

*‘om’ apyayantu mamangani vak pranascaksus srotram-atho-balam-indriyani; sarvam brahma-upanisadam; maham brahma-nirakuryam ma ma brahma nirakarod-anirakaranamastu-aniraka ranam mestu; tad-atmani nirate ya upanisatsu dharmas-te mayi santu te mayi santu; ‘om’ santih santih santih.*

The prayer is, first, for a proper study and proper health of the body needed for the spiritual practice: mama angani apyayantu.

All the organs of my body, like hand, foot and so on, let them become energetic.

‘Apyayantu’ means, flourish, grow strong: paing vrddhau. Flourishing of organs means, their becoming powerful.

What kind of power? The Santimantra of Atharva-Veda explains it:

*bhadram karnebhih srnuyama devah*

*bhadram pasyemaksabhir-yajatrah*

*sthirairangai-stustuvamsas-tanubhir*

*vyasema devahitam yadayuh*

Let us hear good things with our ears: see goodness with eyes: with strong and healthy organs, let us live in such a way that God will be pleased. Thus, our whole body getting tuned to God-awareness is its becoming fully energetic.

Mainly, let the knowledge inducing organs become active:

*vak pranas caksus srotrm-atho balam*

May organ of speech be exclusive for the praise of God.

May breath, energy of Prana, impart new strength for a pious life. May the eye witness good things.

May the ear hear good things.

Most important of all Balam-strength, the mind that imparts strength to all these sense organs, let it reflect only goodness. Let it be strong enough to contemplate on God.

The presiding Deity of speech, Agni, Deity of breath, Nasal vayu, Deity of eye, Surya, Deity of ear, Candra, Deity of mind, Siva and the Deity of strength, senior Prana, may all these stay in my sense organs and impart energy.

Not this or that organ: indriyani ca sarvani.

All the organs, five organs of action and five organs of knowledge be strengthened and assist the practice of spirituality. The word ‘Apyayana’ has another meaning also: becoming happy, becoming joyous. Therefore ‘sarvani indriyani apyayantu’ means may all the organs turn joyous through the learning of Veda scriptures.

All these diligent practices are aimed to understand which thing?

*Sarvam brahma aupanisadam.*

We have proceeded to understand Upanisat. Knowing Upanisat means comprehending Brahman. Because Brahman is Aupanisadam. That alone is the prime meaning of the Upanisat. That is why this preparation of practice.

There are three ways of understanding a thing:

1\) Understanding through Pratyaksa – direct perception.

2\) Understanding through Anumana – logic, inference.

3\) Understanding through Sabda-word, words of those who have understood.

For all these three, Pratyaksa very much is the basic testimony.

In a phenomenon that can’t be perceived by Pratyaksa, logic won’t be helpful. Therefore in Sanskrit, It is called ‘Anumana’ (testimony accordingly).

When logic follows Pratyaksa – direct perception, or Agama -Veda scripture, it becomes the Mana, the testimony.

Anumana is not an independent testimony.

Logic can interpret unseen things in contradictory ways. God is not seen in Pratyaksa. Therefore, Logic too is not capable of proving Him. Regarding God, only Sabda - the word is the sole proof.

That too, what kind of word? It is the Word of those who have seen Him; not of those common persons.

Those who have fully witnessed the Truth, their word is reliable. Upanisat is the words of those experienced sages, who have totally witnessed the Truth. Until we see the Truth ourselves, let us trust the words of those who have seen the Truth. Through that let us also try to see the Truth.

Ancients have warned us that we should believe only after knowing.

The experienced words of the Seers are instrumental in knowing. These words are called Veda-Scripture.

The Rsis of the Vedas proceeded in the path of realization and documented their inner flashes of experience. First, let us study it. Later on, proceeding on the same path, let us also gain such a realization.

Those who witnessed Brahman, how did they see It?

They saw it as ‘sarvam brahma’ everything is brahman.

What does it mean, everything is Brahman? The words of the experienced became a problem in the hands of the inexperienced.

The problem of language is that, at different levels it unfolds in different meanings. When they speak staying at a different level and we listen to it from another level, this problem arises: They tell something and we understand something else.

Therefore when we interpret Upanisats, we should see it from the same level, where the Rsis, who saw the mantras, stood. If we remain at our own level and see it, the meaning will turn to be wrong.

Rsis said ‘Sarvam Brahma’. What does it mean ‘Everything is Brahman’?

This unfolds in two ways.

Everything is a form of Brahman, is one meaning.

In another way, ‘Sarva’ means, capable of everything, omnipotent.

In everyday life also we behave similarly. When we meet an important person we say ‘You are everything’.

By this it doesn’t mean that he is everything in the town.

It only means that the person there, is capable of doing everything. That is why we say ‘You are everything’.

It means ‘You are all powerful’.

In the same Sastras some more interpretations are given:

*sarvesatvat sarvagatvat sarvasya prabhavapyayat*

*sarvasya sarvada jnanat sarvam-enam pracaksate*

He, the God is all powerful. Therefore He is all.

He is all pervasive. Therefore He is all.

He is the creator and destroyer of all. Therefore He is all.

He is all knowing. Therefore He is all.

Gita also speaks the same words:

*sarvam samapnosi tatosi sarvah.*

You pervade everything. Therefore You are everything.

Between these two, which is the right meaning here?

Brahman is all forms or Brahman is omnipotent?

Which one is the right meaning? Not the first one at all.

We began our learning with ‘om’.

It called God ‘A’. It proclaimed for the first time that ‘God is none of these’. What does it mean, ‘Brahman is none of these: but all is Brahman’?

The Santimantra that begins with ‘om', cannot tell a meaning which goes against ‘om’. Let us this sentence itself: sarbam brahma, Brahman means a principle, higher and larger than all. ‘Everything is Brahman’, ‘He is higher than all’! What does it mean?

Therefore it is the second meaning which is appropriate: Sarva means all powerful. Now the meaning of the sentence, ‘sarvam brahma aupanisadam’ is clarified: God is Omniscient, spread all over, the cause of everything, and all powerful. That is why He is Brahma: highest phenomenon of all. The Upanisats eulogies Him in this manner. Such a real awareness of Him is possible only through the Vedas, the sayings of ancient Rsis; especially through the Upanisats, the core of the Vedas.

*nendriyani nanumanam veda hyevainam*

*vedayanti tasmadahur-veda iti*

What do we pray with such Brahman?

*maham brahma nira-kuryam*

*ma ma brahma nirakarot*

I don’t reject Brahman: may Brahma principle too not reject me.

In a hymn of Srisukta, about the fruits of chanting, there is a prayer:

*tvam mam bhajasva padmaksi yena saukhyam labhamyaham*

A devotee prays to Laxmi: I worship you: you too worship me.

Here worship means caring: bhaja sevayam

It is not enough that I love God. God too should love me. Thus love should be in both.

Similarly here too: I should accept Brahman. Brahman too should accept me.

If I study scripture rejecting Brahman, I won’t gain anything from Upanisat. In such a case, there is a possibility that we may not see what is in the Upanisat. What is existing need not be seen. Even if the thing is present, if we don’t have interest in it, it may not be seen.

Ancients called this interest, Sraddha, reverential faith.

Who doesn’t have Sraddha in God, who rejects God, he won’t see God in Upanisat.

In which we don’t have faith, which is not known to us, there is a possibility that it too could be true. Such a faith is very much the root of all knowledge. Through this non-negation there is a possibility of comprehending that which we don’t comprehend.

Therefore, this is our prayer: maham brahma nira-kuryam.

Brahma means the greatest thing among everything. All the things we know are small little things which are impermanent.

Accepting the possibility of the existence of eternal Truth, I commence the study of the Upanisat.

I will never at all reject the existence of Brahman.

Similarly, Brahman too should not reject me.

*Ma ma brahma nira-karot.*

Even as my faith is important for the revelation of Brahman, so too is the compassion of Brahman for it. It is not sufficient that I open up before God; He too should open up before me.

If He doesn’t desire to reveal Himself and keeps Himself closed, however much we try, it won’t be fruitful: yamevaisa vrnute tena labhyah.

I am moving closer to him. But may He not move away. May He not push me away.

I am eager to understand God. Am eager to see Him. Let Him too wish that this small devotee understands and sees Him.

Here, His wish is more important than mine: ma ma brahma nirakarot.

May His blessings be on me: May the flood of His mercy flow towards me.

Stressing the above are the following words: anirakaranam-astu; aniraxaranam mestu;

At any time let there be no mutual rejection.

I don’t push away the Truth.

May the Truth too push me not away.

To gain mercy of God, this body should be purified by devoted practice. About this are these words: tad-atmani nirate ya upanisatsu dharmas-te mayi santu te mayi santu.

The same Truth which was addressed as Brahman earlier, is addressed here as Atman. The word Atman too unfolds in various levels.

For small children Atman means body.

For slightly grown-ups, Atman is the mid part of the body, area of chest. It is the location of Jiva, life. That is the reason we touch our chest when we say ‘I am telling’.

Grown further, we think that Atman means not the mere body. We say that a man is dead. But his body remains as it was earlier. While the person was alive, he had the power to think. There was mind. At this level, the mind within the body became the Atman. Going still deeper it is realized that mind doesn’t think, it is through the mind the thinking is done.

Mind is a machine. The person who employs this machine, who thinks through mind, is a different person. He is very much the living one, the Jiva. He is very much the soul Atman.

The Rsis of Upanisats went still deeper. The living being too is not independent. He is a beneficiary of the machine. But he doesn’t have control over the machine. Who is the sculptor of this ma chine?

Verily, He is the Atman, Supreme Atman and Supreme Brahman.

Thus, the word Atman has a vast spread, from body to God. In the first level, Atman is body. In the final level, Atman is supreme God. Rsis see from this level. We too should see the meanings of their words from such a level. Therefore, here Atman means God, Supreme Atman.

The persons who meditate on God steadfast, are called ‘Atma Niratas’.

Atma Niratas go in for meditations with deep concern in Brahman.

Upanisats speak about their expected conduct. Let such conduct be mine too:

*Ya upanisatsu dharmas ḍṛ mayi santu.*

Dharma means the natural conduct of life. Not an artificial behavior, imposed upon by some external pressure.

Speaking out about things as they exist is the natural Dharma of the tongue. Speaking lies is unnatural. For a living being, which has come from God, moving towards God is the natural Dharma. Rejecting God, living away from God, rejecting God’s creation is a distortion.

Such distorted conduct has become normal for us now!

Therefore is this prayer: May the natural Dharma propounded by the Upanisats be integrated within me.

The reiteration here is to suggest the anxiety and the keen concern in the prayer: te mayi santu te mayi santu.

May those virtues be integrated within me: may those virtues be integrated within me.

The prayer has been that natural conduct be mine, and I move on in such a natural way and realize the true path. Now the cream of the end-prayer: ‘om’ santih santih santih

What is this Santi? Santi doesn’t mean the ‘Peace’, how can we translate it?

None of the translation can capture the sum-sense of the word.

In reality it is not a single word: it is a combination of three words: sa+anta+i

Sa=joy. I=understanding, ‘in gatau’.

Anta=The end of these. The very ultimate is Santi.

It is a chant of the name-trinity.

God is Santi: the ultimate of knowledge and bliss: the very form of infinite knowledge and bliss is God.

My life too, moving towards that totality-as much as my bowl can fill up-be complete and full of knowledge and bliss: be full of knowledge and bliss: be full of knowledge and bliss. So is the prayer thrice repeated.

Why is it reiterated three times?

The phases of life are three: consciousness, dream and sleep.

Times are three: past, present and future.

Worlds are three: Bhuh-Bhuvah-Suvah; earth, sky and heaven.

The basic qualities in the creation are three: Satva, Rajas and Tamas: virtuous, domineering and evil.

Vedas are three: Rk, Yajus and Sama.

The syllables of ‘om’ are three: A, U, and M.

God, who controls the three times, may He fill me with the knowledge and bliss, that won’t be lost for ever.

God who is all over the three worlds, may he fill me with knowledge and bliss.

God, who controls the three qualities, through which the creation, sustenance and Dissolution are controlled, may he fill me with the knowledge and bliss, that are beyond three qualities and eternal. God, who is praised in three Vedas, may He fill me with the knowledge and bliss. He alone is the Santi. Only through him we can attain Santi. God, addressed by the syllable ‘A’ is Santi. God, addressed by the syllable ‘U ’is Santi. God, addressed by the syllable ‘M’ is Santi.

‘om’ santih santih santih

Upanisat begins with the prayer that by the study of this Upanisat, may our life be filled up with knowledge and bliss.

## From head to heart

In the first section of the Talavakaropanisat, there are 9 mantras and in the second section 5. The next 2 sections are in prose. Before entering into the meaning of the upanisat straightaway, there are three things to be known. Rsi, Meter and Deity.

Who is the Rsi, the seer of the Mantra?

Before reading a book, we look about as to who is the author. If the author is well known, we buy the book and read. Similarly, here, remembering the Rsi of the mantra is essential.

Respectfully recalling the tradition of the Rsi that has given us such a unique science is a duty also.

Meter is the second aspect.

Is it verse or prose? If verse, what is the meter? Meter consisting of how many letters? How does it run? Which is its representative Deity and such details.

Thirdly the Deity propounded by the text. Now this text talks about which Deity and which topic?

Before we read any book, we make sure, whether we are interested in its topic. Similarly, here too. It is a scriptural tradition, that before the chanting of a mantra we should recall its Rsi, meter and Deity. With our palm on our head, the Rsi should be recalled. Touching our mouth, the meter be recalled. Touching our chest, the Deity be recalled.

This is the custom of chanting.

Why is it so? Why the head be touched? Why the lips? Why the chest?

It is the external performance suiting the internal concept.

Now the internal ordering is forgotten: mere performance has remained! Mere a play, acting of worship.

The form of internal recollection and ordering is as follows.

Rsi has given us the Mantra. Due to this the heritage of this knowledge has been preserved and sustained. It became possible that we too could get it. Therefore we bow our head to the Rsi and chant the Mantra. Our palm has to touch our head and recall the Rsi.

Representative deities of the meters should make us chant the Mantra clear and faultless. That is why we have to touch our lips and recollect the Deity of the meter.

God’s form propounded by the mantra should be meditated in the heart.

Hence are we to touch our chest and remember the Deity.

## Seven meters and musical notes

There is another reason for understanding the meter. Veda mantras are a kind of musical chants. Sama-Veda, especially, is full of music.

In Sama-Veda there are seven notes – Saptaswaras.

Rg-Veda and Yajur-Veda don’t have seven notes. In them there is a movement of three notes.

There are seven meters in three notes: Gayatri, Usnik, Anustup, Brhati, Pankti, Tristup and Jagati.

These seven meters have seven fixed notes:

Name of the MeterNo of Fixed SyllableNoteDeity1. Gayatri24Sadja (Sa)Swaha, wife of Agni.2. Usnik28Rsabha(Ri)Sanjna, wife of Surya.3. Anustup32Gandhara (Ga)Rohini, wife of Candra.4. Brhati36Madhyama (Ma)Tara, wife of Brhaspti.5. Pankti40Pancama (Pa)Wives of Mitra and Varunas.6. Tristup44Dhaivata (Dha)Saci, wife of Indra.7. Jagati48Nisada (Ni)All Goddesses said above.

Mantras of each meter should be sung in specified fixed notes. If the meter is not known, the fixed note in which it should be sung will also be not known.

Therefore the meter should be known. The representative deities should be understood. By the grace of those deities, the movement of the meter should be understood and it should be sung in proper notes. The words of the mantras should be uttered clearly and perfectly. The deities should bless that there won’t be false notes and wrong words while singing. May they make us pronounce ‘Brahma’ properly, not as ‘Bramha’ from our mouth.

## Who is for Talavakara

Then who is the Rsi of this Talavakaropanisat? Which is the Deity? Which is its meter?

In the beginning of creation, all knowledge emerged from the Hayagriva form of God: hayagrivad-ima vidyah.

Hayagriva is the very first Rsi of all knowledge. He is also the first Rsi of Talavakaropanisat.

Four-faced Brahma, who got the first initiation from God, is the next Rsi: munir-brahmakhilasya ca.

After him in the third stage, garuda is the rsi for all the vedas: garudah sarva-vedanam.

Garudavahana, God riding on Garuda-eagle in the form of Veda scriptures should come closer to us. Garuda should bring Him to the seats in our hearts. Especially to Sama-Veda, Mukhya-Prana and later on Candra are the Rsis: somas-samnam.

What a fine arrangement: Sun is the Rsi of Yajur-Veda; Moon is the Rsi of Sama-Veda.

These Surya and Candra enlightening the spiritual world are the two eyes of the Veda-scripture.

This is the Divine lineage.

Now, for the Rsi lineage of different Veda Schools:

To all the Veda-schools, Rsi Vedavyasa is the basic root.

He preached the four schools to four Rsis: Rg-Veda to Paila, Yajur- Veda to Vaisampayana, Sama-Veda to Jaimini and Atharva-Veda to Sumantu.

Thus, Jaimini is the first Rsi of Sama-school.

After him come Talavakara Rsis.

Thirdly, there come Rsis, connected only to this Upanisat.

This Upanisat is in the form of question and answer.

The question is placed in the first Mantra. The later Mantras are the answer to this question.

The teacher and the student who carried out this dialogue are the exclusive Rsis of this Upanisat.

Who questioned? Who answered?

In the Upanisat, There is no clue, regarding who these were.

Most of the commentators too don’t throw light in this direction. It is a question asked by some student to some teacher, so they say. By saying X student asked Y teacher, not much is gained.

But, Acarya Madhva alone solves this problem, quoting from an ancient scripture:

*vaijayante samasinam-ekante caturananam*

*visnor-vividisus-tatvam paryaprcchat sadasivan*

This is an intimate dialogue between father and son. The questioner is SadaSiva, son of Brahma the four-faced: Brahma, the father, provides the answer.

Therefore Brahma and SadaSiva are the real Rsis of this Upanisat. What they discussed was about Visnu, the father of Brahma. Therefore the advocated Deity of this Upanisat is Visnu. Arriving at the Visnu-principle is the propounded subject.

Thus, this is an Upanisat of the Trinity.

What now remains is the subject of Chandas, the meter.

In the first section there are 9 mantras. Out of it, the first two are in Tristup meter. The subsequent seven are in Anustup.

Tristup is a meter with 44 syllables. In this four-lined stanza, each line has 11 syllables. 11×4 = 44.

Anustup is a meter with 32 syllables. In each line it has 8 letters. 8×4 = 32.

This is a common rule.

Sometimes there are one or two syllables in excess.

Sometimes one or two syllables are less.

If one syllable is less, it is called ‘Nicrt’.

If one syllable is in excess, it is called ‘Bhurik’.

If two syllables are less, it is called ‘Virat’.

If two syllables are in excess, it is called ‘Svarat’.

Let us see the first Mantra of this Upanisat. In its first line, instead of 11, there are 12 syllables: kenesitam patati presitam manah.

45 syllables in total: 12 + 1 1 + 11 + 11 = 45.

It is with one excess syllable, than the given number of Tristup. Therefore it becomes Bhurik Tristup.

The Deity of Tristup is Saci, wife of Indra.

And she is also the Deity of Bhurik Tristup.

We shall see about the further differences of meters, as the occasions arise.

There is also another interesting thing here.

The meter with lines of 11 syllables is related to Rudra.

Eleven Rudras are well known in scriptures. Therefore, 11 is Rudra‘ s number. Tristup of 11 syllables also is Rudra‘ s meter. Here, the meter is related to Rudra: the questioner also is Rudra. This is the uniqueness of this Mantra.

There is another secret here.

It is true, that even with one extra syllable, the meter is not broken. But why should there be an extra letter?

Is there any reason behind this?

There is. It is a special worship, with the meter.

With the excess of one syllable, it becomes 45 syllables in total.

That means 5 lines of 9 syllables. 5×9 = 45.

The line of Brhati meter has 9 syllables.

As Tristup belongs to Siva, Brhati is dear to Brahma and Vayu. ‘Aitareyopanisat’ says: prano brhati.

Here, Brhati is in five lines.

Prana too is five-footed: Prana, Apana, Vyana, Udana and Samana.

Narayana too is five-footed: Narayana, Vasudeva, Sangkarsana, Pradyumna and Aniruddha.

For that reason, Sruti praises Prana and Narayana as ‘pancapadam pitaram’. Converting meters into Brhati and chanting it gives great merits, is one of the messages of ‘Aitareyopanisat’.

The Deity of Brhati is Tara, wife of Brhaspati.

Not only that, Female Deities of all the Vedas, Sri, Saraswati, Bharati are the special deities of Brhati. Garuda known as the seer of all the Vedas, also is a special representative of Brhati.

This is the unique feature of Brhati.

Thus, since this Mantra became one with 45 syllables, which was 44 before, it become Tristup also, dear to Siva and also became Brhati, dear to Brahma.

Therefore, it is a rare meter; representing Siva, the questioner and Brahma, who provides him with the answer.

This is a rare Vedic-worship.

## Section: One

## Mantra: one

God Suryanarayana propounded by Gayatri, abounding with solar energy, controls the world. And Hrsikesa controls living beings, encompassing our physical organs. Upanisat places before us a fundamental question about this cosmic energy:

*kenesitam patati presitam manah*

*kena pranah prathamah praiti yuktah*

*kenesitam vacamimam vadanti*

*caksus-srotram ka u devo yunakti*

(By whose desires, whose prompting, does this mind move towards things? By whose command does chief Prana make the beings breathe? By whose desire do the living ones talk? Which God controls the eye and the ear?)

Here there is a reference to the five organs.

Mind, breath, speech, eye, and ear.

These five are the chief organs, stationed in the head portion.

For that reason, they came to be called ‘Sri’

*ta eta sirsan sriyas sritas-caksus srotram mano vak pranah*

Since these organs are stationed in the Siras, head, they are called ‘Sri’. These are also ‘Sri’ riches of human life.

These are our five riches, five treasures: Eye, ear, mind, speech and breath: caksus srotram mano vak pranah.

The eye that sees good things, ear that hears good things, mind that thinks good things and breath that is specially reserved for a good life.

This verily is the real riches.

This is the wealth that stays on, from birth to death, a God given wealth, a wealth that is in poor persons too. No other wealth is permanent. Today it may be there, tomorrow it may not.

Why did they call only these five the real wealth, excluding hand, feet etc?

Why aren’t they? Hand, feet etc, counted as wealth? Why only these five, eye, ear, etc are the wealth?

Because, these are the five organs, stationed in the head.

These are useful for those, who have right sense.

The eye, ear, nose, mouth and mind are in the head.

In reality the eye, ear, nose, mouth and mind are not there in the head.

A person with right sense understands this riddle.

These organs are in the head; therefore they are called ‘Sirsanyas’ stationed in head.

Yet they are not in the head! What does it mean?

Yes, none of these sense-organs are in this gross-body.

At the time, when Jivas - living beings, leave behind this body, all these sense organs stay with that Jiva. Because, these organs are placed in the subtle body, Lingasarira of the Jiva - the living being.

If it exits in the subtle body, it exits here also. If it was not there, from where can it come here?

All these sense-organs exist basically in the heart.

This gross eye, ear, etc, are its movement channels.

For a blind person, what is the use of wearing spectacles?

Regarding mouth, there are two things:

In it, there is one organ of action and one organ of knowledge. One that takes in and one that gives out.

One organ of taste. The other, organ of speech.

Leaving out the organ of knowledge, that brings the awareness of taste, here the organ of action, which speaks out is opted.

There are two things in the nose also:

One is the awareness of odour and the other is the breathing.

One is knowledge and the other is action.

Here also, giving up the aspect of knowledge, the aspect of action is opted. For us, an awareness of odour is not important. Breathing for a good life is more important. Diligent practice of Pranayama, Dhyana through controlling breath is important.

Similarly, awareness of taste and flavor of food and drinks is an area of food-consumers. It is not such an important thing in the field of Philosophy.

For us, the words are important.

Spirituality is like this: the effort of telling a thing through words, verily that thing which can not be told by words.

This is the big problem of spirituality: yato vaco nivartante.

The thing which can not be reached through ‘words’ and the inevitability of telling about it, only through words.

Even to tell that ‘It can’t be reached by words’, words are very much required.

Thus, speaking through mouth and breathing through nose are two actions. The remaining three are the areas of knowledge.

Awareness of sound is through ear. To understand the words spoken by mouth, ear is needed. To directly see the things heard by the ear, eye is needed. The documenting tool of the knowledge gathered from all the sense-organs, is mind.

Thus the main organs are five.

They are in the head, the important part of the body.

Existing in the head is in itself not a big thing.

There is no fast rule, that those who have these organs have a right sense also.

These organs help those who have a proper sense.

Well, there is head; there are five organs also in the head. What is the discussion about these organs?

What is the problem posed by SadaSiva, the Rsi of the Upanisat, to Brahma?

It is the basic problem that haunted the whole humanity.

Here is a clue, as to where some ‘Westerners’ failed in understanding the Vedic-Scriptures:

They say the following about the Vayusukta, Agnisukta, and Varunasukta etc. etc. in the Vedas.

Ancient Indians during their uncivilized life in jungles evolved the Vedas. They were in forests, lived in thatched shacks. There was storm and the shacks blow away. The surrounding plants and trees fell away.

Those people thought that Vayu, the ‘wind-God’ was angry. They sang and praised the ‘Wind-God’ with Vayusukta.

A wild fire spread out, burning out the forest into ashes. Those living there thought that Agni, the ‘Fire-God’ was angry. They sang and praised Fire-God with Agnisukta.

It rained and there was flood. Everything was washed away. All their belongings drowned in the water. They thought that Varuna or Parjanya, the ‘rain-God’, was angry. They sang and praised the Rain-God with Varunasukta, and Parjanyasukta.

Thus those ‘Westerners’ asserted that Veda-literature was formed due to fear and natural calamities.

Some of our ‘Westernized scholars’ also believed this blindly.

‘Poor people, what else can they do’, who studied unavoidably whatever the westerners wrote, to pass the school exams?

They had not read the original scriptures.

Even if they want to read, they can’t, because they don’t know the Sanskrit language.

Whatever those westerns told, our people believed it to be completely true and became content.

This is the explanation which the westerners presented, literally twisting the texts, as it suited them. Totally unindian concept. This Mantra is a proof of that: kenesitam patati presitam manah...

Let us gaze at our life. Let us think about the functions of our organs, the eye, ear, nose, mouth and mind.

We see with our eyes, hear with ear, breathe with nose, speak with mouth and think with mind.

What is all this? Under whose control is this machine? Who operates all these systematically?

Did we ever think about this? This is a problem that turns complex, the more we ponder about it:

kena isitam presitam manah patati?

‘Isitam’ means ‘wished’. By whose wish does my mind function? Atha kena prayuktoyam...

It doesn’t function according to my wish.

It flows towards directions that I forbid.

It doesn’t go to the place which I desire and sits on strike.

When it doesn’t listen to me, I become helpless in the end and start obeying whatever it orders.

Could we say, the mind works independently, just as it wishes? Anyway it is a machine. A fascinating calculating machine. It doesn’t have its own desire or non-desire.

I say that, it is my mind.

But it is not under my control. I don’t have command over it.

If I am its master, it should have listened to my orders.

Then, under whose command does it function?

Who is the sculptor of this machine? Under whose command does it work?

Prathamah pranah kena yuktah praiti?

The second question is related to Prana, the breath.

Prana means the breathing function. And Prana-principle too, that controls the breathing function.

First, let us consider the breathing. Breathing means being alive.

It is a big mystery that we are breathing. All the living ones tell, ‘We are breathing because we are alive’. Is it so? Are we really breathing? We are not. There is nose and nostrils in the nose. We didn’t create the nose, nor the nostrils, through which breath moves in and out. How can we assess ourselves of it?

Where do we have an awareness of breathing? It is a natural function, working on its own. Ancients taught us Pranayama, so that we should be conscious about our breathing. But we have stopped doing so.

Well, let us assume that we ourselves breathe: prathamah pranah

What is our role in the very first breathing?

What does it mean? I didn’t come here, deciding I must breathe, with this nose.

Prior to this, I was in my mother’s womb. At that time, through mother’s nose, life energy was instilled in me. I didn’t breathe with my own nose.

Whose design is this?

Later, I fell down upon this earth. After this fall, when the umbilical cord was snapped, I started breathing with my own nose, for the first time.

Whose design is this?

Everything is happening in a well arranged way. But we don’t have control over anything. Which is the power that commands all this?

Imam vacam kena isitam vadanti?

Under whose wish do people utter words?

Which is the power that brings about this talk on the tongue?

The words by whose desire and direction, does our tongue speak? This is the third question.

People say, I speak with my tongue.

What an excellent lie!

Animals too have tongue. But animals can’t speak; only the humans do. Why is this difference? Whose trick is this?

Who wrote the first seed-syllable on man’s tongue?

Even among humans, do all speak? Some can’t operate their tongue. Even though some can speak, often the right words don’t come out of their mouth.

Some, speaking what should not be spoken, bite their tongue.

Some others tell that ‘the words to be spoken is inside, it doesn’t come out’.

Why doesn’t it come out, which is in the mind?

Who has given those ideas in the mind? Who is responsible for those words not reaching the mouth?

Some become restless, failing to speak even simple things. Some are gifted speakers, speaking out very subtle ideas of the inner mind, very clearly.

Some search right words for right meaning. In some others, meaning comes rushing, following the words.

Why is this difference?

There is a saying in Purana:

*narayaneti namasti vagasti vasavartini*

*tathapi narake ghore patantityetad-adbhutam*

(The word Narayana is there and mouth is there in our control. Yet the word ‘Narayana’ doesn’t come through our mouth. Why is it so? Thus why do people fall into hell and suffer? Isn’t it astonishing?)

This was Yama’s question.

This same question asked by Yama in the purana, Siva asks in the Upanisat. Both these are the Deities of death.

The same problem haunts them both.

If it is so, the idea that mouth and tongue are in our control is not fully true. Because, they are not functioning according to our wishes. Then, according to whose wish does this machine of speech function? Who makes us speak through our mouth?

There is an episode in Bhagavata:

Dhruva was a child of 5 years. He did penance to see God. Pleased with the penance of the little boy, God appeared before him. Appearing thus He spoke:

‘Ask me, what do you want?’

Then Dhruva spoke:

*yo-antah pravisya mama vacamimam prasuptam*

*sanjivayatyakhila-sakti-dharah svadhamna*

*anyansca hasta-carana-sravana-tvagadin*

*pranan namo bhagavate purusaya tubhyam*

My salutations to you, to such supreme one, the almighty, who entering inside me, make the words hidden in my tongue sprout out: one who infuses energy to all these organs, like the hand, foot ear and skin; one who controls all the energies of the cosmos through His self innate capacity.

Dhruva says: ‘If you stand afar and ask me, what do I want, what can I tell you? You come and sit inside me. Make my mouth speak. Sit in my mind and sprout out my feelings. Sit in my mouth and get the words spoken’.

The same is the problem here also: Our speech is not under our control.

Whose handwork is it?

Similar to our mouth are to our eye and ear too.

Cacsus-srotrsam ka u devo yunakti?

Eye is there, it sees. We perceive.

Ear is there, It hears. We listen to.

But we are not its owners.

Why is it so? Why does it see and why does it hear and some times why doesn’t see and hear?

We don’t know anything.

We are only the beneficiaries.

It is true, we are stationed in this field, the body. But we don’t have the mastery over the field.

Who is the operator, that makes the machine function?

What kind of God is He, who, controlling this eye and ear, makes us see and hear?

Who has imparted to our mind the power of reflection, the memory and forgetfulness?

Who has given us this breath? Who snatches away our breath in the end?

Who implants speech in our tongue and later sprouts it? Which magician makes the eye and ear, see and hear? And blind and deaf too?

Certainly, it is not me.

Therefore, there should be an Omnipotent, who controls all these. The answer is there in the question itself: ka u dev ah?

But who is that God?

To wonder ‘who is that God’, are there a number of Gods?

Not at all. It is known that there is a single God, who controls everything: devah yunakti.

Who is He, is not the question.

Ka u? How is He? What is his greatness? What is His excellence? This is the question, which has to be answered.

Later, while answering, though Brahma speaks about God’s excellence, he doesn’t mention His name.

The name of the God is not the question at all. God can be addressed by any name.

There is no word in the world, which is not an epithet of God.

For instance, let us call him ‘hrsIkeSa’

‘Hrsika’ means-organ. The one who controls it is ‘Hrsikesa’.

Is not Deva also one of his names?

Eko devah: One God. The question of ‘Which God’ arises, only if there are multiple Gods, isn’t it?

If it is so, ‘Deva’ means what?

This word, unfolding in a number of meanings, is derived from the root, ‘Divu’.

Crammer tells that this root is basically employed in 7 senses. Divu-krida-vijigisa-vyavahara-dyuti-stuti & kanti-gatisu.

Modern grammarians add three more meanings: Swapna, Moda and Mada.

‘divyati iti devah’. Divu kridayam.

Even when this world and everything was not existing, the one relaxing in the ocean of dissolution was ‘Deva’.

He, for whom the process of creation, sustenance and dissolution of the world is just a play, is Deva.

Divu vijigisayam.

Who stands above everything, excels everything, is Deva.

Divu vyavahare.

Who conducts every function of the cosmos, without whose prompt ing even a blade of grass can’t move, is Deva.

Divu dyutau.

Who, with his radiant bright body, brightens up the Sun and Moon, is Deva.

Divu stutau.

All the scriptures basically are for whose praise, He is Deva.

Divu kantau.

‘Kanti’ means glow. ‘Kanti’ means desire.

Who brightens up the entire cosmos and whose wishes are ever true, is Deva.

Divu gatau.

‘Gati’ means movement. ‘Gati’ means knowledge.

Who moves all around i.e. omnipresent and is omniscient, is Deva.

These are the few meanings of the name ‘Deva’.

For the present, one meaning alone is sufficient: divu vyavahare.

He conducts every transaction of the cosmos; thereby, the transactions of the organs too.

That greatness and excellence of such a One should be further understood, is the purpose of this enquiry.

Let us see the order of this question:

First comes the mind. After it Prana: following it, the speech and later the eye and ear. In this order of enquiry also there is a new insight. A life is forming in mothers womb. It grows for 6 or 7 months. After this, the fetus thinks, ‘When do I get release from this dark- house?’.

During this, the fetus cannot see with the eye nor hear with the ear. It can’t breathe-through nose, can’t speak from mouth. However, it can think with the mind.

Thus, the mind commenced thinking initially inside the womb, isn’t it? Nothing was there. Just a cell of semen, gets into the woman’s belly, turns into a tiny body in 7 or 8 months. And a tiny ripple of thinking begins in its mind.

From where has it come?

Who initiates the movement, pressing the switch of the mind-machine?

Kenesitam patati presitam manah?

Mind began thinking in the womb itself. When nine months are over, the baby falls down into earth through the vaginal exit. Soon after the fall, the very first function of the baby, is breathing.

First is the functioning of the mind. After it is the act of breathing. That is why the second question comes in the Upanisat: Who is the magician that initiates breathing in the nose?

Kena pranah prathamah praiti yuktah?

After the breathing, the first action the baby does is crying.

First is the action of mind. Subsequent is the action of nose. Third is the action of mouth.

That verily is the third question of the Upanisat:

Kenesitam vacamimam vadanti?

Baby began crying.

Afterwards it opened its eyes. With the open eyes it saw the world.

After this, it opened the ears. Various sounds fell upon its ear. That verily is the next question of the Upanisat:

caksus-srotram ka u devo yunakti?

The baby doesn’t have any idea.

It doesn’t know that it can think with its mind.

It doesn’t know that there is a thing called mind.

Nor does it know that it is thinking.

It doesn’t know that it can breathe through its nose.

It doesn’t know that it has nose.

Nor does it know that it is breathing.

It doesn’t know that sound comes from mouth.

It doesn’t know that it is crying from it.

Neither does it know that with its eye and ear it can see and hear.

It doesn’t know that it is seeing and is hearing.

Everything is new to the baby.

Everything is a surprise, a wonder.

Why only for a baby? For us too, if we refleet.

How amazing! Who sprouted such a power, in that absolutely innocent body of the baby?: ka u devah? Ka u devah?

Who is He? Who is He? How is He?

This is the fundamental question of each enquirer.

The efforts of searching an answer to this question is Philosophy. The efforts of this Upanisat also.

This is the natural question that emerges, when we make ourselves a subject to critical analysis. This is not the question of only the five-faced Siva in front of the four-faced Brahma. This is a five-faced question of the entire humanity.

SadaSiva becomes a mouth-piece to the question of humanity. And that is natural.

Otherwise, Didn’t Siva, the Deity of mind, know about this?

It was known. He asked it, to learn more, from the more learned. It was also to make unknowing persons, know it, that he asked the question.

The unknowing persons don’t know how to ask questions.

Such a question doesn’t arise in them at all.

Those who know things should present questions regarding things, to make the unknowing persons too understand it.

In ancient times, sage Saunaka himself proposed question to sage Suta, Regarding Puranas, to make the students of Naimisa School know It.

By that, the students understood the questions as well as the answers. Kulapati Saunaka’s knowledge became deeper.

Thus, this is the question which SadaSiva asked Brahma, to benefit us. paryaprcchat sadasivah.

A symbolic meaning is also present in it.

Siva is the Deity of mind in all living-beings; Deity of Ahankara-self awareness and ego; Deity of Manomaya Kosa-sheath of mind in totality.

Caturmukha Brahma is the Deity of Citta-memory; Deity of Vijnanamaya kosa-sheath of wisdom.

Mind, Buddhi and Ahankara; collectively form the Manomaya Kosa.

Citta is memory sheath, Sheath of wisdom. In living beings, In the cosmic level it is Mahat-Tatwa, Second principle of creation in the macrocosm.

At first, Ahankara-sense of self – said ‘my eye, my ear’.

Mind got confused and asked, ‘I don’t have control over it. Then how can it be my eye, my ear?’.

Thus, in Manomaya kosa, the question cropped up.

This should be answered by the sheath of memories, Vijnanamaya Kosa or Citta.

This is the answer of Citta to the question of mind.

The answer of Brahma to the question asked by SadaSiva.

There is yet another dimension also, to this mantra.

There is a reference in Brahmasutra text: abhimani-vyapadesas-tu visesanugatibhyam.

Its intention is this: If there is reference to Material things in Veda – Mantras, it is not limited to them. The Deity behind the matter is the real meaning of the word. For instance, let us take the word ‘Agni’.

What is Agni? How to translate it?

The meaning of Agni is not ‘fire’.

Not the inert physical fire that burns wood. Agni means the Deity presiding over fire.

In Sanskrit, in every word referring to inert matter, there is a sense of vitality also. For that very reason, Agni becomes the witness in Hindu marriages. Agni can be a witness to friendship also.

That is the reason why Hanuman proposed the vow of friendship, between Rama and Sugriva, in front of Agni as the witness.

Because Agni is not the inert fire.

It is the vital power behind it. The designating Deity; Divine Agni. Words referring only inert matters are not present in the Vedas.

Mrd-abravit, says Veda.

It doesn’t mean that the mud spoke: it means that the Deity of earth spoke.

Apobruvan, tells Veda.

It doesn’t mean that water spoke; It means that the Deities of rivers spoke.

Acarya Madhva, in the text ‘Sarvasastrarthasangraha’ tells:

*devatavacanannapo vadantityadina vacah nayuktavadi…*

It is absurd, to say ‘water spoke’. Veda doesn’t say like that. It tells that the designating Deities of rivers spoke. Therefore it is not absurd.

During Japa, a mantra is chanted:

*prthvi tvaya dhrta loka devi tvam visnuna dhrta*

Here, earth is not the mere mud. It is the Goddess designating the earth.

Worshippers getting up in the morning offer prayers to the earth.

*bhumidevi mahabhage visnupriyatame subhe*

Here ‘earth’ is not a mere Deity of earth; it is also the spouse of Lord Visnu.

There is a reference in Brhadaranyaka:

*yah prthivyam tisthan prthivya antaro yam prthivi na veda...*

(God is controlling, staying very much inside the earth. But the earth doesn’t know Him.)

Here ‘earth’ is not a mere inert thing. If it was so, there is no reason to say that earth doesn’t know Him. For, there is no possibility of knowing, in an inert thing.

Here the intention is; even the one, which is capable to know doesn’t know it.

We say, a boy is called ‘dumb boy’, when he doesn’t learn well. But no one calls a tree a ‘dumb tree’.

Similarly it is here. Earth doesn’t mean mere mud. Earth is a Divine power and so are water, fire and so on. To those Divine powers too, it was not possible to fully comprehend the God, residing inside them. What then can be the plight of others! - This is the intent of the Upanisat.

Now, with this added dimension, the Mantra unfolds in a different sense. Here ‘Manah’ doesn’t mean the inert mind, a sensing machine.

It is the Deity controlling it.

Who is the Deity of mind?

There are many.

Mainly, Siva is its Deity. Sesa and Garuda also.

Indra and Kama are the subsequent ones.

Next are Aniruddha and Brhaspati.

Then comes Candra.

Then comes Agni.

The last one is Parjanya.

It is generally known that Siva is the Deity of mind.

Here also Siva becomes important, because this question is asked by Siva himself: kenesitam patati presitam manah.

The supreme Deity that controls Siva, the Deity of mind, what is Its greatness? The full context of the question is the following:

Mind is an inert machine. It can’t think by itself. It requires the touch of the vital.

It is indeed with soul. But the problem is not over. The soul doesn’t have the required mastery over the mind. It is just a beneficiary of mind.

Therefore a controller is required, to press the switch of the mind and make it function. Such a controller is Siva.

This is an already known thing. But Siva says: I am very much the Diety of mind. But, I too am not totally free. There is a super power, which controls me and my mind also. Please explain its greatness and excellence.

Mind is Adhibhuta – material.

Soul is Adhyatma – animate.

Siva is connected to Adhidaiva – Devine.

Controller of all these is God. Adhiyajna - the ultimate.

Krsna tells the same in Gita: adhiyajnohamevatra dehe...

Respective Adhidaivas control, the separate parts of the machine. Who then is the President, absolutely controlling the entire machine?

To this question Krsna answers: adhiyajnoham.

I alone am the ultimate Presiding force.

If we are not aware of the National President, there is no use in knowing the village chairman or municipal president.

The latter are mere obeyers of the orders of the National President.

Those obeying the orders are now familiar. Now the Supreme commander who commands should be known.

The second line of the Mantra:

*kena pranah prathamah praiti yuktah*

The Deity controlling the breathing is Pravaha Vayu and Aham- Prana also.

Below them there are Prana, Apana, Vyana, Udana, and Samana known as Panca Pranas. Controlling all these is, Mukhya Prana - Prime Prana: prathamah pranah.

Tell about the super Divine, who controls and imparts breath even to mukhya prana. While, Garuda, Rudra and Sesa are the Deities of mind, Brahma and Vayu are the Deities of breathing.

Now the question has reached the peak. Siva asks:

I am the Deity of mind. You are the father who created me also.

You who controls the breath, tell me about the Father, who ereated you also.

Brahma is the designating divinity of Mahattatva – the second principle in creation.

Siva is the designating divinity of Aharikara tatva, the third principle of creation.

Ahankaratatva was born from Mahattatva.

Siva was born from Brahma.

In ‘Dvadasa stotra’ there is this reference: brahmanah pitaram vande. Sankarasya pitamaham.

(Salutations to father of Brahma, the Grand father of Siva).

Siva is the senior son of Vayu also.

In Brahmandapurana it is told:

*ya marutad garbhamadhatta purvam*

*sesam suparnam girisam surendram*

*caturmukhabhanscaturan kumaran*

*sa draupadi nama babhuva bhumau*

Caturmukha Brahma tells Narada these words: ‘Bharat! got four sons from senior Vayu; They are Sesa, Garuda, Siva and Indra. The same Bharat! took birth on earth as Draupadi’. All the four referred to here are Deities of mind: Sesa, Garuda, Siva, and Indra. All these are the sons of Prana, Deity of breath.

In the previous Kalpa, (1 kalpa = 31thousand million yearsJ.You were the senior-Prana.

In the coming Kalpa, Prana will ascend to your position. Therefore you are the right person to answer my question: tell me about the God who imparts power to such Mukhya Prana also.

The later half of the Mantra refers to three Lustres of the Cosmos- Brahmanda-, which brighten up the entire living-beings.

*kenesitam vacamimam vadanti*

*caksuhsrotram ka u devo yunakti*

Agni is the presiding Deity of the speech organ.

Sun is the Deity of eye.

Moon is the Deity of ear.

This is a trinity of Deities: Sun, Moon and Fire. This is also a triangle in Sricakra - holy graphic design.

By speaking through mouth, hearing with ear, Agni and Candra brighten the lustre of speech.

By directly seeing things, which are heard, Sun, the Deity of eye, enlightens the understanding.

In darkness, sound itself is light.

In the dark, through sound, we recognize things which aren’t seen.

When sun sets and darkness engulfs the outside, Moon and Fire are the light.

To see the forms, Sun is the light outside.

To see the forms, eye is the light inside.

This is the relationship, between sun and eye, the ear and moon. And the mouth and Fire also.

Agni is the light from below. Light of earth.

For that reason, mouth is in the lower section of the face.

Sun and Moon are the upper lights. Lights of sky.

For that reason, eye and ear are in the upper section of the face.

Starting from east to north respectively, Mitra, Yama, Varuna, Kubera, the Deities of directions, are also the Deities of the sense- organ of hearing. So also is Indra.

Sound doesn’t travel in a straight line. It moves in circles, spreading in all directions. They are like emerging wave circles, when a stone is thrown in water.

The Deities of directions, carry the sound waves all over. They also bring them to our ears and make us hear.

Now let us see the trinity of Agni, Surya and Candra.

Mouth, ear and eye are the three lamp-bowls of light of Adhyatma – related to self.

They are mere machines. The Adhidaivas-inner Deities controlling them are Agni, Surya and Candra. Isn’t finally God, controlling all these Deities the root of all these lights? Tell me about him, who controls all the organs.

In reality, Agni, Surya and Candra are the three images of Astamurty Siva. Vayu also.

Earth, Water, Fire, Air and Space, Surya, Candra and Jiva, the worshipping Soul, these are the eight images of Siva.

Therefore there is a relationship between these organs and Siva.

Yet, his question is not answered.

It is true, I am controlling these organs.

Above it is the control of Brahma and Vayu, Deities of life-luster.

It is not yet over, isn’t it? Tell me about Him, who is beyond you also, so tells Siva.

Here, ‘caksus-srotram’ is a single word, meaning eyes and ears.

When it becomes a compound word, it becomes singular: “caksusi ca srotre ca caksusrotram”.

Even when this word is split into two as Caksus and Srotram its meaning doesn’t change.

## Mantra: two

Siva, the Deity of mind, put forward before Caturmukha, the supreme living being, this basic question, that troubles the minds of all meditators. Caturmukha’s answer to it is the mantra which follows.

That mantra also is in Tristup meter.

As in the first mantra, in this mantra too, there is one syllable in excess.

Therefore this too is Bhurik Tristup, containing 45 syllables.

This much is the difference: in the first line of the previous mantra, there Was one extra syllable (12+11 + 11 + 11=45). In the second line of this mantra it is (l 1 + 12+11 + 11=45).

Thus, like the previous mantra, this too is Brhati with 5 lines.

The meter of Siva became the meter of Brahma.

Now, let us see the line of Brahma’s answer:

*srotrasya srotram manaso mano yad*

*vaco ha vacam sa u pranasya dhirah*

*caksusascaksur-atimucya pranah*

*pratyasmal-lokadamrta bhavanti*

(Who gave the power of hearing to the ear, the power of thinking to the mind, the power of talking to the mouth, He verily is the one, who gave the power of breathing to Prana, the power of seeing to the eye. The brave ones, who understand him, escape from the bondage of body and conquer death).

Mind, breath, mouth, eye and ear, this was the order of the organ appearing in the question.

We have seen earlier that this is the order of the organs emerging in the child.

There is another angle also here:

First of all, the internal organ that reaches the experience of the organs to the soul, is mind.

After it, there is the breathing nose, the central point of life, among the outer-organs.

Next, there are mouth, eye and ear placed around the nose. But, here, the form of the answer is different.

While ear is mentioned at the end of the question, here the answer begins with ear.

First comes ear. After it mind. Next is the speech. And then breath. Finally the eye. This is the given order here. There is a specific reason for changing the order in the answer. This is an order which helps the meditation of a Sadhaka.

Sadhana is of two types: studying Sastras and meditation.

Sastra-study also is of two types: Swadhyaya and discourse.

Swadhyaya is learning from listening to the Guru.

Therefore, the ear is mentioned first: srotasya srotram.

Discourse is also of two types: Self-discourse and discourse with others.

Reflecting again and again is self- discourse, an internal churning of the lessons told by the Guru. It is mental reflection.

Therefore is here the reference to mind as the second one: manaso manah.

Teaching others is Parapravacana discourse with others.

This is the third stage of practice:

For that reason is here the reference to the organ of speech, as the third one: vaco ha vacam.
