Learning from Guru, reflecting the heard lessons, affirming those insights from reflections by teaching it to the students, and retaining those insights firmly in the mind, is Dhyana.

The earlier form of Dhyana is Dharana, holding.

The later form is Samadhi – Supreme meditation.

Mind should be steady for Dhyana.

Prana – breath – should be steady, to keep the mind steady.

That verily is the first step of Dhyanayoga. It is Pranayama.

That is why, in Brahma’s answer, there is a reference to Prana, after the organ of speech: sa u pranasya pranah.

Finally, there is revelation: the state of seeing God, eye to eye.

For that reason, here comes at the end the reference to eye: caksusas-caksuh.

Eye is essentially the inner eye. After it, the outer eye also.

Mere, it seems to be having another intention too.

Moon is the Diety of ear and mind.

For that reason there is reference to those two in the beginning: srotrasya arotram manaso manah.

After this comes three images of God as related to the three worlds: Agni, Vayu and Aditya. Agni in earth. Vayu in sky. Aditya in heaven.

Agni is the Deity of the organ of speech.

Vayu is the Deity of organ of breath.

Aditya is the Diety of organ of sight. That is the reason, these three organs are referred in the same order: vaco ha vacam sa u pranasya pranah caksusas-caksuh.

This is arranging three worlds on our very face.

Mouth verily represents earth.

Nose represents sky.

Eye represents heaven.

The Deity of the earth, Agni, verily is the Diety of mouth.

The Deity of the sky, physical Vayu, is the Deity of nose.

The Deity of heaven, Aditya, is the Deity of eye.

Thus these three illuminate the three worlds of the cosmos.

In our face itself there are the three worlds and the three Deities.

What was Brahma’s answer to Siva’s question?

At the outset, the answer is strange:

That, which is ear to the eye (yat srotrasya srotram); that, which is mind to the mind (yat manasah manah); that which is mouth to the mouth (yat vacah vacam); that verily is nose to the nose (sa u pranasya pranah); that verily is eye to the eye (caksusah caksuh)

What does it mean?

The question is understood. But not the answer.

That is the way of telling things, which are beyond words.

It is not understood at first.

First we must know that we do not know.

Then the mind becomes pure, transparent.

When it is transparent, it reflects things as they are.

This is not a matter of knowing by reading.

It is a kind of spark, that gets refined by reflection.

It is essentially a problem of translating Sanskrit words into another language. Especially the words of Upanisats. The numerous things that the word ‘Srotra’ tells, its translated word ‘ear’ cannot tell.

It is difficult to translate it with one word.

Well, Srotra is not mere ear. Then, what is this Srotra?

To understand it, first, its etymology should be known.

From the root ‘sru sravane’ the word ‘Srotra’ is derived.

It has two types of definitions:

*srnoti iti srotram. sruyate anena iti srotram.*

That which hears is Srotra. That through which we hear, also is Srotra. The first meaning is from ancient definers. The second meaning is accepted by the grammarians also.

The right word for ‘that which hears’ should be ‘Srotr’.

How can it be ‘Srotra’?

There is such a usage in ancient Sanskrit:

Hiranmayena patrena...

Here, Patra is the bowl, with which we drink. And also the person who drinks.

*Patrtvat patram. Srotrtvat srotram-itivat.*

These are the words of the scholar, Vamana Panditacarya.

Samvit sastram param padam, says Bhagavata.

Here also, Sastra means Sastr, one who guides.

Sastradrstya tupadesah… in this reference from Brahmasutra too, Sastra means the guide.

Similarly, Srotra means Srotr also.

In the second meaning, that with which we listen, Srotra also means ear.

In the first meaning, Srotra is not the ear.

Ear doesn’t hear. We hear through the ear. But in the full sense, we are also not the listeners.

How much can we hear, even if we do? We hear only so much, that falls on to the ear. Even in hearing that much too, we are not independent.

Therefore we are not the real listeners.

God is the real listener, ‘Srota’, tells the Aitareyopanisat:

*srota manta drasta desta*

Through which we here is the Srotra – in this definition too, ear is not the real Srota.

True, we listen through the ear. But the ear is not the cause of hearing, it is just an instrument.

It is like saying, Rama killed Ravana with an arrow.

It is not the arrow that killed Ravana. It is Rama who killed. Arrow is a mere instrument.

Here too it is similar. Ear is just a machine. The one controlling it, making us hear through the ear, is God.

Therefore in the second sense also, God is the real Srotra who makes us listen to.

God listens all the time, everywhere, everything. All others hear only that much which God makes them hear.

With this background, let us come to the meaning of the Mantra: srotasya srotram.

We hear with this ear.

But it is not the power of the ear.

God has given that power to the ear.

Therefore, He is the srotasya srotram..

He is Ear to the ear; the one who has given the power to the ear to capture sound.

Now let us see this matter, in the context of Presiding-Deities.

We receive sound from ear, through Moon, the Deity of ear.

Next to that, through Mitra, Yama, Varuna and Kubera, the deities of directions.

Through these deities we hear from ear.

Therefore, they too are Srotras.

But they too are the Srotras, under the commands of God. They are not independent. Therefore they are not the true Srotras. God has empowered them, the deities of hearing, to control the ear.

Therefore, He is the Srotra of Candra and others, Ear to the ear: srotasya srotram.

Through God we hear form ear.

Through God, the deities of ear control it.

This ear can’t comprehend God.

These deities of ear too cannot comprehend Him.

God doesn’t need ear to hear: sa srnoty-akarnah.

With all these reasons, He verily is the real Srotra, Srotra to Srotra: srotasya srotram.

The next topic is: manasah manah

Mind to the mind too.

To clarify these words, the definition of the word ‘manas’, should be understood.

‘mana jnane’, from this root, the word ‘manas’ is derived.

*manyate iti manah, manyate anena iti manah.*

That which comprehends things is Manas – mind.

Through which we comprehend things, that too is Manas.

Both these definitions are applicable only to God.

There is nothing that doesn’t come under His comprehension.

He understands everything. Therefore He verily is the manas: srota manta.

He is the hearer, Srotr of everything. Therefore He is Srotra.

He is the thinker – Mantr of everything. Therefore He is manas. This mind, an inert sense-organ, doesn’t comprehend anything. We comprehend through mind.

That we comprehend through mind is also not entirely right. Mind is not the cause of comprehension. It is only an instrument.

The deities of organs are the causes of our understanding things through mind.

Mind is just a machine.

Deities of sense-organs are the ones who press its switch and make it function.

Who are they? This matter is in the question itself.

Brahma and Vayu are Deities of Citta – memory shell.

Garuda, Rudra, and Sesa are, for, Manas, Buddhi and Ahankara – mind, intellect and self-awareness.

Indra and Kama also. Brhaspati and Aniruddha too. Also, Candra, Agni and Parjanya.

However, how do these control? Why do they command? Obeying whose command do they carry out this responsibility?

Siva also knows this control of the Deities. Isn't he too one of the controllers?

Which is that divine power that commands these deities, is his basic question: ka u devo yunakti.

God is the real mind, that controls the mind organ, and the deities of the mind: Mind to mind: manaso manah.

First let us see the plight of inert mind:

It is an incredible machine, this network of Brain.

A dream computer that has 12 billion circuits.

This mind is a mystery. That even our mind cannot imagine.

Despite the enormous growth of science, the secret of mind is yet to be revealed.

Mind is a question.

An answer to it is another question.

Until the root of the brain is reached, there is no final answer to this question.

Until it is answered, all the development of this external science is like a building without a foundation.

Yes, mind is a mystery.

It is a computer that has documented our experiences, through thousands of lives.

If all its circuits are opened up, there won’t be anything left to understand further. Everything turns transparent.

The tragedy is, we have kept all its circuits tightly locked up.

We are utilizing just a tiny piece of it.

There is a huge heap of riches inside the locked box.

And the key is lost.

What an ill fate!

We are the owners of the buried treasure, and we are sitting in it. But alas, as poor ones, we are stretching our hands to others, begging for money!

In reality, the world of mind is a world of dreams.

Day dream while awake and night dream while asleep.

Let us think a bit about this dream in sleep.

Mind is more active in sleep than while awake.

There is no eye, no ear, no mouth, no hand and no foot.

But everything is there.

Even in dream we see, we hear, we speak and we do everything.

Turning into eye, ear, mouth etc, mind does the function of all organs.

The brush of mind paints all the pictures.

While all the organs of knowledge are asleep, mind alone is awake.

We witness a new city in dream. We wander around the city. We travel in an automobile, in a plane, from place to place. It is all a movie, running in an inner screen i.e. Visuddhicakra, an energy point at the tip of urula.

There are thousands of things inside.

Yet we don’t get the dream we desire.

Someone presses the switch and starts something at random.

*ya esa suptesu jagarti kaman-kamam puruso nirmamanah*

Even when we are asleep, someone is awake and shows these pictures.

It is God who has created such a computer and given it to us.

That is why, He is mind to the Mind: manaso manah.

Mind can’t comprehend Him.

The deities of mind also.

Through Him, we comprehend things by mind.

He verily is the one, who has given that power to the Deities of mind.

But, He doesn’t need mind, an inert machine, to comprehend anything.

For that reason, He is Mind to the mind: manaso manah.

The next topic: vacoha vacam.

Vak even to vak – Speech of speech.

What does it mean?

If it is understood what is Speech, Speech of speech will also be understood.

The word vak, is derived from the root, vaca paribhasane.

*Vakti iti vak. Vakti anaya iti vak.*

That which speaks is Vak. Through which we speak too is Vak.

The organ of speech doesn’t speak.,Through the organ of speech, we speak.

The Deity of speech organ is Agni.

Agni stays in the speech organ and makes it speak.

God is the one who commands him.

Therefore, God is the Vak to the vak – Speech of the speech.

The speech organ cannot describe Him. Agni, the Divinity of speech-organ too.

If at all we can talk something through the organ of speech, it is due to God.

Obeying God’s command, Agni controls the sense-organ. And, God never needs this inert machine named speech-organ, to speak.

Therefore, He is Vak to vak.

Here is an unusual word application: vako ha vacam.

‘Vacam’ is a word in the second case.

Here, the first case is needed: vacah vak.

The one who gives the power to talk to the organ of speech.

The one, who gives to the Deities of speech, the power of controlling the organ of speech.

Where is the application of the word ‘Vacam’ in the second case?

The earlier mentioned two adjectives can both be in the first case and the second case: srotrasya srotram manaso manah.

The words ‘Srotram and Manah’ are in neuter gender. Therefore, Srotram can be both in first case or second case. Similarly the word ‘Manah’ also can be in both cases.

Similarly the last adjective also: caksusah caksuh.

Only the remaining two have specific cases:

vacah vacam, pranasya pranah

‘Vacam’ is in second case and Pranah is in first case.

For applying all in first cace, ‘Vacam’ comes in the way.

Applying in second case, it is ‘Prana’, that comes in the way.

Why is it so? What is the purpose of this application?

This is a special form of Vedic language: Speaking with changed cases.

Grammar formulas also accept it.

*saptasu prathama satsu dwitiya*

It means: The first case can be used in the sense of all the seven cases.

Second case can be used in the sense of six cases.

We may here change the second case (Vaco Vacam) into first case (Vacah Vak) according to the definite first case (Pranasya Pranah) Or the first case (Pranasya Pranah) into the second case (Pranasya Pranam), according to the second case (Vaco Vacam).

In the first instance the meaning of the Mantra is this:

Yat srotrasya srotram = the one who has given power of hearing to the ear; Yat manasah manah =.one who has given the power of thinking to the mind; Yat vacah Vacam (vak) = one who has given the power of speaking to the mouth; Sa u Pranasya Pranah = He verily is the one, who has given the power to the nose to breathe; Sa u caksusah caksuh = He verily is the one who has given the power of seeing to the eye.

There is a difficulty in the second instance.

How is the application to the second case, where are the verbs to the complements, objects?

The answer is simple: The verb ‘Yunakti’ of the previous Mantra, has to be applied here also.

Now the meaning of the Mantra is this:

Yat srotrasya = the one, who to the Divinity of hearing organ, Yunakti = gives, Srotram = power of hearing by itself, and power to make Jiva-souls hear, Yat manasah = who to the Divinity of mind, Yunakti = gives, manah = power to think by itself, and make Jivas to think, Yat vacah = who, to the Divinity of speech, Yunakti = gives, Vacam = power of speaking itself and power to make Jivas speak, Yat Pranasya who, to the Divinity of breath, Yunakti=gives Pranam(Pranah)=power of breathing by itself, and make Jivas breathe. Yat Caksusah = who, to the Divinity of seeing, Yunakti = gives, Caksuh = power of seeing by itself and makes Jivas see, Sa u (Devah) = He verily is the supreme Divinity, you had asked for.

Well, the meaning fits in alright.

But why in this way? What is its purpose? Why this confusion of changing the case? Can’t it be said with definiteness, in a single case?

There is a specific purpose for this type of usage. The first explanation tells that, God is the controller of sense-organs. But it doesn’t end there.

He is the commander of the Divinities of sense-organs also, to make this point explicit is this second explanation.

Thus, to tell that both types of explanations are intended here is this explication in two forms, one in the first case and the other in the second case.

This case-difference can be analyzed in another way also.

The first three adjectives are in second case: srotasya srotram manaso mano yat vaco ha vacam.

The next two are in the first case: sa u paranasya pranah. Caksusascaksuh.

According to this explanation, the meaning of the Mantra is this:

Yat = the principle which, srotrasya = to the Divinities controlling the ear, Yunakti = gives, srotram = power of hearing and make the organ hear, Yat = the principle which, manasah = to the Divinities controlling mind, Yunakti = gives, manah = power of thinking and make the organ think, Yat = the principle which, vacah = to the divinity of speech, Yunakti = gives, Vacam = power of speaking and make the organ speak, Sa u devah = that supreme Divinity, Narayana, Pranasya = to senior Prana who makes others breathe, Pranah = the one who has given power to breath and make others breathe; Caksusah = to the Divinity of eye, caksuh = the one who has given power to see and makes others see.

Within the third adjective, there is one syllable – ha:

vaco ha vacam.

What is its meaning?

Some say: ‘ha’ ‘havai’, for such words there is no specific mean ing. It is used merely to ornate sentences or as fillers of sentences. But many scholars didn’t agree with this.

What does it mean, there are meaningless words in Veda?

Let alone a word, there is not a single wasteful syllable or affix in Veda, those scholars say.

In this Mantra, it can definitely be said that those words are employed to complete the line.

This Mantra is in Tristup meter and it needs 11 syllables in each line.

Without ‘ha’ syllable also the line becomes complete: vaco vacam sa u pranasya pranah.

In spite of this, by conjoining ‘ha’ syllable, the line is given one extra syllable.

Definitely, there must be an exclusive reason for it.

What does it mean?

‘Ha’ is employed to address.

Like Hello, Hi, in English.

‘Ha’ is employed also to suggest surprise and delight.

Also, to suggest feelings of success, objection and slander.

Like Ha! in English.

Here, one of its purpose is, addressing Siva and calling his attention.

‘Ha’ is Siva’s name also.

Ha is an abbreviation of Íṁra: hanti iti hah.

It is an exclamation of delight and wonderment.

All of a sudden, what is this delight in this adjective? Such a wonderment in it?

Eye, nose and mind are common to all animals.

They too have mental processes, though it is not so much pronounced, as the mind of the humans. So also the functions of Buddhi, Ahankara and Citta - Intellect, self-ego and consciousness. Only they can’t speak is the difference.

Through sound they express their feelings, present some signals too.

But, the beauty of language is the marvel of the human world.

God has kindly gifted to Humans, such a wonderful art of speaking!

The light of language is exclusive to the human race: sabdahvayam jyotih.

The ‘ha’ syllable in, vaco ha vacam, is this exclamation of this delight and marvel.

The next adjective of the organ of talk: pranasya pranah.

Prana of prana.

What does it mean?

To understand this, first we have to understand the meaning of the word Prana.

The word Prana is derived from the root ‘ana’ or ‘ana’: ana gatau ana cestayam.

*Pranati iti pranah, pranati anena iti pranah.* Or,

*Praniti iti pranah, praniti anena iti pranah.*

The one ever moving is Prana.

That one which makes us move is Prana.

We breathe through the nose: by breathing we move: walk around. Therefore, the nose is called the organ of Prana.

But nose is not the cause of breathing. It is just an instrument. Due to whom we breathe, He is Prana.

We call him Mukhya Prana – main Prana, Mukhya Vayu main Vayu.

Who has given him the power to give us breath? That one is the Prana of prana: pranasya pranah.

Due to that God, this nose breathes.

Due to Him, Mukhya Prana, the Deity of nose, breathes.

Due to Him, Mukhya Prana could give us breath.

For Him, nose is not needed to breathe.

That is the reason. He is Prana of prana.

The last adjective: caksusah caksuh.

Eye of the eye.

What does it mean? To understand it, we should know the meaning of the word Caksus.

Caksing vyaktayam vaci, from this root the word is derived.

*caste iti caksuh, caste anena iti caksuh.*

The one, which sees every thing, is Caksuh.

Through which we see, that is also Caksuh.

We see through eyes, so the eye is Caksus.

But the eye is not the cause of seeing. It is a mere instrument. Due to the Deity of the eye, we see through the eye.

Therefore, the Deity of the eye is Caksuh.

God, who has given the power to that Deity to control the eye, is the eye of the eye: caksusah caksuh.

Eye cannot understand God.

The Diety of the eye also cannot understand Him completely.

The Diety of the eye controlling the eye is also under the command of God. The one who has given the power to see, to the Diety of eye, is God.

But God doesn’t need such an inert eye to see anything.

He is the Eye of the cosmos, He sees all; He is ‘Omni-seer’. That is why He is Eye of the eye: caksusah caksuh.

There is a story in Upanisats:

Once there was a dispute among the deities of the organs:

About who is superior amongst them.

Each claimed that he himself was the main one.

All went in for a competition.

The Deity of speech came out of the body. The jiva - living being didn’t die. He became dumb, but remained alive.

The Deity of eye came out, but the jiva didn’t die, remained blind.

The Deity of ear came out, but the jiva didn’t die, but remained deaf.

The Deity of mind came out, but the jiva didn’t die, but remained senseless.

The Deity of breath came out.

The body collapsed. People said ‘Jiva is dead’.

Then, Fire entered its mouth. But the body didn’t get up, didn’t speak.

Sun entered its eye. The body didn’t get up, didn’t see.

Candra entered its ear. The body didn’t get up, didn’t hear.

Siva entered its mind. The body didn’t get up, didn’t think.

Prana entered the body. The body stood up; Nose breathed. Mind began thinking, ear began hearing, eye began seeing. Mouth began talking.

Then all the Deities agreed: ‘We all are minor Pranas. You verily is the Mukhya Prana – Prime Prana’.

As if reminding this episode, Brahma tells Siva:

*sa u pranasya pranah.*

He verily is the one, who gives the power of Prana – breath – to Mukhya Prana. Prana of Mukhya – Prana.

Among the living souls premier soul is Prana.

He is called Adi-jiva – premier soul.

Among the living souls, there is no other soul surpassing him. Brahma, who is answering, also is one aspect of this Prana principle.

He is the presiding Deity of Mahat-tatva – creator of the universe. He is the main principle of the universe.

The principle above this Brahma, controlling him and imparting Prana to him also, is Narayana: sa u pranasya pranah.

All the others are secondary.

This Prana verily is the one who opens the inner eye.

By meditation the inner eye opened.

By meditating, through the inner eye, the eye of knowledge opened.

The eye of knowledge is not an inert organ.

It is a state of soul becoming the eye and seeing. Eye brimming with vitality.

This knowing eye, full of comprehension, can directly perceive God.

Mukhya-Prana, the Deity of the soul opens up this eye.

Prior to all, the one who opened up the eye of this Mukhya Prana, and made him see the invisible, is God.

Therefore, He is the Eye of the eye of Mukhya Prana:

*sa u pranasya caksusah caksuh.*

Siva had asked: ka u devah?

Who is that God?

Brahma replied: sa u devah.

He verily is that God, who controls all the organs and controls Mukhya-Prana also.

The very word ‘Devah’ suggests this.

Devam means the sense organ that flows towards things, and gives knowledge. Knowledge about the things.

Devah means, their master.

*devam indriyajatam niyamyatvena asya asti iti devah.*

There are not many Gods; He is the only master of all the sense organs.

He is Hrsikesa: eko devah.

Why is it so, that he alone is God?

To this also, the answer is the same: sa u devah.

Sah means, one who is far and also near. sarvatra sarati iti sah, sarvam sarati iti sah.

He who knows everything is Sah.

The ‘Sah’ word is derived from the root, sr gatau.

‘Gati’ means existence, and also knowledge.

Sah means, Omnipresent.

Sah means Omniscient.

The one who is encompassing in and out of the world, He is very much inside the living systems and controls them.

He alone fully knows all the states of the world and the living systems: He is Ksetrajna.

The neuter gender of Sah is ‘Tat’- that.

Both mean the same.

Which spreads with knowledge is Tat.

Which moves everywhere with knowledge is Sah.

Its spread is not limited only to this.

Sah means the Sarabhuta, most essential of all.

‘Sara’ means, faultless.

Sara means blissful.

Sara means, full of knowledge.

Therefore, Sah means Saccidananda; truth, wisdom and bliss.

The indweller, full with knowledge and joy and untouched by any imperfection, is Sah.

He verily is sa u.

He is there afar, is there in the circle of sun.

He verily is tat. He verily is depicted by Gayatri Mantra.

He is stated by all the words, being the absolute essence of essences.

All these words, ear, eye, mind, speech and breath, do not denote organs.

Nor they denote the Deities of organs.

All the words denote God, the essence of all these things.

We say ‘coconut’.

Coconut means not the outer skin.

Nor the inner sell. It is the core kernel.

When it is not seen, we call it as skin, the shell of coconut, until the core is known; the shape that covers the core gets that name.

Similarly, here also.

Until the understanding of God emerges, the core of everything, this name is given to the organs and the deities of these organs for whom God is the base.

When the core is understood, all the words become His name, very much.

There is no word in the world, which is not His name. Sah means, Saccidananda, truth, wisdom and bliss. Sah means, the one denoted by all words.

Hey, who could be this Super Divinity?

It has been a pronouncement of mere adjectives.

How should He be addressed?

The very question itself is absurd.

When it is assumed that with whatever word we address, it is His name, the question how he should be addressed, become meaningless.

Yes, all the words are His names.

But aren’t they employed in this world too?

Like saying ‘Hari’.

Hari means Narayana.

Hari means Vayu.

Hari means Indra.

Hari means Surya and Candra.

Hari means Yama.

Hari means Lion.

Hari means Horse.

Hari means Frog too.

When the matter is like this, how to solve the problem?

If you employ the same name to God, which is employed commonly everywhere, how can we get through to God?

We remain at the level of eye, ear and nose. Beyond that and deeper down, we don’t go. Which is His exclusive name, which is not used elsewhere?

To this also the answer is the same word: sah u.

Sah is very much His name, u is also.

Nobody calls any one with these names.

In the list at naming ceremony, these names are not there at all. U means what?

One who is greater than everything is U. u is the mid syllable of ‘om’: a u m.

God who creates the world, staying within Brahma is ‘A’.

Visnu Himself sustaining the created is ‘u’.

God, who destroys the world, staying within Siva is ‘m’. Therefore, Visnu, being within everything, is the cause of the growth Utthana of the cosmos. His exclusive name is ‘u’.

His other name is Sah.

The following reference is in Vedic lexicon:

a - ka - ya - pra - vi - sam - bhu - ma - sa - kha - ha visnuvacakah:

These eleven syllables are names of Visnu:

ṁ, êṁ, ya, pra, vi, sam, bhu, ma, sa, kha and ha.

Therefore Sah means Visnu.

Sah means one who is everywhere.

Visnu also means the same the same thing.

*Vasati iti visnuh. Visa pravesane.*

The one who dwelling inside engulfs everything is Visnu.

*Vevesti iti visnuh, visi vyaptau.*

One who stands spread and envelops everything is Visnu.

Doesn’t Narayana also mean the same?

*Antar-bahisca tat sarvam vyapya narayanah sthitah.*

Since He is the supreme principle, ocean of all attributes, He is Brahman.

Since He is ‘Atata, spread -inside and outside of everything, He is Atman’.

Vedas often employ words like Atman and Brahman.

To further vivify this word: Puranas employ words Visnu, Narayana etc.

‘Ha’ also is another name, in single syllable, of Visnu.

‘Ha’ means Íṁãṁ as mentioned earlier, now it got a new dimension.

‘Ha’ means not merely the one who destroys the cosmos, he is also absolver of the sorrow of bondage.

One who is Íṁãṁ even of Íṁãṁ, the Salvator Hari is the real ‘Ha’. This is suggested in the line having the syllable ‘Ha’:

vaco-hṁ vacam.

If we go back, the line having the word ‘Yat’, manaso mano yat, that Yat also is God’s name.

‘Yah’ is in masculine form. ‘Yat’ is neuter gender.

Like Yat, Tat too is God‘ s name.

*Yati iti yat. Ya gatau.*

That which is everywhere, is ‘Yat’.

That which is wisdom itself, is ‘Yat’.

Since ‘Manah’ is in neuter gender, ‘Yat’ too became the neuter gender. The same became ‘Yah’ in masculine gender in Gayatri.

*Dhiyo yo-nah pracodayat.*

Which, being (everywhere) became ‘Yat’, that very supreme principle alerts the power of knowledge, engulfing everyone’s mind: yat manasah manah.

Now this Mantra became very much a commentary of Gayatri:

*Dhiyo yo-nah pracodayat.*

God who being wisdom itself (Yah), stimulates (pracodayat) the functions of our sense organs, (nah dhiyah).

We meditate (dhimahi) that over spread (tat), greatest (varenyam) light brimming with knowledge and delight (bhargah) of such Suryanarayana (savituh devasya).

The one who sprouts our understanding, he is very much in the Sun circle, stimulating solar energy.

*Sa yascayam puruse yascasavaditye sa ekah*

The power of living system and the solar system is the same. This is the sense of Gayatri. The same is also the sense of this mantra.

Who prompts the organs, eye, ear etc, He is verily ‘sah’.

The one who is there and is everywhere.

The name mentioned in Gayatri is Savita.

The name mentioned here is Sah.

Sah is also an abbreviation of Savita.

Therefore Sah means Savita. Both speak the same meaning.

*Sute iti savita, sung prasave.*

The basic power that created this world is Savita.

*Savati iti savita, gatau.*

By being spread all over the world it is Savita.

*Savati iti savita, su prerane.*

By being in sun, being everywhere, prompting the elemental world and the world of life, it is Savita.

It is Sah also, sute iti Sah.

By creating everything, it is Sah

By prompting everything, it is Sah.

By pervading everything, it is Sah.

Not only this: syati iti sah, so antakarmani.

By being the annihilator of everything it is Sah.

The is the method of Vedic etymology.

Yaska tells that the words should be defined through the resemblance of syllables:

*aksara-samyat nir-bruyat, na caiva na nir-bruyat.*

Before comprehending the mystery of cosmos, you understand yourself and the power that is controlling you.

Which the power that controls your eye, ear and mouth is?

If it is understood, the power that controls the universe is under stood.

Bhagavata tells about this:

*kayena-vaca manasendriyair-va*

*buddhyatmana va-nustra-svabhavah*

*karoti yad-yat sakalam parasmai*

*narayanayeti smarpayet tat*

(The body, speech, mind and the rest of the organs, intellect, consciousness, with these, whatever the Jiva does according to his own nature, he should dedicate it all to the God Narayana, the great)

these words warn us thus: you have a magnificent body, eye, ear, hand, foot, mind, intellect and consciousness.

What a rare fortune!

But it is not the one you bought willingly. It is there in you.

Its acceptance or rejection is not in your hand.

You are not its master.

Don’t say, this is desirable, this is wanted: this is undesirable, this is unwanted. Don’t say it is mine.

Quitting pride and possessiveness, live with detachment.

When it is there enjoy it quietly, don’t get over excited.

When it is not there, don’t collapse.

God has raised the curtain of the great drama of the cosmos in front of you.

Be an indifferent spectator of the drama.

Watch everything, being eyes and ears bewildered.

Don’t have attachment or dislike.

Even the tragic ending of a drama becomes enjoyable.

Why not in the drama of life?

Birth is persona in the drama. Death is another role.

Birth is wonderment, death too is another marvel.

Watch everything with the innocence of a baby.

Then, besides the joy of wonder, there won’t be pain.

Behind each scenario, there is a clever wonderment of the Sutradhara - the play master.

When the drama is over, and the curtain falls, the memory of Him very much remains. Narayanaeti samaprayet tat.

This indifference of the organs, is the path to move towards the Truth, which is beyond the senses.

Who has not witnessed the drama of death, he can’t conquer death.

That act of living without attachment is the crux of spirituality.

That will be the beginning of getting to know the One, who is detached from everything and always.

He verily is ‘Sah’, He verily is ‘Savita’, He verily is ‘Narayana’. A hymn from Rg-Veda says like this:

*ni su sida ganapate ganesu*

*tvamahur-vipra-tamam kavinam*

*na rte tvat kriyate kincanare*

*maham-arkam maghavan citram-arca*

(Oh, Master of living stock, Master of sense-stock, come over and stay in my organs. Amongst the wise, you are the highest peak, they say. Without you nothing happens, afar or near. You are the eldest among all, oh, Master of all riches; you are venerable to all. Replete with knowledge and delight, your power is incredible. Staying inside me, You venerate Yourself.)

It is a rare mantra, chanted at the commencement of worship.

How much is it true to say that I perform the puja?

I am in this house called body.

But it is not my house. I am a tenant on lease. I stay so long as the owner permits. When the lease is over, silent and ungrumbling, I have to depart.

I see and hear, through my eye and ear. I think through my mind. But this is not my eye, neither my ear. Nor is my mind.

My body is not mine; my senses are not mine. My inner mind too is not mine. Therefore, what does it mean to say that I myself am doing things?

Puja materials are in front of me. Small water container, spoon for holy water etc.

Nobody tells that these perform the puja. For they are just tools.

Oh God, I and my organs too are mere tools. I have spoon in my hand and am the spoon in your hands. I too am a mere tool for your puja. Through this tool you worship yourself.

Let me have the good fortune of becoming a spoon in your hand to your puja, performed by yourself.

Let this body, these senses given by God be reserved for the puja of God; water from the tank be poured back to the tank.

This very awareness is the first step of awareness of God.

What is the result of conceiving in this way?

This is explained in the forthcoming section of the Mantra:

*atimucya dhirah pretyasmallokadamrta bhavanti.*

They are Dhiras, who understand thus.

The prevalent meaning of Dhira is, a person of courage, strong willed, adventurous.

Its real meaning is, a person who has tasted the delight of knowledge:

*dhiya ramante iti dhirah*

Awareness of God, who is behind all the powers, is the real ‘Dhi’.

Those who have gained such awareness are ‘Dhiras’.

Those who have relished the sweetness of such awareness are Dhiras.

Not the bookish scholars: but those who have tasted the wisdom.

Those, in whom such awareness becomes experience, and experience becomes delight. Those, who shifting away from the mere language of words, can read the heart of Upanisat, through the language of heart.

They truly are the daring ones, courageous.

When Truth is known, courage emerges.

Ignorance is fear. Knowledge is fearlessness.

We get scared during night. Because nothing is seen, it is all dark, may be there is a snake or something under the feet! May be, thieves, hiding in the dark! We don’t know. So there is fear.

There is no such fear during the day. It is all bright and light. This and that side, and beneath the feet, all is seen. So comes confidence.

What is after death is not known.

So, death is frightening.

The wise say ‘death is beautiful. It is not frightening’.

Fools don’t know that death is beautiful.

It is scary, because it is not known.

Courageous person means, one who knows, who has conquered fear by knowing.

He is courageous who has become strong after knowing, by knowledge.

Upanisat tells:

The courageous, who have become strong by knowing God, gain the state of deathlessness; understand the deep mystery of the cosmos: dhirah amrtah bhavanti. How? Atimucya, asmat lokat pretya.

First, by divesting from: ‘Atimucya’.

Divesting from what?

Divesting from mundane life.

Divesting from the ignorance, which is its cause.

Divesting from the bonds of Karma.

First, divesting by becoming courageous; ‘Dhirah Atimucya’. Becoming still more courageous, by divesting: ‘Atimucya Dhirah’. After that, escaping this world and this body too: asmat lokat pretya.

Mundane life means, wheeling around and around, in these three worlds, earth-heaven-hell.

On earth, Karma is done through body.

That is why it is called Karmabhumi.

By good deeds, heaven is gained. By evil deads, hell. That is the reason they are called places of undergoing happiness and sorrow.

Back to earth, again Karma and again heaven and hell.

It can’t be escaped so long as the body exists.

It is not enough, that this visible body be lost.

In each death, it is lost.

While one is gone, another comes in.

It should be lost in such away, that it does not return.

Besides, if it is gone, is it the end?

There is another subtle body inside it.

A body that remains even after death. The subtle body, that is instrumental for experiencing, in subtle word, i.e. heaven and hell.

Still further inside it, there is another Lingasarira-causal body - which is attached to the soul since eternity.

Divesting it is the real freedom.

Then only is the real release, immortality.

All these things are told in these words: asmat lokat pretya.

First, this world, the earth, secondly the subtle world of heaven and hell.

Therefore escape from this circular run, around these three worlds.

For that, escape form the captivity of these three bodies.

In the second sense, Loka, means the body, Through which can we gain the experience of the external world, that is Loka.

*lokate anena iti lokah, lokr darsane.*

Through body, we gain access to the world. Therefore the body is Loka.

Freed from this body (asmat lokat) the soul becomes immortal. This body means, the visible body, the gross body.

Is getting free from it, enough?

Is it not essential, getting freed from the unseen subtle and causal body also?

For that reason the word ‘Pretya’ comes in.

‘Etya’ means climbing up. ‘Pretya’ means, climbing up very well. Climbing up well so that there is no return.

This is an upward voyage in a straight line, after escaping the circle.

There is a fine meaning to the word ‘Pretya’, in Sanskrit.

It is, one who has gained ‘Prakarsa’, who has climbed high; who is liberated.

‘Prayana’ means liberation. ‘Preta’ means liberated. Yeyam prete vicikitsa.

Therefore, ‘asmat lokat pretya’, means divested completely the bonds of this body, finally.

In saying, ‘fully divesting’ if it doesn’t include divesting from the unseen body also, fully divesting becomes meaning less.

Now the full meaning of the Mantra becomes like this:

Dhirah = the strong ones, who have understood God, Atimucya = divesting the bonds of karma, Asmat lokat = from the bond this body also, Pretya = completely liberated, climbing high, Amrtah Bhavanti = become the ones, who won’t have death.

Those who won’t have death, they are immortals, Amrtas.

What is death, what dies?

Inert thing doesn’t die.

We don’t say, pot died, house died.

Therefore the inert matter does not die. It gets destroyed.

Body too is inert; therefore it gets destroyed, does not die.

In Sanskrit they say, death is Pancatwa, fiveness of body.

What does it mean? How does it become five, which was one earlier.

Earth, water, fire, air and space, these combined together, a body is formed.

When it is destroyed, it becomes five, which was one earlier.

Earth joins earth, water joins water, fire joins fire, air joins air, space joins space. Nothing died. They got separated, which were together and became fragments.

Therefore, body doesn’t die.

There is no death to the vital soul.

Then who dies? What is death?

To the vital soul, contact with five elements and inert body is the birth.

Getting separated from the body is death.

Thus, the soul which doesn’t have death keeps dying, due to the body.

Therefore, when there is no body, the death, which is in the form of separation from the body, too is not there.

For that reason Upanisat tells:

*asmat lokat pretya amrtah bhavanti*

They get liberated from this body, and, since they are without the inert body, they get liberated from the death also.

## Mantra: three

The next seven mantras elaborated the topics told in the second mantra.

Its first mantra; the third Mantra of this section:

*na tatra caksur-gacchati na vak gacchati no manah*

*na vidma na vijanimo yathaitad-anusisyat*

(Eye doesn’t move towards it; speech doesn’t move; mind too doesn’t move; We don’t comprehend. Don’t know how to comprehend it too).

This is in Anustup meter. Having 8 syllables in each line and 32 syllables in all.

There is a speciality in this too: While there was one extra syllable in the previous Mantras, here it is one syllable less. While in the first three lines, there are 8 syllables each, in the last line, there are only 7 syllables (8+8+8+7=31), yathaitadanusisyat.

So that first two words ‘Yatha + Eta’ are split.

If word division is done in this way, in the last line also there will be 8 syllables. Otherwise it is Anstup with one syllable less.

This Anustup, lacking one syllable, is called ‘Nicrt Anustup’.

The Deity of Anustup is Rohini, wife of Candra, and Saraswati, wife of Brahma too.

Saraswati, the Deity of all the scriptures, is a special Deity of Anustup.

This is the speciality of this meter. This is the Meter most employed in Sanskrit scriptures.

This meter is close to the flow of prose, like the flow of normal conversation. This performs the function of prose and the function of verse too. It is a kind of Proverse.

Out of the 700 verses of Bhagavad-Gita, 645 verses are in Anustup meter. The remaining 55 are in Tristup.

Here also, the same two meters are employed. The first 2 are Tristup and the 7 are Anustup.

This can be seen in a different way. Indra is the Deity of Rg-Veda.

Candra is the Deity of Sama-Veda. Sachi Indra’s wife is the Deity of the first two Mantras in Tristup.

Rohini, Candra’s wife is the divinity of the later seven Mantras in Anustup meter.

Therefore, in a sense, Tristup is the meter of Rg-Veda; Anustup is the meter of Sama-Veda.

The combine action of Rk and song is Sama-Veda: rcyadhyudham-sama.

Therefore in this Upanisat of Sama-Veda, the combination of Tristup and Anustup takes Place.

There is a God controlling all the organs.

What is the mode of understanding that super power, which controls Brahma and Vayu also?

As an answer to this comes the third Mantra: na tatra caksur-gacchati.

This is a most amazing Mantra in the entire collection of the Upanisats.

A poetic Mantra that expresses beautifully the deepest form of spiritual experience.

Take it: There is a spiritual person who has witnessed God. An enquirer asks him the following: You said, there is a God controlling everything. How do we understand Him? you are a wise person with divine perception? One who has seen God directly.

You show us too a bit of that God of yours.

Anyone can ask such a question.

But who can answer it?

To question, knowledge is not needed. Stupidity also suffices.

But, even a good scholar, how can he answer?

How to show the invisible as visible?

This is the dilemma of the seeker, the knowledgeable.

Like the dilemma of a dump person who has tasted a sweet dish! How to express!

It is easy to deny experience through inexperience.

But, how to communicate one’s experience to another?

If you say ‘Show God’. How to show?

How can one expect, that all that exits should be visible?

All that exists need not be visible. All that is seen need not exist.

What can parallel the foolishness of those who claim that whatever they see, that alone exists.

In that case, how can those who know, tell the others who don’t know? Na tatra caksur-gacchati.

One can hold the tail of a cow, its horn, and can say it is a cow. But God can’t be shown.

Two things are needed for a thing to be seen.

Such a thing should have a concrete form that our eye can grasp.

It is not enough that the thing has a form. The person wishing to see it also should have an eye. Both are not here: na tatra caksur-gacchati.

God does not have the material form, which this eye can grasp.

The person wishing to see Him has, not the subtle eye that can see Him.

Na tatra caksur-gacchati. Eye does not go there. For, it is not a thing that is visible to this eye. This eye is not the one which has the power to see it.

It is alright, it is not seen. Can’t it be understood by hearing from those who know?

In everyday life too it happens.

We understand visible things by seeing. Those which can not be seen. We understand it by hearing from those who had seen.

You are a scholar, please tell us about God. Hearing your words we will understand.

Brahma tells that too is not possible: na vag gacchati.

Words too cannot touch it.

For two reasons: The thing which is complete cannot be explained fully, with incomplete words.

However much is told, there remain infinite untold things.

Well, if the whole cannot be told, at least a part of it can be told. That too is not possible: na vag gacchati.

It is not possible to familiarize it through words.

This is the very nature of speech.

It is not possible to familiarize the unknown, through words.

Word merely reminds us of what is familiar. That is all.

One who is not familiar with elephant, for him ‘elephant’ is just a word.

A person who has seen an elephant, when he is told, ‘elephant’, the animal indicated by it emerges in front of his eyes.

Thus speech does not familiarize that, which is unfamiliar.

What is the use of chanting His thousand names, to those who don’t have a sense of God at all.

At the most, through speech, can be told what is God. What he is can’t be told.

God is not earth, nor water, nor fire, nor space. None of the things we know.

Then what is He? You have to experience it.

With whichever words It is addressed, It can’t be understood, before It is experienced.

If so, let us experience It, like a dumb testing sweets.

That too is difficult, he tells: no manah.

Mind too cannot touch It.

There is an experience, which is beyond words.

Though it can’t be told by words, It can be understood by experience.

God is not only beyond words, He is beyond experience also. One who is inaccessible to mind also.

The dilemma of telling the things through words what is beyond words is Upanisat.

The struggle of experiencing the One, which is beyond experience verily is the Meditation.

Which is not seen, which is not heard, how can the mind think about it?

Mind chews the things which are grasped by the external organs. It can’t grasp that, which is not grasped from the outside. Whatever be so, its world is mere inference.

That too is not possible here.

Physical mind cannot infer that which is non physical. Incomplete mind can’t infer that which is complete.

In a way here is reference to three instruments of action: kayena vaca manasa ...

What we can see, we can gain it bodily; but it can’t be seen by the eye.

Therefore it can’t be gained by the body.

It can’t be accessed by words, by mind too.

Therefore it is beyond the three instruments of action.

Here eye represents all organs of knowledge.

Speech represents all organs of action.

To the five organs of knowledge like eye etc. it is imperceptible. To the five organs of action, like mouth etc. it is inconceivable.

Therefore, it is unthinkable to the inner intellect. It is absolutely beyond organic experiences.

There are three modes of proofs to understand a thing: Pratyaksa - direct perception, Sabda - word and Anumana - inference.

God is not accessible to direct perception. Because, eye cannot grasp Him. Na tatra caksur-gacchati.

Through the testimony of word too, one can’t understand Him fully.

Because, no word can explain Him completely: na vag gacchati.

The remaining is inference. It simply cannot succeed.

Its name itself is ‘Anumana’ – inference - becomes a testimony, (Mana) when it follows (Anu), direct perception and the word. For that reason it is called ‘Anumana’.

God is not accessible to direct perception and word. Therefore, inference too can not prove Him: no manah.

Manas means reflecting, thinking about a thing and arriving at a logical conclusion. That verily is inference Anumana:

*Nendriyani nanumanam veds hyevainam vedayanti.*

*Naisa tarkena matirapaneya.*

In Taittiriyopanisat, the following is told:

*Yato vaco ntvartante aprapya manasa saha.*

A pure experience is there behind these words. A deep reflection.

In this small line has been filled up the essence of the entire experience of life, has been filled up.

Those who are unware of this experience, talk merely logically, are losing a unique experience.

Because, logic is just a copy of our intellect.

The very boundary of intelligence is the boundary of logic too.

A foolish brain will see only foolish logic.

Only experience can taste this statement. The inner eye should open. It should be seen with deep experience. There is no other way.

The rest should understand, listening to those who have experienced. After understanding, they should experience themselves and see.

The words of the seers is the prime guide. Veda, Upanisats are the words of such seers.

Finally, the mantra opens up, telling about the presiding deities:

*Na tatra caksurgacchati.*

Surya, the Deity of eye, too does not comprehend It.

With the reference of eye, ear also comes along.

Candra, the Deity of ear too does not comprehend It.

Also, Mitra, Yama, Vayu, and Kubera.

Further, the reference of speech comes.

Therefore, bringing along the ear here is relevant.

Isn’t speech through the ear?

Similarly na vag gacchati.

Agni, the Deity of speech, doesn’t comprehend It.

Parvati, also the Deity of speech doesn’t comprehend It.

It is not yet over: no manah.

The deities of mind too don’t comprehend It.

Agni is the Deity of mind also, so too is Candra.

Here, Garuda, Sesa and Rudra are important.

Also, Brahma and Vayu, the Deities of the subconscious, the deep est inner layer of the mind.

Brahma tells this openly, later on: na vidma.

We too do not comprehend it.

Yet, for Brahma and Vayu, it may be possible to comprehend.

That is why there is no mention of Prana here.

In the context of organ, since nose is not merely a sense-organ, in the analysis of instruments of knowledge, Prana or the nose is not included.

You are talking so much about Brahman; therefore at least you must be knowing it. Brahma’s answer to it is bewildering: na vidma.

Brahma tells, I too do not comprehend it.

Brahma, the prime living being, Jivottama, the Deity of all living things and whom there is no other soul superior to, such Brahma tells: I too do not comprehend!

Here is the word: na vidma (The present reading is na vidmah. The old reading was na vidma. Grammatically both are right)

Its meaning is: We do not understand.

Why did Brahma tell, ‘We’ instead of ‘I’?

There is a simple answer to this:

There is a statement in Grammar: atmani bahuvacanam.

Its meaning is: When a person says ‘I’ the word can be employed in plural also.

So the word ‘we’ can be used in the sense if ‘I’.

Accordingly, we do not understand also means, I do not understand.

There is a special significance, in pluralizing word.

Not only I understand, none of us understand.

None of us means, who are all?

There are five beings above Siva, who had put forward the question.

They are, Vayu and his wife Bharati, Brahma and his wife Saraswati, and the wife of Visnu.

All these five know God better than others. But none of these know Him perfectly.

These belong to the Trinity of Parasuklas - ones having pure and pious qualities.

For that reason, they become well-known as Parasuklas.

They too do not understand Him fully.

Therefore Brahma says: we as Parasuklas too can not claim that, we know him completely.

What is known is only a bit. The unknown is endless.

Therefore, instead of claiming, that we know, admitting that we do not know is much more true.

The idea of ‘we’ can be grasped in still a wider sense.

It is true that, we, direct percepients of the Invisible, we all know God a little bit.

But none of us know Him completely.

Thus in toto, this tells about the limits of understanding of all the knowers of the Invisible.

Only those who know can speak like this.

Because, only those who know, know that they don’t know. Those who don’t know, know not that they don’t know.

At least a little is known. Please tell us that much.

Brahma replies to that; na vijanimah yathaitad-anusisyat.

True, a little bit is known. But we do not know how to tell, even that which is known.

Here also instead of ‘I do not know’ again he says ‘we do not know’.

The intention is the same as the earlier. None of us can speak about that.

It is a dilemma of not being able to speak about that which is known. With which word can I speak about that non-worldly experience?

All the word of the worldly languages fail in explaining that principle.

Basically, experience can’t be trapped by words.

My word can’t become your experience.

If you have to know, you only have to experience it.

Therefore Brahma tells: we too don’t know how to explain it to you; how to dictate it to you. Na vijanimah yathaitadanusisyat.

There is another facet to this topic:

In the earlier meaning, ‘Etat’ It is a word having the meaning, ‘Paratatva ’ supreme principle.

In the following meaning ‘Etat’ means organ systems.

How God controls these organs (etat yatha anusisyat).

It’s depth and spread, we do not know.

The total intention of the Mantra can be gathered in this way: The created thing is visible: but its source is invisible.

The power that has given form to this visible enormous cosmos is not seen.

Even the things which have form are not seen by one and all.

There is an upper and lower limit to the perceiving power of the eye.

Eye can’t perceive the forms below a given level. And also the forms above a level. Even children know about infra-red and ultraviolet, which the eye can’t see. Therefore let there not be the stupidly of claiming, that only what is seen is true.

Don’t be stupid, claiming that whatever is not seen with the eye doesn’t exist.

It is not right declaring that, the things which you don’t know, which your sense organs don’t perceive, they don’t exist.

It is not logical, claiming that ‘it doesn’t exist. Because we don’t know it’. That is exhibiting our foolishness.

I have experienced that. I am experiencing the struggle of being unable to explain the experience through words.

Let not the absence of your experience become an instrument of rejecting my experience.

You too try to experience it.

Try to experience. Understand through the experience.

Let there not be empty argument about the things not known.

Dry logic can’t prove anything, except our dumbness.

Here, experience means not the ordinary experience. It means the experience through scriptural knowledge.

There is an outer way to enter this inner world: Veda, Upanisats are there to show us the way.

It is the words of those who had entered it and had seen the truth.

It is the guiding light to those who want to go inside, who are waiting, searching the path here and there, not knowing the right path. It is the road indicator, the guide.

But the scriptures too don’t carry us directly and present us before God.

They are like the name-boards placed at cross-roads, indicating the name and the direction of the town.

We don’t reach the town by climbing the pillar of the name board. We ourselves have to walk up the path which the name board indicates.

Similarly here also. With the help of those who have witnessed God, we ourselves have to move on in that path. On our own we have to reach the goal. We ourselves have to knock the door of Truth.

It is not possible for us to understand any truth or God through some one else’s experience.

We can’t walk with another person’s feet.

We can not think with another person’s head.

That truth can not be discovered from an outside testimony.

This is an important thing. The discovery of truth requires internal vision.

The real truth shines from inside.

As much this inner vision is animated, so much do we get closer to truth.

This enlightening power was animated among the ancient Rsis. They witnessed the truth quickly. We are losing it.

The information section of the brain is becoming overweight, pampering too much of logic.

The other section of intuition, the center of beauty and fine art is becoming inactive.

Because of this, heartless religious blindness, inhuman violence and ruthlessness are spreading all over the world.

Man is losing humanness.

The blind logic of our philosophers is responsible for it; the extroversion of the scientists is responsible for it.

These two joining together are dragging the world towards the edge of destruction.

Though abit delayed scientists have started realizing it.

A good many scientists says: However much scientific research we may undertake, we may not arrive at the truth if we don’t have inner vision.

Inner vision, it is activated, it is there.

Kathopanisat says, the same: avrttacaksus. By closing the outer eye and opening the inner eye, you should see.

We opened the outer eye, closing the inner eye. It didn’t become visible which had to be visible.

We must try again that task of seeing by opening the inner eye. Then we may understand that we don’t under stand.

*na vidma na vijanimah*

This is a beautiful mantra. I doubt very much, whether the inner throbs of man can be expressed in a better way than this. This is a marvel capturing in words, which can’t be expressed in words.

We have seen earlier that there are only seven syllables in the last line.

There is a purpose in adjusting rhythm by lessening one syllable and the stretching the syllables in the meter.

It is a strange Vedic custom; the limitation of the meaning is shown by the act of the word. When we say he came fast, we pronounce those words also fast.

When we say he came slowly, we pronounce the words also slowly and show the act of his slow walk.

We add new meaning to the word, by this high and low pitch of the sound. Similarly in Veda literature also.

This is a cleverness of showing one’s limitation of explaining God, through an absence of one syllables. Yathatadanusisyat.

Now this is a new dimension to the earlier two mantras.

There is one extra syllable, in the first line of the first mantra.

Instead of 11, there are 12 syllables: kenesitam patati presitam manah.

This indicates the added meaning through the extra syllable.

Among all sense organs, the role of mind is prime, which grasps the subjects of all the sense organs.

In this sense mind becomes important.

Mind is the area under Siva’s command. Therefore this is a matter directly related to him. An important matter from his view point.

To show this extra thing this extra syllable is added.

But in reality, among the commanding Deities, Prana is superior to all. In mind also the role of Brahma and Vayu is most important.

That is why, there is one extra syllable in the second line of the second mantra: vaco ha vacamsa upranasya pranah.

## Mantra: four

Another aspect of the effort of understanding a thing through words, which is beyond words is the following Mantra;

*anyad-eva tad-viditad-atho aviditad-adhi*

*iti susruma purvesam ye nas-tad vyacacaksire*

(Different it is from the known; from the unknown too. And it is much more also. Thus we have heard from the ancients, who explained it to us)

There is no confusion in the meter of this mantra. It is in Anustup meter, with the eight syllable in each line.

The Deity of the meter is also like in the former: Rohini, wife of Candra and Saraswati, wife of Brahma.

If we count the third and fourth mantras together, we can see a hidden interesting thing in it.

The previous two Tristups became Brhati lines, Padas.

These two Anustup lines became 7 Brhati lines.

There are 31 syllables in third mantra. 32 in the fourth.

The total syllables of the two mantras are 63.

These form 7 lines of Brhati having 9 syllables: 63 /9 = 7

The number that encompasses the world collectively, is the number 7.

Seven musical notes, Seven worlds, Seven continents, Seven oceans, Seven days, Seven meters. Thus the entire world is enclosed with the number Seven.

The number of Ramayana too is Seven.

Ramayana’s sections (Kandas) are Seven.

Rama is the seventh incarnation of God.

Rama was born during the seventh star, Punarvasu.

That is why there are seven Brhati lines here.

Organs can’t grasp that super principle, nor logic. Thereby it is above words.

This mantra analyses collectively, the topic mentioned in the previous mantra: anyad-eva tad viditat.

It is different from the thing we know.

Then why is this wasteful effort to know, that which can never be known? To this Brahma tells: atho aviditad-adhe.

It is different from that unknowable also.

It is not knowable; not unknowable also.

In this way Adhi = it is understood.

What does it mean?

It can’t be fully known.

It is also not the one, which cannot be fully known. One should understand as much as one can.

Though we didn’t understand, at least we understood that we can not fully understand it.

So the important thing is now understood.

We realized the secret, that it is not easily comprehensible: and not incomprehensible too.

Yes God is an ocean with no limits.

We cannot fill up our small vessel with its entire water.

For that reason we don’t have to turn back with empty vessel.

We can fill up our vessel to its capacity and take it home.

We cannot be the Agastya sage, who had swallowed the entire water of the ocean. It needs not. We can understand the taste of the entire ocean, even by drinking a mere drop of its water.

This mantra sheds light on God’s nature, which is beyond the world, unique, totally different from worldly nature.

Earth, water, fire, air and space, the aggregate of these five became the world.

Everything is a mixture of these five, there is no sixth one beyond these five.

Amongst them also, only three give visible form to things: They are earth, water and fire.

There is earth, water and fire in a rose. In the thorn of the rose too. In the fertilizer at the bottom of the rose also.

This is a visible face of the world. Air and space are the invisible face, the subtle world.

They called the visible world Sat. The invisible world Asat.

Earth, water, fire are visible, therefore they are Sat. That itself is ‘Vidita’ here in this Mantra. ‘Vidita’ in the general sense, means ‘Known’, But here it is not employed in this sense.

‘Sat’ in the general sense, means ‘Existing’. But here it is not employed in this sense. Sat does not mean the thing that merely exists, it also should have a gross form that can be seen with bare eye.

‘Vidita’ means, not the thing merely understood, it is one which is seen with the bare eye.

God is absolutely different form this: anyad-eva tad viditad.

It is not earth; It is not water; It is not fire.

It does not have material body, formed by a mixture of earth, water and fire.

Therefore it does not have odour; it does not have taste; it doesn’t have shape.

Earth can be seen. Water and fire too. But God is not seen.

Fire has a form; there is taste in water; there is smell in earth.

There is no material form in God; no taste; no smell.

*arupmavyam tatharasam nityam-agandhavacca.*

Therefore He is different from these three, which are seen in direct perception.

These three are gross. He is subtle like air and space.

In that case, God is not too different from the worldly things.

He is different form merely such gross things like earth, water and fire.

He too is a subtle thing like air and space.

How is He different from them?

Regarding this is the following expression: atho aviditad-adhii. ‘Atho’ means, and so.

He is different from the seen world; and so from the unseen world also.

Which is this unknown world?

They called the subtle world that can’t be seen with the eye, asat.

Air and space are not seen. Therefore they are asat. That verily is Avidita here.

Avidita really means, that which is not understood. But it is not used here in this sense.

Asat means non-existing. But it is not used here in this sense.

Here, Asat means not a non-existing thing. It means the thing that does not have a visible form.

Avidita means not a thing, which is not merely understood, but not understood through eye.

Therefore air and space are Asat; Avidita.

He is different from this subtle world also.

He is subtler than them and is spread in them also.

There is sound in space.

There is touch in air.

God is non-sound and non-touch. Asabdamasparsam.

Thus he is divergent from the world.

All this world is collective form of these five elements.

He is none of these five; He is beyond them.

Not only that, in power and pervasion He is greater than them: adhi.

There is a third dimension to this Mantra.

Vidita means manifested. Gross world.

Avidita means unmanifested cause; prime-prakrti, basic nature.

From prakrti the manifest world was created.

This world of organic body and the cosmic body, all these are the derivatives of the prakrti.

During the dissolution nothing was there. Only the prime prakrti was existing, while everything was destroyed.

Again during the creation, prakrti was activated and the world started forming.

The first derivative is mahattatva, the awakening of the sub-conscious mind.

From mahattatva and ahankara-tatva, the awareness of the self emerges.

From that emerge the five elements: space, air, fire, water and earth respectively.

The cosmic egg Brahmanda emerges from these five elements. And this earth we live in. In the earth, first plants emerge, then birds and animals, finally human race.

The last creation of the prime prakrti is the emergence of Man.

Thus He is different from this known world of ours, which is the derivative of this prakrti. ‘Anyaveda tad viditat.’

He is different from the unknown (Avidita) prakrti also. The prakrti that existed even during the dissolution, when this seeing eye itself was not there.

Therefore He is different from unknown ‘Avidita’prime-prakrti also. Atho aviditat.

Here the word is employed in a special sense, ‘still more' still further.

Among the pancabhutas also, the last two air and space are invisible.

Space is more intangible than air.

Still more invisible and intangible is prime-prakrti.

We can recognize air through touch. Can recognize space through sound.

But which is the way of recognizing the prime-prakrti? Only through its derivatives can we recognize it.

He is more intangible than this effect world, incomprehensible than the prime prakrti.

He is different even from prime prakrti, which is more intangible and incomprehensible (avidita) from the gross world.

Two causes are needed for the creation of a thing. One is material cause and the other is efficient cause.

To create a pot, clay is needed and a potter also.

While the potter is present and clay is not there, pot can’t be made, also while clay is present but the potter is not.

Clay is the material cause, potter is the efficient cause.

To prepare a cloth, thread is needed and a weaver too.

Thread is material cause; weaver is the efficient cause.

While the weaver is present but the thread is absent, cloth can’t be made. So too while thread is present but the weaver is not.

Material cause in inert. Efficient cause is alive.

Like clay or thread, the material cause of this world is prakrti.

Like the weaver or potter, the efficient cause of this world is God.

In preparing a pot, potter is more important than clay.

It is true, clay verily becomes pot. But the potter’s power of creation gives that form to the clay. Clay doesn’t have its own power. It is inert, inactive. It cannot become pot on its own.

God is more important than prakrti, in the creation of the cosmos. It is true that the prakrti verily becomes the cosmos.

But the God’s incredible power gives the incredible form to prakrti. Prakrti is inert and inactive by itself.

*‘bahu-citra-jagad bahudha-karanat para-saktih’.*

Therefore God is different from prakrti. Not only that he is a superior power than prakrti too, adhi.

‘Adhi’ means Adhika, bigger.

In ‘Adhi’ instead of Adhika there is a significance.

‘Adhika’, bigger-has a limitation.

Father is Adhika than son; but he is smaller than his own father.

One who is bigger than someone else, is also smaller than some one else.

All ‘bigger’ ones are bounded and limited.

Adhika is like ‘greater’ not like ‘greatest’.

God is not so. He is the greatest of all, greater than the known world, greater than the unknown prakrti also. He is boundless, unlimited and infinitum.

Not only greater than the inert prakrti, but active prakrti, ‘Sri’ his spouse. He is greater than everything that exists. There is nothing greater than him.

To suggest this special meaning, the word Adhika comes here as Adhi, deleting ‘Ka’ syllable.

Adhi means Adhipati. Master of every thing.

Adhi means the highest.

Adhi means unparalleled caretaker, holder of everything spreading all over.

Another rare word in this mantra is ‘ Atho’.

‘Tha’ syllable derived by joining ‘A’ and ‘U’ syllables of ‘om’. a+tha+u=atho.

This is a unique worship in the Veda scripture.

A means Anya - the other. Its elaboration is: anyadeva tad viditat aviditat.

A means greater, its elaboration is: viditat aviditat adhi.

U means highest. One who is higher than everything.

This is the explanation of A syllable.

Why is He A? why is He U?

As an answer to this Tha syllable has come in between.

Because He is Tha. He verily is the one, who staying inside every thing controls them.

Therefore He is Atho.

Thus inherently this word ‘Atho’ captures in a nutshell, entire first half of the entire Mantra.

Bhagavadgita calls the known world as ‘ Ksara‘: ksharah sarvani bhutani.

It calls the unknown prime-prakrti ‘Aksara’, kutasthoksara ucyate.

Purusottama is the one beyond these Ksara and Aksara: uttamah purusastvanyah.

Ksara purusa, Aksara purusa, Uttama purusa, this trinity verily is the absolute essence of spiritualism.

This sloka of Bhagavadgita is the discreet explanation of this mantra:

*yasmat ksaram-atitoham-aksaradapi cottamah*

*atosmi lore vede ca prathitah purusottamah*

Now we have understood how difficult it is to understand God.

Prakrti, the basic cause of this world, itself is difficult to understand.

While it is so, God the foundation of this prakrti and who creates this cosmos controlling this prakrti, how can this mind understand him?

Then why is all this talk - business?

What is the proof, that at least you know It, which cannot be known? Brahma replies to such a doubt: iti susruma purvesam.

From the ancients we have heard that this is so.

The total sense of brahmas reply is this:

In reflection varied ideas emerge. They should gain form as experience. They should become concrete as spiritual experience.

Then the perceived vision will never become false.

Thus, Brahma described to Siva, the truth is seen in his inner mind.

But there is a problem here also. Very often, the very illusion of our mind is perceived as experience.

That is why we can’t totally believe one’s experience.

The question remains unresolved also. Whether it is one’s inner vision or a mere dream.

In such a situation, there is a need of an interaction with another’s experience.

If it is a mere dream, it is not possible that all will have a similar dream.

If it is an experience, it is possible all can have a similar experience.

That is why Rsis of Upanisats say: taduktamrsima.

The truth seen by them was witnessed by their predecessors also, therefore you need not doubt, they assure us.

Brahma also gave the same assurance to Siva: iti susruma purvesam ye nas-tat vyacacaksire.

We have heard, our predecessors, who have explained this principle to us, have said in the same way.

They voiced their experience; I heard the same.

And experienced it after hearing, tells Brahma.

In the very resolution of one problem, resides the seed of another problem. Somehow the strength of the experience has been established. But who are those ancestors who told Brahma about their experience?

The prime living one is Caturmukha Brahma himself. Through him only is the creation of all the living systems. He verily is the presiding Deity of all the lives.

Only God was there before him.

This being so, who are these predecessors, who explained things to Brahma?

The answer is also simple: God himself is the predecessor who explained it to Brahma.

This is the advice given by God about God.

Where can we find a more authentic advice than this one?

Svetasvataraopanisat tells the same:

*yo brahmanam vidadhati purvam*

*yo vai vedansca prahinoti tasmai*

He himself is the one who created Brahma before anyone. He verily is the one who preached Veda-scripture to him before all others.

This reference comes in Bhagavata also: tene brahma hrda ya adikavaye.

God is the one, who affectionately preached the Veda-knowledge to Caturmukha Brahma, the first knowledgeable one.

Well, we got the answer. But the problem is not yet fully resolved.

In such a case Brahma should have used the singular.

But Brahma says: Thus we heard from the ancients.

Who are these ancients? Who are these many persons?

The various forms of God.

Brahma has only one predecessor. But that Predecessor has several forms, incarnations. Brahma got the preaching not from a single form alone. He got it from several forms: from Narayana manifestation; Hayagriva manifestation: Hamsa manifestation: thus from several manifestations.

That is the reason the plural case is used: iti susruma purvesam.

The Rsis like Jaimini and others, saw this Mantra, remembering very much the several Rsis previous to them: iti susruma purvesam.

They told their students: Several divinities and Rsis who were our predecessors, have seen this in the same form; after seeing it, they preached it to their students in the same way.

The problem of one plural-case is solved.

But there remains another problem of plural-case.

Brahma tells: iti susruma.

We have heard.

Brahma alone had heard. Then who are these ‘We’?

We have seen the answer to this question in the previous Mantra itself.

It is the normal custom in Sanskrit language, using ‘we’ in place of ‘I’.

This is one explanation.

The other answer found there is also useful here.

At first Brahma, Vayu and Saraswati, Bharat!, these four got God’s preaching and then Siva got it.

On behalf of these senior disciples, the plural-case is employed.

Thus, this is the message given by Purusottama - God Himself.

The message directly heard by the first four persons in the creation.

This is the message of Caturmukha.

The message later passed down to the fifth one, Pancamukha from Caturmukha.

The message later came down to humans from the tradition of Rsis, the message, having no beginning and will be eternal.

## Mantra: five – nine

In the second mantra there was a puzzle-like answer: ear to the ear; mind to the mind etc.

The following five mantras explain those previous five adjectives with further clarity:

*yad vacanabhyuditam yena vag-abhyudyate*

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidam-upasate \|\| 5 \|\|*

*yan-manasa na manute yenahur mano matam*

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidam-upasate \|\| 6 \|\|*

*yac-caksusa na pasyati yena caksunsi pasyati*

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidamupasate \|\| 7 \|\|*

*yacchrotrena na srnoti yena srotramidam srutam*

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidam-upasate \|\| 8 \|\|*

*yat pranena na praniti yena pranah praniyate*

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidam-upasate \|\| 9 \|\|*

(It doesn’t need the help of mouth to speak. But the mouth speaks due to It. Dwelling inside you It is with you. Realize that, That verily is the Brahman. Not this world as worshipped by some people.)

(It doesn’t need the help of mind to think. But the mind thinks due to It. Dwelling inside you It is with you. You realize that, That verily is the Brahman. Not this world as worshipped by some people.)

(It doesn’t need the help of eye to see. But the eyes see due to It. Dwelling inside you, It is with you. You realize, That verily is the Brahman. Not this world as worshipped by some people.)

(It doesn’t need the help of ear to hear. But the ears hear due to It. Dwelling inside you It is with you. Realize that, That verily is the Brahman. Not this world as worshipped by some people.)

(It doesn’t need the help of breathing to be alive. But all breathe due to It. Dwelling inside you, It is with you. Realize that, That verily is the Brahman. Not this world as worshipped by some people.)

The meter of all these five mantras is Anustup.

We have seen earlier about the syllables of Anustup meter and the presiding Deities.

But there are some significant things here. Let us look into them.

In the two lines of the first half of the fifth Mantra there are seven syllables each. And nine syllables in the last line. Totally (7+7+8+9)= 31 syllables.

In the second line of the sixth Mantra there are seven syllables. In the last line.

Totally (8+7+8+9)= 32 syllables.

In the next three Mantras also, there are nine syllables in the last line.

Totally (8+8+8+9)= 33 syllables.

In the first Mantra, one syllable is less.

In the last three Mantras, one syllable is in excess.

Earlier we have seen that if one syllable is less, it is called Nicrt. And if one syllable is in excess, it is called Bhurik. Therefore, the first one is Nicrt Anustup. The last three, Bhurik Anustup.

There is a custom of adjusting the number of the syllables by separating the joint syllables, when the number of syllables are less.

The well known Gayatri Mantra itself is an example for this.

There are only seven syllables in its first line: tat savitur-varenyam.

On adjustment, the word ‘Varenyam’ is expanded into ‘Vareniyam’.

By this it becomes eight syllables.

Similarly, here also, when the words in the first line of the fifth mantra, are separated and spelt, it becomes eight syllables: yad vaca anabhyuditam. By that it too becomes Anustup of 32 syllables (8+7+8+9=32)

Only in the final three Mantras, having nine syllables in their fourth line \[nedam yadidamupasate\] with this one syllable in excess, it becomes Bhurik Anustup.

In reality, all these five mantras, actually become Bhurik Anustup only, with 33 syllables. When recited separating the syllables this

becomes clear. The first half of the fifth Mantra should be split this way:

yad vaca anabhyuditam (8)

yena vagabhi udyate (8)

The later half with 17 syllables, is common to all the Mantras.

The second line, with seven syllables, of the sixth Mantra, should be split as having eight syllables:

yena ahurmano ṅṁḍṁṅ (8).

Now, all the five Mantras remain in Bhurik-Anustup meter only, having 33 syllables.

God’s form that we worship, which dwells inside us, and is smaller than smallest, is the supermost principle, spread all over. To indicate this very much, this excess of one syllable has come in the last line.

When it is felt when saying ‘so much’, the words don’t convey the strong intensity of the feeling, we say ‘sooo much’. We often experience such situations of excess of sound suggesting an excess of meaning. Similarly, here also, the excess of the syllable indicates the excess of meaning.

In another way, these are in Brhati meters.

To see it, let us combine the syllables of all the five Mantras. There are 31 syllables in the first Mantra.

32 in the second.

33 in the rest of the three Mantras.

Thus, in total (31+32+33+33 + 33) = 162 syllables.

Which means 18 Brhati lines with 9 syllables each: 162/9=18

Ordering all the meters by converting it into Brhati, is a special tradition of Veda-scholars.

This is the beauty of Anustup turning into Brhati.

The beauty, that the Talavakara Upanisat showed it so well.

18 is a rare number. Jiva has 15 inert fences: belief, ether, air, fire, water, earth, organs, mind, food, strength, thought, speech, deed, possession and name.
