*pranat sraddham kham vayur-jyotir apah prthivindriam*

*manonnam ann ad viryam tapo mantrah karma loka*

*lokesu nama ca.*

The Jiva living in the bondage of samsara, bound by these fences, is the 16thone.

Prakrti, who has tied this fence around this Jiva, is the 17thone. God, Purusottama, the 18thone, is the one who releases the bonds. Thus, everything is contained in 18. Nothing is beyond 18.

That is the reason why Mahabharata’s number is 18.

There are 18 sections, Parvas in Mahabharata. Mahabharata war lasts 18 days. 18 Aksohini army takes part in the war. Bhagavadgita, the essence of Mahabharata, has 18 chapters, Adhyayas.

That is the reason for the 18 Brhati lines here.

There is a significance in these five Mantras. The first half of the five mantras vary, but the second half are similar:

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidam-upasate.*

The beauty and focus emanating from the reiteration of the same line is unique.

If a thing we are talking about is very important, and has to be carefully received, we iterate it again and again. Similarly it is here.

This is the portion that stresses the essence of the topic and concludes it.

It is like Pallavi, chorus, sung together at the end of a stanza.

It is called ‘Abhyasa’ - reiteration in Sanskrit.

Reiteration and repetition are different. Reiteration is a positive quality.

Telling again and again wastefully, with no new things to add, is repetition.

To stress the importance of the thing told, telling meaningfully again and again is ‘Abhyasa’ - reiteration.

It is the core section of the message, a subject of great importance. It is the matter to be retained carefully.

That is the reason it is reiterated here five times.

By this cycle of reiteration, a wonderful beauty emerges here in this composition

Such a beauty can’t be brought about, by other words.

Here too, there is a reference to five organs.

Earlier also it was referred twice.

This is the way it was in the first Mantra: mind, breath, speech, eye and ear.

In the second mantra it is like this: ear, mind, speech, breath, and eye.

This time it comes in a different form from the earlier ones: speech, mind, eye, ear and breath.

We had seen the appropriateness and significance of meaning, in the earlier two orders.

Then, what is the intention of this third one?

Well, there is a system in changing systems.

This is a special system. A system of opening up another face of the worship.

A system of suggesting the worship of new representing Deities; the system, prefacing the story that comes up in the later section.

Speech and mind is one combination; words and reflection.

It is Parvati and Parameswara.

Eye and ear are another combination: Surya and Candra.

Finally there is Prana, controller of all.

This is the order here.

Earlier it was said that Agni is the Deity of the organ of speech. How is it that it is Parvati here?

‘Vak’ means speech and also organ of speech.

In both these sense this reference comes here.

Parvati is the Deity of speech.

Agni is the Deity of the organ of speech. And Parvati also.

When Parvati is the Deity of speech, Siva is the Deity of mind.

It is a speech - mind union.

In an other Upanisat also this reference appears;

*yacched vangmanasi prajnah.*

These are the words in Kathakopanisat: one should reflect.

Uma, the Deity of speech, immersing in Siva, the Deity of mind. This is a new kind of worship, not mentioned earlier.

Later, the story of Parvati, the Deity of speech, preaching Indra occurs.

For that reason also is this reference here, about the Deity of speech.

In another aspect of worship, the Deity of speech is Saraswati. Deity of mind is Caturmukha Brahma.

Speech and mind are Laksmi and Narayana too.

Thus, there is a contemplation of the Trimurthy-trinity-couples.

In another aspect, the Deity of speech-organ is Agni.

Agni is the presiding Deity of Citta, intellect, which nurtures mind.

Thus, Agni is a compound Deity of speech and mind. For that reason too, the combined reference comes here.

Mind thinks. What is thought is spoken by mouth. Therefore these two are always companions.

Agni's place is in the fifteenth step of divine gradation.

Now the duo of eye and ear.

Surya and Candra are the Deities of day and night.

That is why these two Deities of light are addressed by a single name, ‘Puspavantau’, in Sanskrit.

*ravisasinau puspavantakhyau*

At some other places they have another name: Puspadantau.

*puspadantau puspavantav-ekoktya sasibhaskarau*

The twelfth step in the ladder of divine order is the place of Surya and Candra.

Finally there is Prana, Deity of breathing.

He is senior to all the Deities of organs.

Thus, this is a directive to introduce a new way, in the worship of presiding Deities.

It is the same in whichever way it is told.

Different types of notions are possible, when narrated in different ways.

Thus, different parts of the scripture speak in different ways. The principle behind everything is important.

What is the necessity of Upanisats for those who don’t have this insight?

Now let us come to the meaning of the Mantra.

The first reference: yad vacanabhyuditam.

‘Vak’ is not only the organ of speech, It is speech also.

The intent of the part of this sentence is this: Which cannot be expressed by the organ of speech, which cannot be fully expressed by any speech, which cannot be conveyed by anybody through words and which doesn’t need the help of the organ of speech to speak that one, is ‘vaca anabyudita’.

To speak, we need the organ mouth.

It is not enough that we have the mouth. There is no rule that all those having a mouth could speak.

Some times we say ‘he doesn’t have the mouth’.

What does it mean?

He has mouth, but he can’t speak, isn’t this its meaning?

There is a need of Agni, the presiding Deity, to enable the mouth to speak.

This mouth is a machine, which speaks, as he enables it to speak.

This inert mouth, it’s Deity Agni, Agni’s supervising action, the one controlling all these, is God.

Mouth cannot speak about him. Agni too.

Then this speaking is about whom?

This speaking is verily about the One, who can’t be spoken about. Then what is spoken?

However much is spoken, It cannot be completely spoken about. Because It is complete.

That which is complete, can’t be completely spoken about.

If it is completely spoken about, it means it is incomplete.

For that reason Upanisat says ‘anabhyuditam’.

an+abhi+uditam.

Uditam means, the spoken. ‘Abhi+Uditam’ means, ‘Abhitah Uditam’- which is spoken from all levels, completely.

God is not such thing. It is not possible to talk completely about Him.

Therefore He is ‘Anabyudita’.

But, everything spoken by everyone is through Him only:

*yena vak abhyudyate.*

Vak means organ of speech, the mouth.

Vak means, the speech, worded by mouth.

Vak means, Agni, the Deity of the organ of speech.

Vak means, Parvati and Saraswati, Goddesses of speech.

Vak is He.

Due to Him organ of speech speaks.

Due to Him, words spring.

Due to Him, Agni makes the mouth to speak. Agni himself speaks because of Him. Therefore He doesn’t need an assistance of all these: yad vaca anabhyuditam.

To speak, He doesn’t need a mouth.

Help of Agni, the Deity of mouth, is not needed for Him.

Help of the Deity of speech too, is not needed.

Vaca = through the organ of speech, through it‘ s Deity. Anabhyuditam = which is not spoken.

*vaca na vidyate abhyuditam abhtvadanam vyaharanam yasya tat*

It doesn’t speak through mouth: It doesn’t need a mouth like us to speak.

What does it mean?

Upanisats say: God can tell everything; can hear everything: vakta srota.

He speaks, but doesn’t need mouth to speak.

He listens. But doesn’t need ear to hear.

He sees. But doesn’t need eye to see.

Just tell, whether He has mouth, ear, eye etc or not.

If said yes, it is yes; if no, it is no.

This is confusing.

When things are known, there is no confusion. Men of letters do not get confused.

When things are not known, it is confusion. Everything is confusion to the ignorants.

Such is the way of Upanisat: solving confusion through confusion itself.

Thorn needs to be plucked by thorn only.

There is no other way to explain the Unseen thing.

There is a reference in Upanisat: sa paryaga-chukrama-kayam-abranam.

God doesn’t have body; He is body less (Akaya), tells this Mantra. The same Upanisat tells further: yat te rupam kalyanatamam tat te pasyame. I see Your auspicious form.

He doesn’t have a body. Therefore no shape; no figure.

He is ‘Akaya’. Therefore He is formless.

How then to see Him who is bodyless?

To this the Upanisat tells: It is true, He is bodyless. But He has an auspicious form. Therefore He can be seen.

What is the meaning of, seeing the beautiful form of one, who is formless?

It is a riddle.

When the riddle is broken the Upanisat opens up.

In another place is said like this: saksi ceta kevalo nirgunasca. God doesn’t have qualities.

But he is ‘Saksi’ witness, capable of seeing everything straight; is energetic, capable of comprehending everything. He is without quality; yet he is fully qualified!

We don’t understand this.

If He is qualityless, He can’t be full of qualities at the same time; if fully qualified, can’t be quality less.

If He is formless, He can’t be having a form at the same time; if is with form, can’t be formless.

How can one be both, at the same time?

If I say ‘she is my mother and also is a barren woman’, people will say, he is lunatic.

Upanisat also speaks similarly.

But it has not lost its head. And we too should not lose our heads. We must read between the lines.

Then will the Upanisat be understood.

Here too, a continuation of the same talk is witnessed.

God can speak without mouth!

Now let us get inside these statements and see.

It is true that God doesn’t have form.

He doesn’t have the kind of form we have.

We know about our form and shape.

That was formed in mother’s womb; and one day it is not going to be there.

Thus it goes on changing.

The previous form isn’t there now. The present form won’t be there in the coming life. Yet one or other form continues to remain.

Thousand births; thousand dramas. Thousand colours; thousand forms.

All these masks are the creation of Pancabhutas - five elements.

God doesn’t have such masks.

Íṛ doesn’t have a body, which is physical, cause of birth and death, and is different from His self.

Therefore He is bodyless.

Words like Deha, Kaya, Sarira, synonyms of ‘body’ indicate that. What is Deha?

Diha upacaye. Dihyate iti dehah. That which grows according to the food eaten, is Deha.

Growth is a metamorphosis. It is not in Him. So He is ‘ Adeha’. What is Kaya?

Kai sabde; kayati iti kayah. That which survives praising others and depending on others, is Kaya.

According to another definition, Kaya and Deha have the same meaning.

Eating and growing is Kaya: citate iti kayah

God doesn’t have such a Kaya; Therefore He is Akaya-bodyless. What is Sarira?

Sr hmsayam. Siryate iti sariram. While Deha is that which grows by eating.

Kaya is that which collapses, shrinking when it grows.

God doesn’t have such Sarira. Therefore He is Asarira-bodyless.

God doesn’t have such a mask that is worn and is discarded. But He has His own face. He has shape, which is His own self.

God doesn’t have material shape, created by earth water and fire.

But He has one non-material shape, in the form of knowledge and Bliss.

God doesn’t have a shape, which is momentary and unsteady, that emerges and vanishes.

God has a shape which will never be destroyed, and which is unchanging and eternal. God doesn’t have shape, which is different from himself and inert.

God has the shape which is His own self and awareness.

For that reason, it is called ‘Kalyanatamarupa’ - auspicious form.

It is the form of God. God himself is that form; that form itself is God.

It is similar when God is called ‘Nirguna’ - qualityless.

It is true that God doesn’t have qualities.

He doesn’t have the kind of qualities that we have.

What is quality?

We say, ‘he has fine qualities’. Here, quality means good quality; merit.

We say, ‘we don’t like his quality’. Here quality means bad quality; demerit.

In general, quality is an aspect of the thing; characteristics.

Merit is a quality; demerit is also a quality.

Joy is a quality; sorrow is also a quality.

The basic constituent qualities in us are only three: Satva (wisdom), Rajas (passion) and Tamas (darkness or ignorance).

Vigorous and evil.

Without these three, there is no creation. This material world is the collective of these three.

We can’t say who has which quality prominently. But there is none who doesn’t have these three qualities.

Therefore, we all are immanent with qualities.

But only in God, this game of qualities won’t work.

Therefore He is Nirguna, beyond qualities; quality free.

This doesn’t end here. He is a mine of immense attributes. So that He is full of good qualities.

To fill us up with all these aspects, Upanisat employs such a language of riddles.

God is formless; but He has auspicious form.

God is qualityless; but he has infinite auspicious qualities.

For that reason one Upanisat proclaims:

*pasyatyacaksus sa srnotyakarnah.*

He doesn’t have eye, yet he sees. He doesn’t have ear, yet He hears.

‘No’ doesn’t mean ‘not at all’.

He doesn’t have eye or ear, of our kind of flesh and blood.

Not the eye or ear, made of five elements and which get annihilated. Yet, He has indestructible infinite eye and ear.

Purusasukta proclaims the same: sahasrasirsa purusah sahasraksas sahasrapat.

He has thousands of heads; thousands of eyes; thousands of feet. Not only that; He has thousands of mouths too.

But they are not like our eye, ear and mouth.

Our organs are mere inert machines.

His organs are not inert; they are Cinmaya - wisdom themselves.

Because they are not different from Him. God himself is the organ; the organs verily are the God.

His quality, action, organ, shape, all are Cinmaya, wisdom themselves.

It is an absolute Cinmaya form.

For that reason this Upanisat says: yad vaca anabhyuditam.

He doesn’t need a null and void machine called mouth, to speak. He Himself is His mouth.

Why does He need this fumbling, one day finally word-failing, mouth?

More significant thing than this is: yena vak abhyudyate.

Yena = from whom, Vak = speech, Abhyudyate. = is pronounced.

He has power over speech. He has power over the mouth that speaks. The Deities making the mouth to speak are also under His thumb.

They speak due to Him.

They make us speak due to Him.

He makes everyone’s mouth speak. Does He need another mouth like ours to speak?

Abhyudyate is the form of the root ‘Vada’: abhi-udyate.

The more frequently employed word is ucyate.

Both mean the same: talking, speaking: vada vyaktayam vaci;

Vaca vyaktayam vaci. According to some root enumerating texts it is: vaca paribhasane.

Both mean the same.

Animals can express their feelings in a gross manner. Man express in a finer manner, with all the details, and so on. For that reason it is abhi+udyate. Abhi = Abhitah = in detail.

The succeeding words are: tadeva brahma tvam viddhi.

Realize That, that verily is Brahman.

Which one?

That one, which doesn’t need a mouth like ours, to speak.

That one, which speech cannot fully speak about.

That one, because of which, one can speak.

That one, which prompts the Deity of the organ of speech.

Isn’t that why it is called Brahman?

What does it mean?

That which is enormous is Brahman. Which is bigger and greater, than everything, which is at such a height, that our senses cannot reach, the most high, and which heightens us through wisdom, that is Brahman.

*atha kasmad-ucyate brahmeti brhati brmhayati ceti*

‘Brhati’ means is high, ‘Brmhayati’ means carries high: brha bhri vrddhau.

Rsi Kasakrtsna gives its etymology like this: brheru ro mani.

When the suffix ‘Man’ is added to ‘Brha’ root, ‘R’ changes into ‘Ra’ syllable:

‘Brh+man’ becomes ‘Brah+man’.

Caturmukha told, Realize That, that verily is Brahman: tadeva brahma tvam viddhi.

But then which should not be considered as Brahman?

*nedam yadidam-upasate*

Not this one, which people worship.

This means which? That is Brahman, This is not; if you say like this, what does it mean?

This means which? The one which is in front of us. This tangible world: idamah pratyaksa-gatam.

This world of matter and energy is in front of us. This inertwrid formed by the triplex of earth, water and fire and living being.

This is very much in front of us and all over in our head too.

Caturmukha tells: it is not Brahman. That (Tat) one is God, that won’t be seen by our outer eyes.

But ordinary people worship this outer thing only.

They worship the sex, the gold and the land in front of the eyes.

This is a very general thing.

There is also another warning in these words:

Some people worship in a mistaken way due to the illusion this moving and static cosmos itself is Brahman.

*sarvam khalvidam brahma*

The assertion that all is inherently Brahman, is the cause of their illusion.

Caturmukha tells: This world is not Brahman as conceived by some ignorants.

This is inert. He is vital.

He is different from this. He is far higher than this:

*nedam yad-idam-upasate*

Some others say: Brahma is not the inert world.

He is the vital world, yes.

All the Jivas are Cinmaya - immanent vital. Brahma also is vital.

These drops together become that ocean.

There are not many Cinmayas. This verily is That.

Caturmukha says: This ‘Jiva’ is not Brahman, as these people think of and meditate upon.

*ned am yad idam-upasate*

This ‘Jiva’ is just a reflection. ‘Pratibimba’ The original ‘Bimba’ inside this reflection is That. Realize That.

Then what about ‘sarvam khalvidam brahma’?

Yes, everything is Brahman. Because it is all - capable, omnipotent.

Away from It, the rest don’t have their own free existence.

There is another type of warning in these words.

For a Puja worship, there is need of an Image.

Some worship Idols; some worship Linga or Salagrama.

Among idols also there are various types: statue of Visnu, statue of Durga, Skanda, Surya, Ganapati and so on.

Thus, some worship God as an Image, as an Idol.

Caturmukha tells: The idol people worship as God, it is not Brahman.

The idols are made up of Pancabhutas, made of mud, stone, metals and so on. It is inert. God is Cinmaya, absolute vital.

Therefore, idol is not God.

Don’t worship idol as the God.

Then? Idol worship should not be done?

One can do. Not only that, it should be done also.

But one must not think that the idol itself is God.

One should know that God is within the idol.

It is an image of God; by itself the idol is not God.

God is within the image. The invisible God is within the visible image.

Now, let us move in to the inner world:

One Yogi is immersed in Dhyana.

Deep in concentration, his mind meditates on God‘ s form.

The bright solar circle. Narayana in the centre. He is in Padmasana posture.

Divine diadem (Krita) on head. Necklace in the neck. Crocodile like rings in ears. Armlets in upper arms. Body with gold color. Sankha and Cakra - Conch and wheel in the hands. Lo, the same form comes up in the mind. His Dhyana has been fruitful.

The form he desired stands in front of his eyes. He saw God.

Caturmukha says: No, he saw only the mental image, not God: nedam yad-idam upasate.

What they saw in meditation is not God.

It is also an idol of God.

There was an outer image: made of mud, wood, stone, metal and so on.

The meditator began the inner worship. In this mental Yajna, another idol image emerged inside.

It is not made of mud, not of wood, neither of stone nor of metal. Not sculpted by any sculptor.

Our mind itself became a sculptor, and carved an image of our liking and showed it.

This is the world of Manomaya - mind shell; more than that, it is the world of Vijnanamaya, creation of Citta - memory shell.

That is why Brahma said: What the people saw in their meditation, that also is not Brahman: nedam yad-idam-upasate.

What is it then?

What is seen so far is the statue on the pedestal, in the outer temple.

Now what is seen is the mental image on the pedestal of heart, in the inner temple.

One is an outer view, another is an inner view.

Both are a kind of show, not the real vision. Because, this material eye, the inner eye also, cannot see the non-material God.

For that, soul’s non material eye of cognition should open up.

Thus, Brahman is not the organs; It is not the soul that sees through the organs.

It is none of that, in the vital and non-vital world.

Nedam.

If not this, then what is it?

As answer to this question, this Mantra portion opens up in another way:

*yadidam upasa ḍṛ tadeva brahma*

In the previous sense, ‘Upasate‘ is a single word.

With the preposition ‘Upa’ is the root ‘Asa’: asa upavesane, or asu upvesane.

Now one has become three: ‘upa asa te’. (Which) Asa = is, Upa = near, Ḍṛ = you (that verily is Brahman).

This ‘Upa + Asa’, is not the form of Asa root as in the earlier case. The form of Asa root is this: asa vidyamanatayam.

This is a confusing thing to grammarians.

‘Asa’ root’s past tense form is not ‘asa’. It is ‘Babhuva’, say the scholars in grammar. ‘Babhuva’ is right, ‘Asa’ also is right.

When it is used in scriptures, who are we to say it is not right?

Grammarians should also pay obeisance to the usages of the ancients.

In literature also this ‘Asa’ form is found in plenty.

Yes, some scholars considered it as ‘erroneous application’.

In reality, it is not an ‘erroneous’ application. It is a proper application.

For that reason, scholars like Sakatayana and others called it an ‘indeclinable particle’ - Avyaya: vibhakti-pratirupakam-avyayam.

Some others say that this is a form of the root ‘Asa’, counted in the group of root ‘Bhu’ having a meaning of illumination: asa diptyadanayosca.

All these are grammatical acrobatics.

If it is a form of the root ‘Asa vidyamanatayam‘, it will look more natural, from the view point of meaning.

If it is Dhatu, it is alright.

If it is an Avyaya, having the similar form of the Dhatu, it is also fine. In either case, there is no change in the meaning.

Now, let us see the meaning of the Mantra: None of this is Brahman principle. This animate and inanimate world is a mere image of God, and one must recognize the energy within the image.

For that there is need to travel inside. If it is searched from outside it remains far away, like the horizon: tad dure.

When searched from inside, it is closer than the closest: tadvantike.

Because, that very principle is the one which dwells inside everything: tadantarasya sarvasya.

Acarya Madhva tells the same:

*durad durataram yattu tad-evantikam-antikat.*

Therefore, one should go inside. Absolutely inside.

Breaking the fence of five elements, tearing the fence of organs, Pulling down the fence of name and form, should go deep inside.

Then comes the realization of the soul. What is ‘I’ gets known.

It doesn’t end there. This Jiva, soul, is also a reflection of another.

It too is an image.

It was like seeing the form of another person in the mirror.

To be seen in the mirror, behind oneself and in front of the mirror, somebody should be there.

Without, Bimba the original, the reflection can’t be seen.

The person seeing the reflection is eager to see the original.

He turns back. He sees him.

Here also it is the same, with the Realization of the soul, the Jiva sees himself.

It gets confirmed that he is a mere reflection.

Then, which is the original, the Mulabimba?

Turn back and see. There is the original.

Within yourself, very close to you.

There is no other thing closer than it.

That verily is Brahman; tadeva brahma tvam viddhi.

The coming Mantra tells that, where speech fails, the mind too fails:

*yanmanasa na manute*

Nobody can tell about it wholly, because, nobody can catch it completely, through mind; nor grasp it.

Yat = which (no one), na manute = can grasp, manasa = through mind, (That one is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

Like the previous Mantra, this one too opens up in various ways.

No one can grasp It through mind.

This is one face.

It doesn’t grasp things through mind: to grasp things it doesn’t need the help of a dead weight called mind.

This is the other face.

Yat = which, Na manute = doesn’t grasp, Manasa = through mind (that verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

Unlike us, it doesn’t need the help of the Deity of mind, to grasp things.

This is the third face:

Yat = which, Na manute = doesn’t grasp, Manasa = with the help of the Deity of mind (that verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

Then, does God have sense-organ called mind, or doesn’t He?

It is yes and also no.

Mind is there: a nonmaterial mind - Cinmaya, which is wisdom itself.

A mind, which is God Himself.

He doesn’t have the mind: mind which is inert, perishable, different from His own personality which resembles ours, such a mind He doesn’t have.

One Sruti says:

*buddhi-manonga pratyangavattam laksayamahe*

Mind, intellect organs of sense and action, all these He has.

Yet none is there. Why?

They are not similar to those which we consider in this world as mind, intellect and so an.

He doesn’t have the mind and intellect that we know.

His mind and intellect, we don’t know.

It is a typical from this world. Therefore, That is not this.

This question is similar to the query, whether God is male or female.

He is neither male nor female.

Yet, He is male and is female!

If it is no, how is it yes?

If it is yes, how it is no?

He is not like the male and female of the world. Why?

In this world, through bodily configurations we identify one as male or female and behave accordingly.

In this physical sense, He is neither male nor female.

Yet he is male and at the same time female also.

Isn’t it He, who is inside all the males with his male form?

Isn’t it He, who is inside all the females with his female form? He is male inside males.

He is female inside females.

Therefore, He is both male and female.

For that reason, He is the father of the universe, and also the Mother.

*tvam-eva mata ca pita tvam-eva*

Similarly, here also: God has mind: but not the kind we possess.

To think, we need an inert machine, a computer called mind. Well, the computer is there.

But we don’t know how to control the computer. Don’t know how to operate it also.

A ‘Yantr’- mechanic, is needed to operate it.

We are simply possessed of the machine, Yantra and the mechanic, Yantr.

The presiding deities are called Yantrs.

We have inert machine Also, there are the controlling Deities. Garuda, Sesa and Rudra are there.

Indra and Kama are there. Aniruddha is there. Divine master Brhaspati is there. Candra is there. Mitra-Varunas are there. Agni is there. Parjanya is there.

Since they are performing their respective jobs, the function of our organs is going on.

We thought we did everything.

These are the ones who did it all.

Staying inside them ‘He’ did it.

If He does not do it, how can They do it?

If they don’t do it, how can we do it?

These are the words in the second line of the Mantra:

*yenahur-mano matam*

Yena = by whom, Manah = mind, Matam = gains knowledge (that is Brahman): tadeva brahman.

Not only that: the knowledge of presiding Deities of mind is also under His control:

Yena = by whom, Manah = Deity of mind (also), Matam = gains knowledge and gives us the knowledge through mind. (That verily is Brahman): tadeva brahman. Or, ‘manah’ means, the thing to be reflected.

‘vak’ means mouth, and speech from the mouth also.

With which we understand, that is mind:

*manute anena iti manah*

What we understand, that is also mind:

*manyate iti manah*

Now the meaning of the portion of the Mantra:

Yena = by whom, Manah = Knowable thing; Matam = gets understood, (that is verily is Brahman): tadeva brahman.

Therefore for it to think, there is no need of a machine called mind.

For it to control that machine there is no need of deities of organs.

Don’t go hither and thither search out It.

For, the more you go searching for It, the more It goes away from you.

Search It within, within yourself: near your self:

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi*

Since It is beyond the mind, it is obvious that what is seen in your Dhyana is Its image:

*nedam yadidam-upasate*

Kathopanisat tell: avrtta-caksuramr-tatvamicchan.

Do you want to conquer mortality? Then close your eyes and look inside.

Purandaradasa says: ‘Lo behold from inside of the inner eye’.

It doesn’t end by saying ‘See from inside’. Even by seeing inside, seeing through mental eye, He is not seen.

One should go inside the inner eye.

There is another eye inside the inner eye: The eye of the self; the eye, which is wisdom itself; the eye that can see God directly.

Until it is opened, nothing can be seen.

If this be the story of the inner eye, what can be the story of the outer eye?

The next Mantra, the seventh one, is an eye opener about the blindness of the outer eye.

*yac-caksusa na pasyati*

No one can see It through this eye:

Yat = which, Ne Pasyati = nobody sees, Caksusa = through gross eye (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

This is one side.

It doesn’t see with eye. To see things, It doesn’t need an inert sense-organ named eye.

This is the other side:

Yat = which, Na Pasyati= doesn’t see, Caksusa = with eye, (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

To see things like us, It doesn’t need the help of the deities of the sense-organ, eye.

This is the third side:

Yat = which, Na Pasyati = doesn’t see, Caksusa =with the help of the Deity of eye, (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma

In that case, does God have a sense-organ called eye? Or doesn’t he?

Both are right:

Eye is there: non-material eye, Cinmaya, the eye wisdom itself. The eyes which is God Himself.

Eye is not there: inert eye, that turns blind one day, different from the Self, like ours, such an eye, God doesn’t have.

It is not similar to the one which we consider as eye in this world.

His eye is not inert; doesn’t become hazy; doesn’t get sick; won’t turn blind; it beholds everything nearly and nothing afar.

He doesn’t have the eye we know.

We don’t know the way of His eye.

It is unworldly; therefore That is not this.

He doesn’t have eye like ours.

But He has thousands of eyes:

*visvatascaksuruta viswatomukhaha,*

*sahasraksah sahasparat*

He has thousand forms. Thousand eyes.

Thousand is not thousand; thousands of.

Countless thousands of eyes.

Eyes that don’t die.

We can’t do anything behind His eyes.

His eyes are everywhere.

We absolutely need eye to see, ear to hear.

It is not so with Him. Everything is everything for him.

His eye is also ear and the ear also the eye.

He can see through the eye, can hear through the eye, can speak also through His eye.

For, His sense organs are very much. He Himself.

He doesn’t have anything in Him, which is different from Him: neha nanasti kincana.

He is omnipresent, His organs also are omnipresent.

His eye is Himself and His ear too is Himself.

Therefore, His eye is very much His ear and His ear is very much His eye.

To see, we need an inert machine called eye.

Well, we have the machine, Yantra.

But we don’t know how to control the machine.

We need a Yantr - mechanic, to operate it.

With the machine and the mechanic, we are mere Yantris possessors of machine.

An inert machine called eye is there.

Controlling it, the Yantr-Surya, the presiding Deity is there.

Because He makes us see with our eye, we are able to see.

We imagined that we saw ourselves.

Surya showed us.

Staying within Surya, He showed us.

If He doesn’t show, what can this Surya show?

If Surya can not show, what can we see?

The further Mantra next tells us this: yena caksunsi pasyati.

It is a strange usage!

Caksunsi = eyes, Pasyati = sees!

What does it mean? What kind of language is this?

Rsis of Upanisats impart new meanings to words, through such unique usages, breaking the rules of grammar.

Some call such usages ‘Chandasa’.

It is a general rule that verb should have the same number, which the noun has. But here the noun is in plural: eyes caksunsi. The verb is in singular: sees: pasyati.

Such usage not found in the mundane world, when employed, is called Vaidika-Vedic, or Chandasa usage.

What is meant by all this?

It simply means that there are no such usages in this mundane world. But this does not answer why such usages come in Veda.

Somehow, grammarians try to answer this: Gender, number and cases can be used in changed forms in Veda:

*sup-ting-upagraha-linga-naranam-vyatyayamicchati*

It is a last resource of explanation to Vedic usage, that is all. Accommodating the existing thing.

But the question, why it is there in such a way, is not answered.

Why such rules are broken in Vedic usage?

To find answer to this, we have to go to the definition of the word ‘Caksus’!

What is the intention of those, who call such usages ‘Chandasa’?

They tell: ‘Eyes is seeing’, here noun is in plural number and the verb is in singular number. To bring accord with the noun, the verb should be changed into plural number. ‘Eyes are seeing’: caksunsi pasyanti.

There are some who chant the text itself like this:

*yacchaksusa na pasyati yena caksunsi pasyanti*

Not withstanding, the problem is not solved.

How ‘eyes are seeing’ is right?

Eyes don’t see. We see with the eyes.

Therefore even when it is considered as the special Vedic usage, the problem is not solved.

The answer to this is the very definition of the word ‘caksus'.

What does caksus mean?

We know: This is a word derived from the root, ‘caksing':

*caksing vyaktayam vaci*

The real meaning of the word not to see: but to talk clearly:

*vyaktayam vaci*

Therefore meaning by the definition is: That is caksus, with which we speak well.

Then caksus means mouth, not the eye.

That is the cleverness of the ancients.

Caksus means eye; not the mouth.

Well-speaking organ is not mouth, but the eye.

What is spoken by mouth need not be the word of the heart, it could be one above the neck.

One thing is inside, coming out is something else.

But eye doesn’t know to tell lie.

Eye shows out like a mirror, what ever gets inside it.

What the mouth can’t tell, the eyes can.

When lovers come together, they speak a hundred things, through their eyes, which their mouth cannot speak.

Eye is the mirror, held to the interior of the individual.

The full stamp of the individuality is in the eye.

If it is intended that a person in a portrait should not be recognized, his eyes are covered with a band.

Therefore eye is a clean speaking organ.

Therefore eye is Caksus.

Let us leave out the topic of talking. For a while let us come to the subject of seeing. With which we see, understand clearly, that is Caksus eye.

We see with the eye: therefore we think the eye is Caksus.

It is not fully true, that we see with the eye.

The one who has given the power to the eye to see, is its presiding Deity, Surya-sun.

In reality we see because of sun. Therefore the real Caksus is Sun. After him the Physical eye also:

*abhimani-vyapadesas-tu visesanugatibhyam*

The eyes, coming under the presiding Deity are numberless. Hence, the plural number in the word ‘caksunsi’.

Surya alone controls all those eyes.

Therefore the singular number ‘pasyati'.

Thus, to suggest that this sentence should imply the aspect of the Deity also, this change of number is done:

Yena = by whom, Caksunsi = presiding Deity of eyes, Surya (also), Pasyati=sees himself and imparts power to others to see through eye, (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

Thus we have seen one meaning, in the context of changed numbers.

With change of cases, its another face opens up:

There is a reference, that, first case can be employed in the senses of all the seven cases: saptasu prathama.

Accordingly, here, let us change the first case of ‘Caksunsi’ into third case.

By this, the meaning of the Mantra becomes as follows:

Yena = by whom, Caksunsi (Caksurbhih) = through eyes (this Jiva) Pasyati-sees, (that is Brahma): tadeva brahma.

Caksus means, not just the sense-organ through which we see, the person who sees also is Caksus: caste iti caksuh.

Therefore, Caksunsi means, the Jivas, who see through the eye.

Now, when the singular number of ‘Pasyati’ is changed into Plural number as ‘Pasyanti‘; the Mantra opens up in a new way:

Yena = by whom, Caksunsi = Jivas, who grasp things through eye, Pasyati (Pasyanti)=See, (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

Even without any change at all, this Mantra opens up in another form.

What is that form?

A totally new form. But older than old.

In this sense, ‘Caksunsi‘ doesn’t mean eye; but the forms seen by the eye.

We have seen in the previous Mantras:

‘Vak’ means mouth; ‘Vak’ also means the speech from the mouth.

‘Manah’ means; mind; ‘Manah’ also means the things grasped by the mind. Similarly here too:

Caksuh means the eye: ‘Caksuh ‘means the world full of forms seen by the eye also.

Through which we see, that too is Caksus:

*caste anena iti caksuh*

The things we see is also Caksus.

*caste jana idam iti caksuh*

Therefore Caksus means different colours and forms.

Now the meaning of this section of the Mantra: Yena = by whom, Pasyati - (Jiva) sees, Caksunsi - various scenes, (that verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

Thus, this Mantra can be seen in a number of ways. For this very reason, the ancient Rsis change often the gender, number and cases of the words.

To impart the spread of the unfolding to the language; to strengthen the language for a deeper operation; to give it a new beauty by infusing a new force in it.

Again the same ending:

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidamupasate*

Brahman also is not the one that can be seen by this outer eye.

It can’t also be seen by the mind’s eye, while worshipping.

For, It cannot be seen by the eye.

Eye can’t illumine It.

But It brightens everyone’s eye; the sight of eye is Its gift.

That verily is the Brahman that you desired to see:

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi*

In the forthcoming eighth Mantra, after the eye, it is the turn of the ear:

*ycchrotrena na srnoti*

No one can hear It.

Yat = which (no one), Srotrena = from ear, Na Shrnoti = nobody can hear (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

This is one aspect.

It doesn’t hear from ear. Tî hear, it doesn’t need an inert machine named ear.

It is another aspect:

Yat = which, Srotrena = through ear, Na Shrnoti =doesn’t hear (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

This is the third aspect:

Yat = which, Srotrena =with the help of the Deity of the ear, Na Srnoti = doesn’t hear, (that verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

We need an inert machine called ear to hear.

Well, we do have the machine.

But we don’t know how to control the machine. We need a mechanic to operate it.

The inert machine is there. Candra, its Deity who controls it, is there.

Because he makes us hear with our ears, we are able to hear. Earlier, we were under the illusion that we heard by ourselves. But Candra made us hear.

Staying within Candra, ‘He’ made it heard.

If ‘He’ had not made it heard, how this Candra can make it heard? If Candra didn’t make us hear, what can we hear?

This is told in the portion which follows.

*yena srotramidam srutam*

This ear hears from That.

The power of hearing in the Deity, who has given the strength of hearing to our ears, is also His gift. It became possible for the Deity to control and make our ear to hear, that too is due to Him.

Yena = by whom, Idam Srotram=this ear, Srotram =gained the power of hearing, (that verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

*srutam sravanam asya astlti sravanam*

Yena = by whom, I am Srotram = The presiding Deity of the ear, Srutam = gained the power of hearing, (that is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

Yena = by whom, Idam Srotram = this ear, Srutam = gave the power of hearing to the ears (that is Brahman) tadeva brahma.

Such a God, does He need an inert ear to hear?

Or, should He hear from ear only?

His eye is ear also, and ear is eye also.

For. God verily is ear; ear verily is God.

Like Him, His organ too is Cinmaya - the wisdom itself.

Like Him, it is Omnipresent.

You understand that, such a non- physical principle is indeed God. Not this physical world.

Not the material image tat emerges in mind during Dhyana.

Not the atomic feeble Jiva also, though he is also non-material.

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidamupasa te*

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidamupasa te*

We saw the status of vak, manas, speech and mind, the area of operation of Parvati and Paramesvara.

We saw the story of eye and ear, the field of sun and moon.

There is another power, the root of all these: That is ‘Pranasakti’, ‘Prana’s power.

When there is no power of Prana mouth cannot speak, though Parvati is present; mind cannot respond though Siva is present.

In the absence of Pranasakti, even with the presence of Sun, eye can’t see; with the presence of moon, ear can’t hear.

Everything is there if Pranasakti is present. If Prana leaves nothing remains.

For that reason they addressed him as ‘Prananam Pranah’, Prana of Pranas; called him Chief Prana.

He is the prime ‘Jiva’, presiding Deity of all the living beings.

Controlling the power of life and everything.

If Prana is there, one can live even if mind, Speech, eye and ear are missing.

If Prana is not there, there is no life.

The basic sutra - thread of life is Prana. That is why scholars call him ‘Sutra’.

Life depends on breathing. Mukhya-Prana is the one who makes the breathing possible.

Dhyana also needs the help of Pranasakti.

Dhyana is achieved by the control of breathing.

Prana is needed to be alive; for Dhyana also; Prana is the strength of worship.

Such a Prana is none other than Caturmukha Brahma.

Prana’s after form is Caturmukha.

Caturmukha’s former form is Prana.

Such a Prana too is nothing before God.

The rein of Prana is also in the hands of God.

Prana is Sutra and he is Sutra-Dhara.

Thus, through Prana principle, Caturmukha confesses his dependence on God, in front of Siva:

*yat pranena na praniti*

Now let us see its different stages.

In the first stage, Prana means Ghrana; the breathing organ; the nose.

Nose has two functions: Smelling and Breathing.

That which smells is Ghrana; that which breathes is Prana.

*ana svasane; ghra gandhopadane*

It doesn’t breathe with nose.

Because, it doesn’t need nose to breathe; and it doesn’t need breathing to be alive.

Yat = which, Na Praniti = doesn’t breathe, Pranena = from nose, (That verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

‘Pranana’ means not only breathing; it implies all the functions of the nose.

If we see in this sense, this mantra opens up in another way:

Yat = which, (whom), Na Praniti = nobody can understand by smelling, Pranena = by nose, (That verily is Brahman), tadeva brahma.

Why can’t we understand by smelling?

Because one can’t smell it?

Or even by smelling can’t understand?

Both are right.

We are not capable of smelling. To smell we need nose.

The inert nose know only smelling the inert smell.

God’s is non-material smell, Cinmaya smell wisdom itself. Anandamaya smell, bliss itself: saddehas sukhsgandhasca.

How can this perfume reach the nose?

It has a fine fragrance; but not an inert perfume.

Therefore, it can not be known by smelling: nityam-agandhavacca yat.

In the second stage, Prana means Mukhya Prana.

We have nose, we breathe through nose.

It is not altogether untrue.

But not fully true also.

All those having nose need not breathe.

Often we struggle with a blocked nose.

It is because we have a nose, we die with stopped breathing.

In the womb of breathing, life exists, death too.

It is not enough that nose is there.

Prana, breathing force should be there inside.

Only then, our breathing, our life and all that is possible.

But God requires which Prana to live?

He is the one who has given Prana to that Prana, who makes everyone breathe.

In front of Him, Prana is helpless.

Yat = which, Na Praniti = doesn’t breathe, Pranena = because of the help of Mukhya Prana, (that verily is Brahma): tadeva brahma.

But if Mukhya-Prana has to breathe, He should make him breathe.

*yena pranah praniyate*

Yena = by whom, Pranah = Mukhya Prana (too), Praniyate = prompted, to breathe, (that verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

When the fate of Mukhya Prana is such what could be of this, that breathes because of Mukhya Prana?

Yena = by whom, Pranah = the nose of living beings, Praniyate = made to breathe, made to smell (that verily Brahman): tadeva brahma.

It is true, Prana makes us breathe. But he does it, obeying the commands of God: bhisasmad vatah pavate.

Prana’s control over us also is under the command of God.

Prana is standing above all the living being.

One day can he grow beyond the control of God?

Caturmukha replies to this doubt, from this line of the Mantras: yena pranah praniyate.

Yena = by whom, Pranah = Mukhya Prana, Pramyate = is taken to such a height, (that verily is Brahman): tadeva brahma.

It is God who has taken this Prana principle to such a height.

It is God, who, later on awards the position of Brahma to Prana, taking him up to another higher stage of glory.

The questioner also knows this: sa visnuriti dharyatam.

Thus, this is the portion of the Mantra, which explains, stressing the inner layer five times, the foundation of worship.

*tadeva brahma tvam viddhi nedam yadidamupasa te*

Thus, with these nine rare Mantras, the first section of Talavakaropanisat ends.

## Section: Two

## Mantra: one – two

In the first section there are nine Mantras; in the second section there are five Mantras.

These are the verse portions of the Upanisat. Next portion is in prose.

The first two Mantras of the second section:

*yadi manyase suvedeti*

*dahramevapi nunam tvam vettha brahmano rupam,*

*yadasya tvam yadasya deve svatha nu*

*mimamsyameva te manye-aviditam \|\|1\|\|*

*naha manye suvedeti no na vedeti veda ca*

*yo nastad veda tad veda no na vedeti veda ca \|\|2\|\|*

(If you think that ‘I know very well’, really you don’t know about God even a little. You don’t know His presence in you, in the Deities. Therefore I think you should reflect more. I don’t also think, one can know Him well even by reflection. I don’t think that nobody can know Him also. There is one who know Him well; who is atop the greatness of life. I don’t accept the stand that It is beyond comprehension. Those who think that we can understand It according to our capacity, and those who do not think that no one can understand It, are the ones who know It.)

You may get a doubt now.

This is the prevailing text of the first half of the first Mantra:

*yadi manyase suvedeti*

*dahramevapi nunam tvam*

*vettha brahmano rupam*

But here it is a different text.

Instead of vettha brahmano rupam, here it is na vettha brahmano rupam.

How did it become ’Na Vettha’ instead of ‘Vettha’? from where did ‘Na’ come from?

In none of the printed texts now we come across, this version can be found. Not only the printed texts, no commentator has written about this ‘Na’.

Then, how does it appear here, which is not found anywhere else? There is a reason. There exists one ancient commentary, a rare commentary.

It was authored by Vamana Panditacarya, son of Narayana Panditacarya, grandson of Trivikrama Panditacarya.

He lived during the end of 13lhand mid 14thC. AD. He had heard Acarya Madhva’s text-version directly from his father and grand father. Perhaps he had seen Acarya Madhva in person; enjoyed his discourses. This much is certain; his is a text, approved by Acarya Madhva; his interpretation is the interpretation of the Acarya.

This is his interpretation of the Mantra section.

*yadi kascit sutaram veda brahmeti manyase, aham-*

*suvedeti va, tarhi tvam dahram sva-hrdaya-*

*sthameva brahmano rupam nunam na vettha*

From this we can conclude that the version ‘Na Vettha’, the version having ‘Na’, is the pure version, the ancient version accepted by Acarya Madhva.

Texts get changed due to faulty teaching traditions and that can be seen in some other Upanisats also. For instance, let us take up the well known Santi-patha of Taittiriyopanisat.

Its prevailing one line is like this:

*tvameva pratyaksam brahma vadisyami*

But, the text accepted by the Acaryas of all schools, the text written by Sri Hrsikesa Teertha, the direct disciple of Acarya Madhva, is this:

*tvameva pratyaksham brahma vadisyami*

This is nothing new for those who search for the original texts.

For those who are not aware of research, who digest the printed errors, this may cause a dilemma, which doesn’t have a solution.

These two Mantras join together and become a duet.

Not only from the view point of the meaning; from the view point of meter also.

It is a unique combination of meter. Such an example is rare in Veda-literature.

It is a move of four strings.

An expansion of the wonder of four meters in two Mantras!

The meter flows parallel to the meaning.

Meaning emerges parallel to the move of the meter.

*vagarthaviva samprktau*

First, let us see the form of the meter of the first Mantra.

Many don’t have an idea that it is set in meter. Most think that it is in prose.

In appearance it looks like prose. Yet it is in verse form.

A verse looking like prose: proverse!

Jagati is the meter of the first Mantra.

But its flow is not like the flow of Jagati.

Jagati is four-lined. Four lines with 12 syllables each. 48 syllables in toto.

Of course, here there are 48 syllables, 25 in the first half and 23 in the later half.

But it is not four-lined, it is Pancapadi - five lined.

That is why it is a rare Jagati!

In the first half it is not a move of 12 syllables, but is a move of 8 syllables.

There are not 8 letters consistently in all the lines.

Some times it could be 9 also.

The move of the three lines, in the first half is like this: 9+8+8. First pause, with the 9th syllable:

*yadi manyase suvedeti*

Second pause, at the 8th syllable:

*dahramevapi nunam tvam*

Third pause, again at the 8th syllable:

*na vettha brahmano rupam*

The move of the two lines of the later half is like this: 12+11.

*yadasya tvam yadasya devesvatha nu*

*mimamsyameva te manye-aviditam*

There is one excess syllable in the first half.

It is Gayatri, due to excess of one syllable it is Bhurik Gayatri.

One syllable is less in the second half.

In the 2ndhalf, the first line having 12 syllables, is Jagati.

Second line, having 11 syllables, is Tristup.

Thus all the five lines joining together, became one Jagati in Pancapadi - five lines: 25+23=48.

It is Tripadi in the belly of Pancapadi, and Gayatri in the lap of Jagati.

Two meters in one Mantra.

Swaha, Sanjna, Rohini, Tara, wives of Mitra & Varuna and Saci are the Deities of Jagati meter.

Laksmi, Saraswathi, Bharati, Garuda and Swaha, these five are the Deities of Gayatri.

The gimmick of the meter of this Mantra is not yet over.

There is another strange thing here. The last word of this Mantra is related to the next Mantra. It doesn’t have direct application here.

*te manye-avmitam*

Here, with atha nu mimamsyameva te manye, the sentence is complete.

The statement is over, but the verse is not over.

Three syllables, to be added to the next verse, have entered here. Generally, Vedic hymns won’t be like this.

Every verse is self complete.

A piece of the later Mantra never appears in the previous Mantra. Then why is it so here?

It has a reason related to meaning, and another reason related to meter.

In manyeviditam, word-splitting can be done in two ways:

manye viditam and manye aviditam.

Both the words have application in the next verse:

*viditam na manye, aviditam na manye*

It will be clarified later, in the explanation of its meaning.

For the convenience of this pun, which flashes two meanings from a single usage, this word has been included in the first Mantra.

This is the reason related to the convenience of meaning.

Now let us see the surprise of the move of the meter.

Leaving apart the three syllables, applicable to the later Mantra, let us see this Mantra.

Earlier, Jagati of 48 syllables became a new chandas-meter, of 45 syllables!

First it became Gayatri. Later, it became Jagati.

Now, it became Pancapadi-five-lined, Brhati, having 9 syllables in each line: 9\*5=45.

The meter, having 9 syllables is called Brhati:

*navaksara brhati*

Brhati chandas is very dear to Prana and Narayana.

Prana has five forms, Narayana too has five forms, and here Brhati too has five lines. This is a rare combination.

Four meters in one Mantra: Gayatri, Jagati, Tristup and Brhati.

Laksmi, Saraswati, Bharati, Garuda and Brhaspati’s wife Tara are the presiding Deities of Brhati chandas.

There are four lines of 8 syllables in the second Mantra.

It is a natural Anustup meter: 8\*4=32.

But it doesn’t end here.

The words ‘Viditam’ and ‘Aviditam’ from the previous Mantra are brought over and included here.

At the most, four extra syllables get added here.

When 4 extra syllables join Anustup meter of 32 syllables, it becomes Brhati with 36 syllables.

Therefore, what was Anustup, grew into Brhati.

This transposing of Brhati is a secret worship in the Vedic tradition.

What is superficially seen as Jagati and Anustup inside them, two more chandas are flowing in secret: they are Gayatri and Brhati!

It is the proclamation of the wonder of the presence of four meters in two Mantras. This verily is the move of Veda-scripture in four strings!

Like, river Saraswati, flowing unseen, along with Ganga and Yamuna.

This is seen by Yogis, and seen by the doctors too.

Another thing is there within it; not seen by doctors; seen only when the inner eye opens:

*tasyante susiram suksmam tasmin sarvam*

*pratistitam*

In that pouch of the heart, there is a small cave; a small tiny point. God resides in it.

Not only is it the abode of God, it is the abode of Jiva too.

*tasya madhye vahnisikha antyordhva vyavasthita*

There is a tiny flame of fire; the fire of awareness, the self-form of Jiva.

*tasyah sikhaya madhye paramatma vyavasthitah*

God is residing inside the Jiva, the fire of awareness, as Bimba-rupa.

This Upanisat also tells the same thing: You have not seen the finest of the finest ‘dahram’ form of God, in that tiny point in the heart.

In total, the sense of all these talks is this:

In the cave of heart, there is a very fine, point-like space.

It is called ‘dahrakasa’ of ‘daharakasa’. There is a point of light inside it. Within it resides God’s glow.

Dahra within a Dahra - micro within micro, is God’s form, smaller than smallest. That Dahra - the micro, itself is the Cosmic form of the Cosmos. Mahato Mahiya - macro of the macro, bigger than biggest.

That drop is this ocean:

*sa brahma sa sivah sendrah soksarah paramah svarat*

He verily is Narayana. He verily is the Super-power, that commands everything, residing within Brahma, Siva, Indra and others.

All the words are verily His name. Verily the epithet of His glory.

What a carelessness it would be, claiming that one knows everything, without knowing all these things!

Basically, one thing should be kept in mind always; that it is never possible to know God completely.

Understanding Him fully means. He can not be understood fully. If you don’t know that, it means you don’t know anything:

*dahramevapi nunam na vettha*

Because, which should be basically known, that itself is not known.

This is Brahma’s caution to Siva.

The caution given by the Rsis, the Seers of Vedas, to us desiring to proceed in the path of spirituality.

Now Siva may answer, and all the Sisyas practicing spirituality, also:

It is true that God cannot be understood fully. Therefore it is also true that I don’t understand Him fully. I am not telling that I have understood fully. But what I am telling is that I understood quite well: sudeva iti.

Though it is impossible to know Him completely, at least can’t we know Him well whatever we can?

The following section of the Mantra comes as an answer to this:

*yadasya tvam yadasya devesu...*

Asya Brahmanah = Of the Brahma Tattva, Yat = which, Tvam rupam = farm within you, (and) Yat = which, Devesu = forms in your organs, forms in the Deities of organs (that too), Na Vettha = don’t know.

You are net aware of the God inside you; you are not aware of the God within your organs; you are not aware of the God indweller of the Deities and controlling them.

An awareness of God, pervading infinite Time and Space, remains a far away question.

There is m question of knowing what cannot be known.

Even that which can and must be known, you don’t know it well.

Then how can you claim that T know God well’?

Sa yascayam puruse. The God within the microcosmic world; that itself is no known.

Yascasaviddhe - then, the God principle within the macrocosmic - world, how can it be known!

How one can know Trivikrama when he has not known Vamana!

For that, every worshipper should become ‘Bali’. Finally, should offer even the head: nayamatma balahinena labhyah.

There is a beautiful saying here: yadasya tvam.

God’s form named ‘You’.

Yadasya devasu - God’s form within the Deities!

This is understood. But what is that?

The form tamed ‘You’? What does it mean?

It is another Vedic usage: employing the first-case in the sense of all the cases: saptasu prathama.

To make one statement, to come up with the various meanings, such usages are employed.

‘Asya tvam’ doesn’t mean, His ‘You’ form; it means, His form in you: yadasya tvayi.

In the next sentence it is in the seventh-case: yadasya devesu - the form in the Deities.

This previous sentence is also like the next sentence.

Therefore, it is clear that here ‘you’ means ‘in you’.

Those who know the art of speech, understand this word also.

To those who don’t have the art of language, whatever is told, it is all the same.

True it is understood. But why should it be told like that? Why should it be understood like this? Why not tell openly, ‘in you’? Why confuse us by changing the cases?

There is a reason for it.

This first-case is also the seventh-case and the seventh-case is also the first-case.

This reference of two cases in two places, is to tell, that in both the places both the cases should be employed.

First, we understood the meaning, by changing the first case into seventh case.

Yadasya tvayi - the form inside you.

How should this form be called? What is its name?

The same answer to this also. Now the same first case is required.

Yadsya tvam - The name of the God’s form within you is ‘you’, Tvam.

The succeeding expression is also similar: yadasya devesu the form within the Deities.

What is its name? How is that form to be addressed?

The same answer to this also. Now the seventh-case has become the first-case:

Yadasya Devah - The form within the Deities is called as ‘Deva’. What is meant by all this?

You are not you; the God within you is ‘you’.

I am not I; the God within me is ‘I’.

Devas - Deities, are not Devas; the God within the Devas is ‘Deva’. Since that ‘you’ is within you, you have become you.

Since that ‘I’ is within me, I have become I.

Since that ‘Deva’ is within the Devas, Devas have become Devas.

He is ‘you’ to you, ‘I’ to I, ‘Devadeva’ to Devas.

Despite telling this much, nothing is understood.

To know clearly, one should become more transparent.

To know that we do not know is not enough.

An urge to know should emerge.

Me for myself is ‘I’, and me is ‘you’ to all others.

For only myself, I am ‘I’, I am ‘you’ to million others.

For one ’I’ there are millions of ‘you’.

‘I’ is ‘Aham’ in Sanskrit. ‘You’ is ‘Tvam’.

It doesn’t end there. ‘Aham’ is, I, ‘Tvam’ is you, this is like, learning by heart, lesson for children.

Why should it be so, ‘aham’ to mean I?

Why should it be, ‘tvam’ to mean you?

To know the meaning of these words, we should get the answers to these questions.

‘Aham’ means ‘Aheya’, which we cannot remain without.

*na hiyate iti aham. ohak tyage.*

I cannot remain without me. Therefore I am ‘Aham’ to me.

You can remain without me. Therefore you are not ‘Aham’ to me.

I am ‘Aham’ only to me. To you all, I am ‘Tvam’.

No one can remain apart from God. He is ‘Aheya’ to all. He is ‘Aham’ to all.

He is the universal ‘Aham’.

Therefore He is the real ‘Aham’ - I.

Geeta tells the same:

*na tadasti vina yat syanmaya bhutam caracaram*

Without whom the moving and the static universe cannot live, He is the ‘Aham’ of the universe.

For that reason, Vedas address Him very much as ‘Aham’:

*aham tat tejo rasminnarayanam purusam*

*jatamagratah*

That primeval Prime Purusa, that Narayana verily is ‘Aham’.

He, being addressed by the word ‘Aham ’, and by His being within me, I too am ‘Aham’ -I.

Why ‘Aham’ means I, is now understood.

But why ‘Tvam’ means you?

For that we have to break it. We have to look into the basic etymology.

‘Tvam’ means Tvesa, light: tvisa diptau.

That which comes into light before me, that which is seen in front of me is ‘Tvam’ to me.

‘Tvam’ means, movement, mobility: tvagi gatau.

That which moves, moves about in front of me, is ‘Tvam’ to me.

To me the people in front of me are ‘Tvam’. To you the ones in front of you.

The one who imparts movement to all, being within everything, who in front of some, makes some others move about, who brings things to light. He is God. Therefore He is ‘Tvam’ to everyone. He, being addressed by the word ‘Tvam’, and by His being within you all, all of you are ‘Tvam’ to me.

He is a real ‘you’. And that ‘you’ is in you. That is why you are ‘you’.

Brahma asks Siva: yadasya tvam.

The God within you is ‘Tvam’ to me. He verily is ‘Aham’ to you.

You are different and I am different. This is Dvaita.

But, the ‘you’ within you is not different; the ‘I’ within you, is not different.

My ‘you’ (Tvam) is your I (Aham). Your ‘you’ (Tvam) is my ‘I’ (Aham).

You have not witnessed the wonder of the inner mind, where this ‘Tvam’ and ‘Aham’ become one.

This is the Advaita - Singularity, witnessed by the Rsis.

Advaita of the Indweller.

One who doesn’t know Dvaita - Duality, won’t know Advaita.

One who doesn’t know Advaita, won’t know Dvaita also.

He who says that there is only one, doesn’t know both.

He who says that there are two, doesn’t know the only One.

By the being of one, the second has a shelter.

By the being of second, the first has a worth.

There are philosophers who don’t have a shelter or foundation; and some vedantins who don’t have worth.

In the hands of such ones Vedanta loses its foundation; loses its worth. And Atheistic neisciences parade wearing masks of philosophy.

Brahma tells further: yadasya devesu.

Not only in ‘you’ of you and T of you; in the ‘Devas’ inside you also, He is very much there.

Who are these ‘Devas’? Those in this body?

Yes; there is a conclave of ‘Devas’ in this body and a conclave of Devils too.

Who are these Devils?

Bhagavata clarifies this:

*daityesvasaktam visayatmakesu*

The objects of evil pleasures are Devils. Sense-organs are ‘Devas’.

This is the unending war of Devils and ‘Devas’, within us.

Eye, ear, nose, tongue and skin are the five Devas.

Form, sound, odour, taste and touch are the five Danavas-Devils.

The clashes of these five some is the universe.

The war between eye and form; war between ear and sound; war between nose and odour; war between tongue and taste; war between skin and touch.

When eye buckles to the pull of the beauty of the form, form wins over; eye gets defeated.

Devil-power conquered; Devine-power surrendered.

For that reason scholars called sense-organ ‘Deva’: devamindriyam.

‘Deva’ means that which provides the light of knowledge: devayati iti devamindriyam.

Thus, the name ‘Deva’ to the organs is meaningful.

Especially, the sense-organs are Devas. And in general, organs of action also.

Thus, not only in our body, and inside our own self, inside our ten- organs also, God is pervading in His ten forms. Within the inner organ, mind also. For that very reason, He is Hrisikesa.

Sense-organs don’t have the power to grasp objects on their own. To moderate them, there are the presiding Deities.

They too don’t have the power on their own to regulate the organs. For that, God is residing within the Deities.

Now ‘Devesu’ does not mean, in the sense-organs.

It means, in the Deities of the organs.

This is about the microcosmic world.

Not merely this; He is residing inside all the Deities in the macrocosm as the regulator.

In this body and in universe, He pervades in infinite forms and infinite modes.

Without knowing all these, if you say ‘I have understood’, it means nothing has been understood: dahramapi nunam na vettha.

This is the concluding section of the Mantra:

*atha nu mimamsyameva ḍṛ manye*

Atha = Therefore, (Atha = hereafter) Ḍṛ = You have to, Mimamsyameva = understand (Brahma), Nu = Isn’t it so? (thus) Manye = I think.

‘Atha’ is a rare word in Sanskrit.

Like îṅ, it is a word laden with meanings.

The varied meanings of ‘Atha’ can rarely be found in words of other languages.

First of all, the word ‘Atha’ collects all the causes mentioned so far, and presents them summarily: Atha, with all these reasons, therefore.

Secondly, It also alerts us that, what is not gained so far, should be gained hereafter: Atha: hereafter.

Thirdly, it clarifies the capacity of the person: after gaining the capacity to do.

Fourthly, It tells about the thing which needs to be known.

‘A’ is the abbreviation of ‘Atita’ - beyond.

‘Tha’ is, which is within us; residing within (Sthita).

That God, which is beyond our understanding, is very much within us.

Such an ‘Atha’ we must know. There is no other choice.

Therefore too I say: mimamsyaameva te manye.

You thought that you understood. But didn’t understand anything. Now you understand that it is beyond understanding. Now you have become qualified to understand.

Therefore now you should start to know Him.

This is suggested in ‘Ḍe' also.

Te = to you. What kind of you? You who thinks that you know everything. Therefore to you; who do not know anything.

Another word that is in this Mantra: Nu.

This is a word that implies a consoling of the questioner.

When Brahma speaks to Siva, a new meaning emerges:

Brahma asked: ‘Nu?’ Is it so? It may be true to an ordinary worshipper, but is it so to you?

In this asking ‘is it so’, the answer that ‘in reality it is not so’, is also implied.

This is the intention of Brahma: It is true that one who thinks that he knows everything, doesn’t know anything. It is alright. It is about ordinary persons.

But you are not ordinary. You have climbed unusual heights in your meditation.

You are the Deity regulating everyone’s understanding.

I know that you don’t think so. You know that you don’t know. Therefore you know the Brahman also.

The last word of this Mantra continues along with the next Mantra.

The reason for it, we have seen it earlier: the convenience of the two ways of dividing the word.

For that reason, the next sentence also turns into two types:

*viditam naha manye.*

*aviditam naha manye*

This is the continuation of Caturmukha’s words.

This word, failing to join either in front or behind, has confused many heads: viditam.

According to them, mimamsyameva te manye - here, Guru’s answer ends.

From naha manye again Guru’s answer continues.

That which came in the middle, viditam, is the word of the Sisya, student.

Listening to the explanation of the Guru, Sisya seems to say, ‘viditam’. It is understood.

What is understood? Understood that it is not understood.

Also understood that it cannot be fully understood.

This is the explanation given by many.

Separating one word from the entire Mantra and calling it the word of the student, doesn’t sound well.

That the entire Mantra is Guru’s talk is a very appropriate explanation.

Ancient commentator, Traivikramaryadasa, who presents such a proper explanation, along with the two types of the word-splitting says:

*viditam naha manye.*

*aviditam naha manye*

To this Mantra, there is a different reading:

Some read, naham manye and the others, nahamanye.

In the second reading also, there are two types of word-division.

According to many commentators, the word-division is ‘Na aham Manye’.

According to the Vakyabhasya, supposed to be from Acarya Sankara, the word-division is, ‘Na Aha Manye’.

The same reading was employed by the ancient commentator, Traivikramaryadasa.

Therefore we can conclude that ‘na aha manye’ is the original reading.

The pure reading is understood now.

It will be helpful to know the pure meaning by this.

Now, let us come towards the pure meaning.

Caturmukha tells: viditam naha manye.

If anybody claims that Brahman can be grasped fully, I won’t agree with that.

Because I know that it cannot be fully made out.

Aha = Oh!, Na manye = (I) don’t think, (that Brahman is) Viditam = fully grasped.

‘Aha’ is an adverb suggesting the feeling of amazement.

Brahma feels bewildered while thinking fully, inconceivability of Brahman.

Then, can we say, It is not known at all? That too is not right:

*aviditam naha manye*

If told that I don’t know Brahman at all, that too I won’t agree with.

Again Brahma exclaims: Aha = Oh! It is the exclamation of his merging in the ocean of the bliss of the Brahman-experience.

I don’t think that It is absolutely incomprehensible and undefinable.

Aha = Oh!, Na manye = (I) don’t think, (that Brahman is), Aviditam = absolutely unknowable.

If it is said that, nobody knows about It, then there is no proof about its existence.

If it is said that, I know about It and nothing more remains to be known about it, Then its being eternal makes no sense at all.

It is not unknowable, because we have an awareness of Its existence.

If it also not fully knowable, because for us the incomplete ones, it is impossible to know it completely.

If it is said, it is fully known, It cannot be full.

There is another dimension to this Mantra:

That all can know Brahman, is also wrong.

That none can know Brahman is also equally wrong.

It cannot be grasped by all.

It is not ungraspable also by all.

Na manye = (I) don’t think (that Brahma is), Viditam = known to all.

Na manye = I don’t think (that Brahma is), Aviditam = not known by anyone.

All those reflecting on Brahman cannot know Brahman.

Only the pure qualified ones can know It.

Hence, Brahmasutra says that, knowhow of Brahman is not for all: asarvatriki.

It is not enough that we decide to know God.

God should consent that His form be understood by us:

*yamevaisa vrunute tena labhyah*

All cannot know, true. But it is not true that nobody can know.

Isn’t He the Merciful one, who reveals out in front of His devotees?

Earlier also this reference had come:

*anyadeva tad viditadatho aviditadadhi*

This is the tradition of the scholars; repeating a statement again and again. Saying in various ways. Saying again and again means, reiteration; stressing the said thing.

It is their intention, that it is an important saying, you understand it firmly, and don’t forget it.

The compassion they have on us makes them repeat the things again and again.

A joyful thing repeated a number of times, itself is a joy.

They enjoy telling it.

They told it with joy.

They told that repeatedly listening to it, we should enjoy.

What then does all this mean?

It is not an unknowable thing.

Yet It is not a thing that can be fully known.

*nativedyo nacavedyah*

It is like an ocean.

Whatever is the capacity of our vessel, so much should we fill it up.

Vessel got filled, but the sea didn’t get emptied.

We filled our bowl, but the sea didn’t go empty.

We filled our drums, but the sea didn’t go empty.

Vessel got full, bowl got full, drum got full. But all are not fully full.

All are full themselves. But it is relative fullness. Delimited fullness.

God is also full. He is an infinite ocean. His is absolute fullness, unbounded fullness.

Our job is just filling up our vessels of understanding.

According to the size of the vessel, emerges the size of the understanding.

The understanding is not uniform.

There are those who fill up their small spoon. And also those who fill up their big drums.

Which is the biggest vessel?

Who has understood God better than all the rest?

Who has understood the maximum, that can be understood about God?

There is such a one: suvedeti.

(One) Su Veda = Knows all, Iti = that verily is the very end of understanding God, the peak of understanding.

There is no other Jiva who knows better than him.

His is the finale of understanding.

He is the eldest among the Jivas, greatest Jiva.

Who is He?

Here ‘Na Veda ca’, the ‘ca’. syllable has come to stress the thing: knows not at all; na vedaiva.

One should not also think that God is an unknowable thing.

One also should not think that God can be fully known.

The path of knowing has a beginning; but no end.

## Mantra: three

It is possible to understand God properly.

But not possible to understand fully.

Brahma is not satisfied even after telling it in so many ways. Siva also is not satisfied listening to it so many times. For that reason, Brahma presents it again from a different angle.

Yasyamatam tasya matam, matam yasya na veda sah

avijnatam vijanatam vijnatam-avijnatam \|\|3\|\|

(If someone thinks that, It has not been grasped through reflection, It is indeed grasped in his reflection; he who thinks he has grasped It through reflection doesn’t know. Those who think they know, It is not known to them. Who think they don’t know, It is known to them.)

This is a complete Anustup Chandas: 8+8+8+8 = 32 The details of this meter has been mentioned earlier.

Let us agree that God can’t be known fully. However much it is known, it is too little.

It is a drop of that infinite ocean. A mere ‘Dahra’ micro drop.

Alright. But that drop at least can be fully known?

That too is not possible. The awareness of that drop too is too small.

This is a remark here: yasyamatam tasya matam.

A beautiful remark. Who doesn’t think that he has fully reflected about God, he is the one who has reflected well about God.

People say: this person has reflected a great deal about God; He is a great spiritual practitioner.

Ask him. He tells: No no, I didn’t do much. I don’t know about my God. My thinking, reflection, about Him is not at all sufficient.

He is the one who knows; one who has reflected well. That is why he says so with modesty.

There are some other practitioners. They go around telling like this: ‘Who else is there, who knows as much as I do? Who has reflected as much as I have done and understood?’: konyosti sadrso maya?

Such persons don’t know anything. Their reflection is very thin. God apart, they don’t know about themselves; they don’t know about their own foolishness. That is why they talk like that.

Yasya = whose, (Iti matam = conception is that), Amatam = (God is) not fully graspable in reflection, Tasya = to him, Matam = (God) is graspable.

Yasya = who, (Iti matam = knows that), matam = (God is) fully graspable in reflection, Sah = he, (from reflection) Na Veda = has not understood anything.

The magic of the words which follow is fascinating. It is a golden crest to the tower of talks we had so far.

*avijnatam vijanatam vijnatam avijanatam*

Those who know it, do not know; those who do not know it, know it.

‘Janatam’ means ‘To the knowers’.

‘Vijanatam’ means ‘To those knowing fully’.

Here, it means ‘to those who think that they have understood comprehensively’: vijanatami iti janatam.

It is understood that God can be understood only partially. Therefore someone thought like this: I have understood a portion of God fully. But His portion is also infinite. A fraction also is infinite.

Therefore, even a little bit also cannot be understood fully.

If anyone thinks contrary to this, it means he has not understood anything.

Vijanatam = to those who think they know fully, (that Brahman is), Avijnatam = not known.

Avijanatam = those who think that they don’t know (the Brahman is), Vijnatam = It is known to them.

Who know that they know everything, they don’t know anything.

Who don’t know that they know everything, they know.

Who think that they have understood God completely, they don’t know God at all.

Who think that they don’t know about God at all, it is already known to them, that God is an enormous thing, that it cannot be known comprehensively at all.

Such a person has advanced ahead in the path of understanding.

We go to Badarinath.

We saw the Himalayas, we say.

It is true that we did see. But what we have not seen is even more true.

We had seen what came across our eyes on the way, only that much.

The other large portion of the Himalayas, we had not seen at all. Knowing God also is something like this.

What is seen is only a bit. Unseen is endless.

This awareness verily is the vision.

We grasped what was seen; and also grasped that the unseen was not seen.

To those who don’t know truth, to explain them the truth, such seemingly contradicting riddles are needed.

Those who know it, do not know it and those who do not know it, know it.

This is poetic exclamation. Spiritual poetry.

When experience is ripe, it becomes spiritual.

Ripened poetry turns into Upanisats.

Then, through contradictory talks, it becomes possible to enlighten unconflicting Truth.

Not knowing is not a disqualification. Not knowing also is a qualification to knowing. It is a natural quality of man.

It is not a fault that one doesn’t know. But not knowing that one doesn’t know is a fault.

Those who know this, don’t need much time to understand.

Those who don’t know this, don’t have any chance at all of knowing.

Such ones don’t go to those who know; don’t ask them; don’t understand by asking them; don’t grow by understanding.

Because, they don’t know that they don’t know.!

One who knows this, he succeeds.

Thus realizing the limitation of our understanding, making efforts to understand God according to our capacity, is real practice.

The following should be kept in mind, against this background of practice: The power controlling this entire universe is residing within me; it controls me also; It controls all my functions staying in all my sense-organs.

It is the origin; I am just a reflection.

He is the Puppeteer; I am just a puppet.

*pratah-prabhrti sayantam sayadi pratarantatah*

*yat karomi jagannatha tadastu tava pujanam \|\|*

*pratah-kaladikam karma nityam naimittikam tatha*

*kesavaradhanarthaya karisyeham tadainaya \|\|*

*mayi sthito madangesu svatantro vyaptavan harih*

*saravasah svatma-tantro mam svecchaya vartayatyajah \|\|*

*svatmanas-tasya pujas syuh-mano-vak-kaya-vrttayah*

*mamaita iti kartaramuddesyam ca harim smaret \|\|*

(From dawn to dusk, from dusk to dawn, whatever I do, may it be your worship, Oh, Jagannatha. The day to day and incidental duties which I do from morning onwards as per His command, may it be the worship of God Kesava. God Hari is within me. He is absolutely free and pervades all my organs. As per His wish, He guides me in all my works. All these functions that I perform through my mind, words and body, belong to Him who is residing within me. Thus, one should reflect that everything is done for Him and the doer of everything is He.)

The words referred to here also is the same: The one who per vades our body and makes it move is God: srotasya srotram manaso manah...

How can I understand such a Power? But I don’t have a choice than to know Him.

It is not possible to know It. And it is not possible also not to know It.

The same is the prayer in Gayatri: dhiyo yo nah praccodayat. Let us meditate upon that power which stimulates all our insights.

## Mantra: four
