The real Great one is God.

These are nothing in front of Him.

These don’t have the capacity to move even a leaf of grass! From this perspective, no one is great.

But it is alright to consider, one is greater than the other, in a relative sense.

This sense of hierarchy is the basic nature of the world. One is not like another. All are different. One is superior and another is inferior. The entire world is like a ladder, the steps to climb towards God. If it is seen from this angle, Vayu is on a higher step than Agni, and Indra than Vayu.

Below these three, there are hundreds of steps.

From this perspective, these three are the upper ones.

The womb of the word ‘Iva’ of the Upanisat is filled with all these ideas.

It was said earlier that these three are greater than the other Devas;

*ati-taramivanyan devan*

Who are these other Devas?

They are the nine crore Devas like Mitra, Varuna, Asvins, Ganesa, Kubera, Parjanya and so on.

There are only 34 Devas above Agni.

Only 27 are above Vayu.

Above Indra there are only 8 Devas.

All the rest of the 9 crore Devas are below these three.

Therefore the Upanisat tells: ati-taramivanyan devan

Why did they become greater than the rest? The succeeding statement provides the answer:

*te hyenat nedistham pasprsuh*

Ḍṛ = they, Pasprsuh hi = touched very much, Etat = this Yaksa, Nedistham = from close.

This ‘touching’ is not with hand; it is touching with the eye; touching with the Mind.

Agni and Vayu, touched the Yaksa first with the eye, and later through the Mind,

Indra touched Yaksa through mind at the first time itself.

Here, to touch is to grasp, to recognize.

If a thing reaches mind very deep, we say ‘it is heart-touching’, this is like that.

This Yaksa episode touched their mind very deep.

It made them reflect very deep.

For that reason, Upanisat says ‘Pasprsuh’

The word ‘Nedistham’ speaks about the closeness of space and also the closeness of Time.

They saw the Yaksa from close and understood. Not just that, they also understood It quicker than the others.

The next section of the sentence clarifies it:

*te hyanat prathamo vidancakara bhahmeti*

Te = they, Vidancakara = understood (each as individuals), Eenat = this Yaksa, Brahma iti = as Brahman, Prathamah = as the first ones (to see It).

This is a strange usage.

The noun is in plural number: te.

The verb is in singular: vidancakara.

The adjective of the noun also: prathamah.

It is something like saying, ‘they understands!’ What does it mean? What type of language it is. What kind of grammar?

The wise ones have a tradition of breaking the grammar, to flash new meanings.

The foolish ones write, contrary to the grammar because of their ignorance.

The wise ones write, deliberately breaking grammar, to give a new dimension to the language.

‘They’ understood, as the first one.

But they didn’t understand at the same time.

Before everyone could, Indra understood Him.

Agni and Vayu understood after Indra.

After Vayu, Agni understood, before the remaining nine crore Devas could.

To show this gap, the hierarchy, this kind of usage is employed:

*te hi prathamah vidancakara*

Among them, each one understood Him, prior to the others.

Indra was the first to know.

After Indra, Vayu was the first.

Here first means second.

After Vayu, Agni is the first to understand.

Here, first means the third.

In this sentence, the syllable ‘Hi’ is there. It is a way of convincing the listener.

The thing that they reached first, came to know first, was already known, when the story was heard. Should it be again explained? The adverb ‘Hi’ conveys all these things.

It is a thing already known to you. So it doesn’t need any explanation, does it?

What is the need of the effort, to prove a thing which is already well known?

## Prose: three

The hierarchy indicated by the singular number is clarified by the following sentence:

*tasmad va indrah-atitaramivanyan devan, sa hyenannedistham paspar, sa hyenat prathamo vidancaraka brahmeti \|\|3\|\|*

(Verily by that, Indra became greater than the rest of the Devas; Because he was the one who experienced the Brahman from close. Indeed he was the one who understood It as Brahman first among the rest.)

Indra also became superior very much because of understanding God first: tasmad vai.

Among these three, Indra is much closer to God. The first two, saw God from close, yet didn’t recognize Him.

Indra didn’t see Him first, yet understood Him without seeing.

Seeing is not what matters; understanding matters.

They didn’t ‘see’ Him, despite seeing; this one ‘saw’ Him, despite not seeing.

That is why the Upanisat stresses again and again that Indra is superior to the rest of the Devas.

Tasmad vai = for that very reason, Indrah = Indra, Atitaram iva = stands surpassing, Anyan Devan = the other Devas.

Like earlier, here also the word ‘Vai’ is present.

And also the word ‘Iva’

The special meaning of these words should be understood, like it was done earlier. This is another example of, how the prose gains a new power, by using similar words repeatedly.

If we are to taste the natural beauty of Sanskrit, we have to surrender to the Upanisats.

In the hands of the later poets, Sanskrit prose became very artificial, due to complicated long compound-words. Language became the testing-stone of the poets, became an uproar.

Indra became the superior not only to the other Devas, he surpassed Agni and Vayu too.

Because he found the answer to Yaksa’s problem before those two did:

*sa hyenat nedistham pasparsa*

Sah = That Indra, Pasparsa hi = touched indeed, Enat = this Brahman in Yaksa form, Nedistham = very close, above everyone, before everyone.

Here also it goes as it was earlier. Touching means, touching with mind, understanding.

The same is told by the following words:

*sa hyenat nedistham pasparsa*

Sah = that Indra, Vidancakara Hi= understood, Enat = this Yaksa, Brahma iti = is Brahman, Prathamah = first among the others.

Thus, Indra understood first among the three.

From him Vayu understood and Agni from Vayu.

Therefore this Indra is superior to these two, so tells the Upanisat.

Here only the name of the three Devas is mentioned.

Behind this, there are some material and spiritual aspects. Without understanding that, this cannot be understood fully.

In the first two sections, there is a reference to the five sense- organs: The eye, ear, nose, mouth and mind.

In it, two Deities of the senses are mentioned here: Agni, the deity of speech and Vayu the deity of breath.

In the physical form also, the three worlds are there: Bhuh, Bhuvah and Svah. The first portion is mouth, the site of Agni. That verily is the site of earth, Agni is very much the residing deity of the earth.

The second portion is nose, the site of Vayu. That verily is the site of Bhuvah, the sky. Vayu is the residing deity of the sky.

The third portion is eye, ear and forehead; the site of Sun. That verily is the site of Svah, the heaven. Sun is the deity of the heaven.

On earth, God is worshipped through the image of Agni. In the sky, God is worshipped through the image of Vayu. In the heaven, through the image of Sun.

First Agni, the deity of earth went to the Yaksa. But he didn’t understand.

Next Vayu, the deity of sky went. He too didn’t understand.

To the site of heaven, instead of Sun, Indra went. That much is the difference.

While Sun is the deity of heaven, Indra is the deity of all the three, the earth, sky and heaven.

Indra is born also from the face of God: mukhadindrascagnisca.

Agni is the deity merely of speech.

Indra also is the deity of speech; and the deity of nose, eye and mind also.

He is the collective deity of all the sense organs.

Therefore, it is appropriate that he goes at the end to see the Yaksa.

Etymologists too say: ‘in the site of heaven, sometimes Indra is referred to, in place of Sun’.

Acarya Sankara expresses a doubt, that the Indra of this Upanisat might be Sun also.

In fact, Indra here is not Sun. He is very much Devendra. Here he is presented specially as the deity of mind.

Thus, the three sense organs are referred here; speech, breath and mind.

First, the speech went to the Yaksa. But didn’t reach God.

Power of breath, Prana went. But it too couldn’t know God.

Finally the mind went. It also couldn’t see God. However, by the kindness of the goddess of speech, and with the help of listening and reflecting, the same mind found God in Dhyana — meditation. So the story of the spiritual practice is narrated through Agni, Vayu and Indra. This war between Devas and Asuras becomes very much the story happening within us. This is our own Mahabharata. The war of Kauravas and Pandavas, taking place in the Kuruksetra of our hearts.

In this war within us too, Asuras win and Devas also win.

Mostly the Asuras win; off and on Devas succeed.

Then we imagine: ‘very much because of us this happened. We won very much because of our power. ’

This is the story of Mahabharata. The story of Talavakara.

Talavakara is a micro-commentary of Mahabharata

The three sense-organs appearing in the previous chapter appeared here also.

Those sense - Deities are the actors of this Yaksa episode.

But the reference of eye and ear, Sun and Moon has not come here.

There is a reason for not taking them up here:

Agni, the deity of speech-organ, is in the lower portion of the face.

Vayu is the deity above him. His site, the nose also is above the mouth.

Eye and ear are above the nose.

But their Deities, Sun and Moon are not superior to Vayu, they are below him.

Pravaha Vayu is on the fifth step.

Sun and Moon are on the fourth step.

Agni is on the first.

For that reason, Indra on the eight step has been taken up here, leaving aside Sun and Moon.

In the context of practice, these three Deities can be seen from a different point of view.

Agni is the Deity of Citta — intellect. Citta is a basket of memory.

Recollecting God, reflecting His names are done through Citta. With the help of Agni, God was reflected in the mind.

But God could not be reached.

Pravaha - Vayu is the Deity of the power of knowledge.

What is power of knowledge?

It is knowledge combined with logic.

It is confirming the already known thing further with logic.

Still he could not reach God.

First, reflection through scripture was done.

God couldn’t be reached.

Now, with the help of logic the confusion were cleared. The understanding of the scriptures were reinforced.

Now, there was a greater development.

Yet God was not seen.

Indra is the Deity of great talent.

What is meant by great talent?

It is the inner flash seasoned with experience, the inner flare.

In that flicker of heart, God’s form flashed.

This very much is the episode of Agni, Vayu and Indra here.

This verily is the srotavyah mantavyah nididhyasitavyah.

Reflection of scriptures was done in Citta, through Agni.

That verily is ‘Srotavyah’ - one who should be heard.

God who is to be heard was heard.

Then the reflection of scriptures was confirmed with logic, through Pravaha Vayu.

That verily is ‘Mantavyah’ - to be reflected.

God, who is to be reflected was reflected.

After that, mind became steady in Dhyana and remained in God. That verily is ‘Dhyatavyah’ - to be meditated.

God who is to be meditated was meditated.

As a result, He was seen; there was revelation:

*atma va are drasitvyah srotavyo mantavyo nididhyasitavyah…*

There was a Mantra in the second section: pratibodhaviditam matam...

Now a new meaning emerges to this Mantra.

In the earlier sense we had seen, ‘Pratibodha’ and ‘Viditam’ were different words.

Now it becomes a single word, getting compounded: pratibodha+viditam.

First, it is ‘Matam’.

It is the awareness of God, through reflection.

By that, here emerges Pratibodha; inner flash, inspiration.

From Pratibodha, in the glow of Dhyana, God flares out.

Thus, when God is revealed ‘vidita’, the devotee overcomes death: amrtatvam hi vindate.

To know Narayana, to move up in awareness, these three turned into three steps.

Mercy of these three is essential to climb that mansion.

But the steps to the mansion are not just three.

The mentioned ones are three and the unmentioned ones are many.

To show the three stages of worship, only these three steps are mentioned here.

In reality, this upper story of the mansion has fourteen steps.

The very first step is Agni.

When the fourteen steps are climbed, in the fifteenth step itself, the God is present.

Aitareya Sruti tells this:

*agnirvai devanam-avamah, visnuh paramah tadantarenanye devah*

If we take up nine crore Devas, below Agni there are still some more steps.

In it fifteen steps are important ones.

It is a journey from Agni to Narayana - in Agni.

It is a journey from the site of earth to the site of Salvation.

Visnu is on the other end, Agni is on this end. In between are the rest of the Devas.

Here again, another two divisions.

In the mid-point between Agni and Narayan, Indra is there.

From Agni to Indra, the steps are covered with nescience.

After that, Parvati is present, who had preached Indra. She is not veiled with nescience.

That is why she is in the upper stage. She didn’t have doubts about God.

Below Indra it is the stage of enquiry. Above Indra, it is the stage of wisdom.

Over there Suparni, wife of Garuda, is present with Parvati; Varuni, Sesa’s wife also is present. Garuda, Sesa and Rudra are there. Saraswati and Bharati, Brahma and Vayu too are present. Beyond them there are Laksmi and Narayana. Indra is in between; seven stages are above and seven stages are below him.

Totally, there are fifteen stages, fifteen steps. Agni is on the first step. Narayana is on the fifteenth step.

Those standing closer to God are the Uttamas, the superior ones respectively.

Those staying farther are Avamas., the lower ones. This is the measuring scale for the hierarchy of the Devas.

Now, first let us see the fifteen stages, the steps of hierarchy:

1\. Agni, Bhrgu and Daksa’s wife, Prasuti.

2\. Narada.

3\. Varuna.

4\. Candra, Surya and Yama.

5\. Pravaha-Vayu

6\. Aniruddha, Svayambhuvamanu, Daksaprajapati, Brhaspati, Sachi - Indra’s wife and Rati - Kama’s wife.

7\. Ahankarikaprana.

8\. Indra and Kama.

9\. Parvati - wife of Siva, Varuni - Sesa’s wife and Suparni - wife of Garuda.

10\. Six wives of Krsna: Nila, Bhadra, Mitravinda, Kalindi, Laksana and Jambavati.

11\. Garuda, Sesa and Siva.

12\. Saraswati and Bharati.

13\. Brahma and Vayu.

14\. Laksmi

15\. Narayana

Yaksa, by becoming a question, didn’t trouble any one above Indra, from Parvati to Brahma and Vayu.

They didn’t have the pride of triumph nor any doubt about the Yaksa.

They are present to remove the doubts of those in the lower eight

stages.

They are very close to God.

In the next eight lower stages, the first one to understand God is Indra.

Therefore, he becomes superior to the Devas of the eight stages from Agni to Ahankarika Prana.

In Indra’s stage, there is another one. He is Kama.

He is the one who was curious to know, before the others, and advised them to know.

By that, he became equal to Indra.

Daksa, Manu, Guru, Aniruddha, Surya, Candra, Varuna, and others are the ones who accepted Kama’s advice.

By that they became the lower ones to Indra.

Though Pravaha Vayu saw God, he could grasp Him only after Anirudhdha, Guru, Manu, and Daksa.

Therefore, he became lower to them.

Agni went before everyone, but understood after everyone did. That made his step the last one.

Thus, the variations in the time-scale of understanding God became the reason of the corresponding hierarchy of the Devas.

This is the account of the principal Devas.

The rest of the nine crore Devas are in the lower steps.

Yet, they all are above us.

They are closer to God than us.

Because, we are still in search of the steps!

## Prose: four

We know that this entire Upanisat is a commentary of the Gayathri.

The subject discussed so far is the explanation of the last line of Gayathri:

*dhiyo yo nah pracodayat*

Now we get two meanings to the word ‘dhiyah’. They are, the sense-organs and their presiding Deities.

God is the one who prompts our organs of knowledge, towards their objects.

God is the one, who prompts the Deities of the sense-organs also, residing within them.

On the whole, God stimulates all the states, motivates all the sense-organs and regulates all the Devas.

In three chapters, the Upanisat explained the intention of the third line of Gayathri.

The coming section is the explanation of the first two lines of Gayatri.

*tasaisa adesah, yadetad vidyuto vyadyutadaaa iti nyamimisadaaa iti adhidaivatam \|\|4\|\|*

(This is a special preaching of God: The power that flashes the glow of lightening and so on all over, the Power that is asleep with closed eyes, in Yogic-slumber, this verily is God. This is the way of Adhidaiva practice.)

Caturmukha tells Siva: tasaisa adesah.

Listen to the exceptional qualities of God, who showed Himself as the Yaksa.

Esah = this, Adesah = is the preaching regarding the exceptional qualities, Tasya = of that God.

‘Desa’ means ‘Updadesa’ - preaching. For that reason ‘Desika’ means Guru who teaches.

‘A+desa’ is a special preaching, the preaching of special attributes of God for specific practice.

What is the special practice?

*yadetad vidyuto vyadyutadaaa iti*

Here, ‘Vidyut’ doesn’t mean, just the lightening.

It is all the lustres that brightens the universe:

*vi+dyotante iti vidyutah. dyuta diptau*

The Sun, Moon, Stars and Lightening that shine in the sky and the fire on the earth too is ‘Vidyut’.

This reference comes in Kathopanisat:

*na tatra suryo bhati na candratarakam*

*nema vidyuto bhanti kutoyamagnih*

*tameva bhantamanu bhati sarvam*

*tasya bhasa sarvamidam vibhati*

(Sun’s light won’t glow there, neither moon and stars. Nor do these lightenings will shine there. Then what about this poor fire? Following His light all these glow; verily by his light, all these shine.)

There is a statement in Bhagavadgita:

*yadadityagatam tejo jagat bhasayatekhilam*

*yaccandramasi yaccangau tat tejo viddhi mamakam*

(The Light that brightens the universe, residing within Sun, the Light within moon and fire, you understand that all that is mine.)

Candra’s light is not his own; it is a reflection of Sun’s light.

Upanisat tells: Not only that, Surya’s light too is a reflection of God’s glow.

That is the only real Light. All the rest are the shadows of that light.

Nothing is self-luminous. All these are mere bulbs. If He switches on there will be light, otherwise no.

‘Abhi’ means, ‘Abhivyapa’, pervading in and out.

God doesn’t shine bright, staying somewhere afar. He himself pervading inside the bulbs, illumines them; ‘Abhi+Vyadyutat.’

‘Vyadyutat’ (Vi + Adyutat) is also a strange usage. To mean ‘ he lights’, it should be ‘Vyadyotayat’.

Such usage is common in other languages also The same word is used for both the sense like, ‘he lights’ and ‘ he makes it light’ or ‘he gets it lit’.

Similarly, here also, in place of ‘Vyadyotayat’ using ‘Vyadyotata’ is alright.

It doesn’t end here.

Here ‘Vyadyotata’ also got shortened and became ‘Vyadyutat’.

All actions of God are for others sake. To indicate that He doesn’t need anything for Himself, the Atmanepada - voice for oneself (Vyadyotata) became Parasmaipada - voice for another (Vyadyotat).

And again, the ‘o’ syllable of the root became short ‘u’ syllable; ‘Vyadyotat’ became ‘Vyadyutat’.

The short form of this word connotes, how these material lights are dwarfish in front of God’s light; ad infinitum.

The later ‘pluta’- the lengthened syllable (Vidyutat + Aa) echoes the wonder of this difference between God’s Light and the Physical lights.

Ah! What a strange that non-material Light of God is! Despite lighting all these, It is hiding in the dark, without being seen! There is no other light to show It! What a wonder!

Ah! It is a complete Light; and these are the fragments of light!

That is an ocean; these are mere droplets!

From That, this is lighted.

That doesn’t get lighted by this.

*na tad bhasayate suryo, na sasanko na pavakah,*

*na tatra suryo bhati...*

The fact of these words appearing in the Gita and Srutis, the Seer of this Upanisat relishes slurps with joy, with the single lengthened syllable, Aaa.

Yat Etat = that which, Vidyutah, Vi + adyutat = makes all lights luminous (it is God). Iti = so is, (Tasya = the God’s Adesah = advice for the special practice), Aaa = what a wonder it is, the fullness of that Light!

‘That is how, when It opens its eye, the whole world is aglow; if It blinks, it is dark all over’, tell the next words: nyamimisadaaa ityadhidaivatam.

Ni amimisat = (verily That which lightens the world) is sleeping with eyes closed. Iti = so is (Tasya = that God’s, Adesah = advice for that special practice), Adhidaivatam = about that Diving power that engulfs the universe, Aaa = what a wonder it is, that response in Yoga!

What else can it be other than wonder?

Sleepless God, sleeping with closed eyes!

What else can be a greater wonder than this?

What is all this?

In one place, It lights everything.

In another, It is sleeping with closed eyes!

These are the two faces of Creation and Dissolution.

God opened His eyes. And Sun was born. It became day; there came the light. The process of Creation began.

The Creation continued, the light turned bright.

The span of Creation concluded. 432 crore years were over.

Again the Dissolution. A night of 432 crore years; Yoga-repose.

This is one day and one night of Caturmukha.

It expanded still wider; The universe grew, well lit, one full life of Brahma. A span of 31104 thousand crore years is the time of light. It is the time of the expansion of the Creation. All the worlds and lights became bright:

*suryacandramasau dhata yathapurvamakalpayat*

*divam ca prthivim cantarikshamatho suvah*

Earth, Sky and Heaven were created.

Expanding, the three became seven. Seven became fourteen. Surya and Candra lighted the worlds: vidyuto vyadyutat.

Again the dissolution lasting 31104 thousand crore years. God, putting aside the Creation, slept off, closing His eyes: nyamimisat.

But what a surprise! Such an incredibly long time is not even fraction of a moment to Him: A.

He slept 31104 thousand crore year: at the same time He didn’t sleep even for a moment!

We can only react to it opening our mouth wide - ‘Aaa!’ - in big wonderment.

Which mind can capture the infinite pranks of Him,

Which counting can catch Him, who is beyond time.

There was nothing during the Pralaya - the dissolution.

But, It was there.

How was it? It was asleep. Yet It was awake.

Time for Creation came. Everything emerged out from It.

It lighted everything. But that Light itself couldn’t be seen. The wonder of this contradiction and the complexity is expressed competently by the lengthy syllable ‘Aaa’.

Because it is an experience beyond words.

It is difficult task as to how to express it.

If we think about it, it thrills us; no words are there to express.

The same is the saying in the beginning of Gayatri:

*tat savituh varenyam bhargah*

The ‘Bhargah’ referred here is again light.

The basic meaning is ‘burning light’:

*bharjate iti bhargah, bhrja bharjane*

Savitr means not mere Sun. It means, Sun and all the lights like Sun.

‘Light flows’, we say. ‘Light moves faster than everything’ we say.

‘Savita’ also has the same meaning:

*suvati iti savita, su gatau*

One who moves is Savita.

The sun who seems to move from east to west, sunrise to sunset is Savita. Similarly, all the moving lights too are Savita.

Not only that. Our activities commence, when light dawns. All activities cease when it is night.

Light is the prompter of our movements and actions. Therefore too, the light is Savita: su prerane.

The Big-Light (Varenyam Bhargah); that brightens such lights, is of Narayana.

This is referred to in Padmapurana also:

*tapani pacika caiva sosani ca prakasani*

*naiva rajan raves saktih saktirnarayanasya sa*

(Burning, cooking, drying, brightening- none of these are the power of Sun. They are the power of Narayana)

What is this form, that lights all the other lights?

Scholars say that this form, mentioned in the Mantra of Talavakara, is ‘Kapila Vasudeva’.

Now a new dimension comes to the deliberations of Gayatri.

In the first half of Gayatri, this Kapilavasudeva form is also worshipped.

Now the next is about the sleep with closed eyes.

This too comes in Gayatri: devasya.

‘Deva’ means, a sleeping person also:

*divyati iti devah, divu swapne*

The wakefulness of the creation, and the sleep of the dissolution. When the eye opens, sun and moon are created with the cosmos; when the eye closes, it is the darkness of dissolution.

Bhagavata tells about this:

*sa vai kilayam purusah puratano*

*ya eka asidavisesa atmani*

*agre gunebhyo jagadatmanisvare*

*mimilitatama nisi suptasaktisu*

(Isn’t this Krsna that Puranapurusa - the ancient one? The one who doesn’t have differences in His forms and qualities. The one, who stood alone, eyes closed, in darkness of dissolution, prior to the creation of the cosmos of three qualities, when all Deities were asleep within Him, the Master of the Universe.)

Thus is the worship of God, who is beyond time, in the infinite wheel of creation and dissolution.

*ityadhidaivatam*

Iti = thus, Adhidaivatam = is the worship of the power of God, pervading in the presiding Deities of the universe.

## Prose: five

After the practice of Adhidaiva, it is the worship of Adhyatma - related to Self. The worship of power of God in the physical form:

*athadhyatmam yadetad gacchativa ca manonena*

*cainadupa smaratyabhiksnam sankalpah \|\|5\|\|*

(Now the worship of God inside the body: The feeling that the mind dreaming unhindered, moves towards Him, is also due to Him. It is due to Him only that the mind becomes capable of remembering Him.)

The One which brightens the light of the universe, that verily brightens the light of the physical form.

Sun, moon etc, are the light of the cosmos. Mind and sense-organs are the light of the human body. Practice in the cosmos is Adhidaiva. Worship in the body is Adhyatma.

Through the practice in human body, Upanisat moves into the third line of Gayatri: dhiyo yo nah pracodayat.

That Power which stimulates the functions of the organs of knowledge that verily is mentioned here: anena cainadupasmarati.

In the worship of Adhidaiva - the Divine principal that provides light for the creation of the world; the principle that provides darkness for the dissolution.

The same principle staying in the human body in our sense-organs, regulates them. It especially regulates the mind. The rest of the sense-organs are behind the mind.

If the mind stops functioning, eye can’t see, ear can’t hear.

All the sense-organs are regulated by mind. Things are grasped through the mind.

We say, ‘mind was somewhere; therefore though we saw it was not seen. Mind was somewhere; so though we listened it was not heard.’

Mind’s rein is behind everything.

Mind moves towards worldly things.

Mind flows towards God.

Does it really flow?

It is true that it seems like flowing.

We sit down in Dhyana. With the brush of mind, we paint the form of God.

Watching His form, the mind dances in joy.

We say, ‘the mind flows towards God’.

But don’t say ‘my mind saw God, that was possible because of my efforts’.

‘I didn’t send the mind towards God, God Himself came and stayed there. This awareness should remain alert during the worship’, tells Parvati, the deity of speech: yadetat gacchativa ca manah.

This is one face of ‘Gacchati + iva’.

Neither we nor our mind, have control over the movement of the mind. It is the kindness of God.

For that reason, the Upanisat says, ‘Gacchati iva - seems like it goes’ instead of ‘Gacchati’ it goes.

There is a tone of an absence of freedom in this word ‘Iva’.

It has still another aspect:

The word ‘Iva’ presents two opposed things together.

‘Gacchativa’ means, it goes and also it doesn’t go.

It seems like it goes. But it doesn’t go completely.

It grasps a drop from the ocean.

A drop was seen; but the ocean was not seen.

We say ‘I went to Badrinath, saw the Himalayas’.

This is like that.

What was seen was a portion of the peak.

The unseen portion of the Himalaya is enormous.

That is why the Upanisat says: gacchativa ca manah.

Mind goes towards God; but it never goes completely.

Even the little bit of moving towards God, little bit of thinking about Him, was possible, only due to His kindness, not my mind’s own strength.

*anena cainadupasmarati*

Anena ca = because of this God, Yat etat = this, Manah = mind, Gacchati iva = seems like moving (towards God), Ca = and, Upasmarati = thinks (about), Enat = this God.

‘Smarati’ means thinks, reflects.

Here it has come as ‘Upasmarati’.

‘Upa’ means ‘high’ and also ‘closeby’.

The mind was wandering closeby, in this world.

Afterwards it climbed high. And then it started to wander closer to God.

Thinking about God is not possible, without climbing high.

However much it climbs up, it can see God only from a closer distance.

But it cannot fully understand Him.

Those having greater good qualities go more close to Him.

Those having lesser qualities go less close to Him.

That is the reason, the Sruti tells:‘Upasmarati’.

Mind grasps the things. It ruminates, munches the grasped things again and again, recollecting it. All these are the play of God’s kindness.

God, who gives light from outside, to Sun, Moon and so on, gives the light of awareness from inside.

What is this form that regulates the mind? Those knowing well say that it is ‘Aniruddha’ form.

‘Kapila’ is the one who gives light from outside.

‘Aniruddha’ is the one who gives light from inside.

Now the later half of the Gayatri also gets a new dimension:

*dhi yo yo nah pracodayat*

This is a meditation on Anirudhdha, who stimulates the power of intellect.

As mentioned earlier, in the first half of the Gayatri, Kapila Vasudeva is spoken about; and Anirudhdha in the later half.

This is a rare new face of examining, - Anusandhana.

What is the real nature of the mind, that climbs high by meditation, grasps God and reflects about him?

*abhiksnam sankalpah*

Abhiksnam = all the time, Sankalpah = seeing dreams of desire. Mind doesn’t have eye, neither the ear.

It munches the things seen by the outer eye.

It munches the things heard from the outer ear.

The external five sense-organs provide food for the mind, and the mind munches it again and again.

That is the nature of the mind: abhiksnam sankalpah.

The eye didn’t see God.

The ear didn’t hear about God.

Then, how can the mind think about God?

Mind should make an effort to move high, above the level of the worldly things, fed by the external sense-organs.

As it moves higher, it gets closer to God. Height is closeness. For that, the mind should be up-focussed, up-faced.

When the mind frees itself from this mesh of material world and moves high, it ‘Upasmarati’- it thinks about It, from close.

But what is the real nature of the mind?: abhiksnam sankalpah.

How does it become possible for the mind to move high, away from this mesh of desires?

It is not due to our efforts.

He carried it high. So it went.

Or, He came down for our sake, and stayed in our mind.

This verily is called ‘Avatara’ - coming down, incarnation.

Baby tried to climb the storey. It could not. Each time it tried the steps, it slipped and fell down.

Then the mother comes. She takes up the baby, and carries it up to the upper storey.

God too is like that.

Mind tries to climb up. It could not.

Whenever it tried, it fell down due to the slippery moss of temptations.

He came down and carried it up.

Otherwise, how would it be possible for the mind, to climb the height?

Is it ever possible to escape away from the mesh of the worldly desires? Abhiksnam sankalpah.

## Prose: six

Aniruddha stood inside and showed compassion.

Mind stood up overcoming its base nature.

It got ready to move up.

Moving high, it desired to reflect about that highest principle. How should it be reflected upon?

*taddha tadvanam nama tadvanamityupasitavyam,*

*sa ya etadevam vedabhi hainam sarvani bhutani sam vanchanti \|\|6\|\|*

(Tadvana is the name of that Brahman. It should be understood as ‘Tadvana’ and meditated. All the living beings will adore such a person, who understands it, in this way)

Upanisat suggests a unique name for the worship of God: tadvanam.

God is ‘Tat’. God is ‘Vanam’, therefore, his name is ‘Tad+Vanam’.

This is the God’s secret name, like ‘Tatvamasi’: like ‘ Ahamasmi’.

It is a secret name, but well known to scholars.

It is very much employed in the practice of the erudite scholars.

‘Ha’ syllable shows out that practice of the scholars; tat ha.

Tat = that Brahman, Tad vanam nama ha = is famous as ‘Tadvana’.

The word ‘Nama’, can suggest fame also. Then, this ‘Ha’ syllable, enjoys the bliss of this worship.

What does it mean?

Why is God named as ‘Tad Vanam’?

Superficial meaning is this;

‘Tat’ means that; that which is far off and not seen to us.

God too is not seen. Therefore He is ‘Tat’.

‘Vana’ means forest; an impenetrable maze of a jungle.

God too is impenetrable like a jungle. Therefore he is ‘Vana’.

So he is ‘Tad Vana’. A thing that remains unseen, unimaginable and impenetrable.

Well, somewhat we understood. But it is too thin a meaning, too superficial a meaning.

Though it is understood, it doesn’t seem to have been understood. After knowing what has been said, we still feel it is not enough, there must be something more.

This very restlessness is the beginning of the search of the Upanisat.

Upanisat is not a superficial literature. There must be some deeper thing in it. For that, one should go deeper, dive deeper.

In depth one should search, and in the search one should pick.

Pick the shells of pearls, and break them open and see.

What precious pearls and gems!

What is this gem of a meaning?

‘Tat’ doesn’t mean only ‘That’.

‘Tat’ is that which ‘Tata’; which is spread everywhere.

That which is spread everywhere with good qualities is ‘Tat’; spread in time is ‘Tat’; spread in space is ‘Tat’.

It is there, it is here too.

It is far, it is nearby too.

*durad durataram yattu tadevantikamantikat*

It is beyond all the things which are far off.

It is also very close by, than all the things which are very nearby.

*tad dure tadvantike*

It is outside, it is also inside.

It is in the cosmos, it is also in the human body.

It is very much inside me.

It is ‘That’, and it is ‘This’ too.

That is why it is ‘Tata’.

This reference comes in Geeta:

*om tat saditi nirdeso brahmanas-trividhas-smrtah*

Like ‘om’, ‘Tat’ also is a secret name of God. Besides the importance of the meaning, it is also a symbol of Gayatri.

The combination of the first and the last syllable of Gayatri be comes ‘Tat’.

It is the abbreviated form of Gayatri.

*tat saviturvarenyam bhargo devasya dhimahi dhiyo yo nah pracodayat*

The beginning ‘Ḍṁ’ and the ending ‘t’, joining together became ‘Tat’.

Inside the egg of ‘Tat’, the whole Gayatri lies hidden.

And, all the three Vedas lie hidden too, like in ‘om’.

The other face that comes to be known is ‘Vana’. Tad vanam.

‘Vana’ means what? Why is God ‘Vana’?

‘Vana’ means that thing, which everyone should consume, vana sambhaktau, vanyate iti vanam.

The thing, which everybody sings and praises is ‘Vana’.

‘Vana’ is the thing which is the meaning of every word, vana sabde.

‘Vana’ means that thing which should be meditated by everyone. Vanu smarane.

‘Vana’ means that thing which should be prayed by everyone for release from this worldly bondage, vanu yacane.

‘Vana’ means that which should be worshipped, revered with devotion. For this reason, he is called ‘Bhaga’ also. Bhaja sevayam.

‘Bhajana’ means Bhakti-Devotion. Bhakti means love: adaro bhajanam bhaktih.

Among all the things that we should worship with devotion. He is the biggest; The master of all, who deserves to be worshipped. By that, He is ‘Vanaspati’.

This mode of worship comes in Gayatri also: tat saviyuh varenyam.

The tat of here, is the tat there also.

The vanam of here, became varenyam there.

‘Vana’ and ‘Vananiya’, both are the same.

‘Varenya’ and ‘Varaniya’, both are the same.

It is the thing which is great, which should be sought for shelter and should be worshipped:

*gunaistatah prasavita varaniyo gunonnateh*

Because of that, God is ‘Tadvana’.

That is why He should be worshipped as ‘Tadvana’:

*tadvanamityupasitavyam*

(Tat = that Brahman), Upasitavyam = should be meditated, Iti = as Tat = pervading everywhere, is full of great qualities, Vanam = is the super protection, and should be worshipped with devotion.

What is told in the Upanisat ‘ Upasitavyam’ - should meditate, Gayatri tells the same as ‘Dhimahi’ - let us meditate, and confirm it.

‘Think, that, it is greatest of all, closest of all, dearest of all’.

For, every move of your life is under Its control. Your very form is its reflection. Nothing exists without it: na rte tvat kriyate kincanare.

‘Are’ means, at far and near: arad durasamipayoh.

Whatever the thing or action be, far or near and even within us, Nothing can be done without It.

Love that supreme power of Viswakarma, which controls all the happening of the cosmos, in a matchless mode.

God is not a thing to be scared of. He should be adored.

He is not fearsome. He is a very dear one.

We should not become afraid of God like Hiranyakasipu was, who scared of God, hating him.

We should be like Prahlada, adorers of God.

Let us love that supreme principle that controls, brightens the light of the entire cosmos, and the light of our inner awareness. And adoring it, let us understand it.

What happens by understanding?

*sa ya etadevam veda*

One who understands like this, becomes dear to all the living beings.

Like this means what? That God is the most dear thing. That It should be loved by all. That It is thing very close. That It is abound ing inside and outside of every thing. That It brightens all the lights. That It sleeps with eyes closed in Yoga-slumber, during dissolution. That It does everything at all times and controls all the things. That, that is reason, why it is called as ‘Tadvanam’.

If we love God, the whole world will love us. According to our feelings and perceptions, we get the reactions.

Love begets love, hate begets hate.

*tam yathayathopasate tadeva bhavati*

This is Vedic psychology.

What you practice in your mind, only that you gain back.

If you become dear to God, you become dear to the world too. If you give love, you get back the same love:

*sarvani bhutani enam abhi sam vanchanti*

‘Bhutas’ means living beings.

This is about worldly life.

And this applies to Salvation also.

‘Bhuti’ means height, climbing high, Salvation.

Such persons who have reached high are ‘Bhutah’, those who have reached Salvation.

Such a practitioner is loved by those souls in Salvation.

God is the Mahabhuta.

*eko visnurmahad bhutam prthag bhutanyanekasah.*

*asya mahato bhutasya nisvasitametat...*

If you win over such a Mahabhuta-God, how can the other Bhutas remain unwon?

When the image comes in front, how can the reflection turn the face away?

This love is not momentary. It is a permanently imprinted love: samvanchanti. They love very much.

This love is not unidirectional, not uniregional. It comes from all directions and in all forms: abhi+vanchanti.

That state, which becomes dear to all, becomes blessed with God’s compassion, what a blissful state is that!: abhi ha.

Sa yah = one who, Veda = understands, Etat = this, Brahman, Evam = thus, Sarvani = all, Bhutani = living beings, Abhi+sam+vancanti= always, in every way, love, Enam = him, Ha = Oh, what a joy!

Don’t get denied of that bliss.

Adore God, gain the love of the world.

Live a life, dear and sought by everyone, here, there and everywhere.

## Prose: seven

The thing to be told was conveyed.

Still some questions are pending with Siva.

He asks Brahma:

*upanisadam bho bruhiti, ukta ta upanisat, brahmim vava ta upanisadamabrumeti \|\|6\|\|*

(Siva said: ‘please tell me the Upanisat’. Brahma replied: ‘you have been told’. I have told you the Upanisat regarding the Supreme God’)

Siva, who has been fully preached about God, asks once again: ‘my teacher, I want to hear about Upanisat’. If it is so, what was it that Brahma preached so far? What was it that Siva had listened to so far? To understand this complication, we need to know the sense, the import in which the word ‘Upanisat’ has been employed here.

‘Upanisat’ means elevated wisdom.

Upanisat means, the secretive wisdom.

It was already preached by Brahma and was already heard by Siva.

The literature that gives us such wisdom also is Upanisat. It is the spiritual text, that carries one high, the text which preaches the mystic knowledge.

The manner of the meditation of the practitioner, desiring to gain such knowledge, also is Upanisat. It is the mode of worship we should have in us to achieve the spiritual knowledge. It is the ethical code, life style.

Siva is asking about these two modes of the Upanisat! Which are the required texts to gain such knowledge? And, what is the ethical code that we have to practice to understand it?

We gain knowledge from our Gurus. The gained knowledge should be retained and nurtured.

But it doesn’t happen so. God has given us two ears. They are in a straight line.

The words enter this ear, and go out from that ear. Even after hearing the Upanisat, we remain as we were, without growing up. We don’t change.

What enters the ear doesn’t touch the heart.

It should not be so. What is heard should enter inside and touch the heart. Sometimes we feel that it went inside, but it doesn’t stay there. All that was gets forgotten.

What is the way to retain the heard things in memory?

What is the secret of memory? We should know that secret.

This is the question that haunts everyone.

On behalf of all, Siva had asked this question: upanisadam bho bruhiti.

Bhoh = Oh teacher, Bruhi = please tell, Upanisadam = the secret of meditation, Iti = thus (Siva asked).

The known thing should be further known.

It should be known deeper.

What are the further studies required for that?

This is the second question: upanisadam bho bruhiti.

Bhoh = Oh teacher, Bruhi = please tell, Upanisadam = about the secret of the study.

Thus, the Upanisat gets three faces:

Knowledge of Brahman, the meditation and practice to gain that, and the scriptures required to study that.

Brahma tells that the first aspect has already been told in detail:

*ukta ta upanisat*

Upanisat = the great mystic knowledge, Ukta = has taught, Te = to you.

But it is not something that can be told completely.

There is no end to it also.

Yet, the important things have been told:

*brahmim vava ta upanisadamabrumeti*

Upanisadam = the secret, Brahmim = regarding the Brahman, Abruma vava = I have told well, Ḍṛ = to you, Iti = so (said Caturmukha)

## Prose: eight

Now two more aspects remain to be told. They are, the secret of meditation and the texts to be studied for that.

The next sentence clarifies it:

*tasmai tapo damah karmeti pratistha vedah sarvangani satyamayatanam \|\|8\|\|*

(Tapas, self-control and karma- duty, are the foundations of that mystic-knowledge. All the Vedas, Vedangas - auxiliaries of Vedas and Mimamsa the text that helps to decide the Vedic meaning are the base.)

We should adopt three qualities in our life. It is, Tapas, Self-control and Action.

Upanisat stays in if they are present.

If they are missing, Upanisat doesn’t stay.

For that reason those three qualities are called as ‘Pratistha’, with regard to knowledge.

What does it mean?

That by which knowledge gets established in us is Pratistha.

Its is the mode of life, the mode of worship that establishes Vedic knowledge within us.

By these three practices of life, the knowledge settles down firmly; it remains strong without getting lost in forgetfulness.

Tasyai = to that Upanisat, Tapah = Penance, Damah = Self-restraint and Karma = performing the right actions and duties, Iti = these three are, Pratistha = the foundation.

What is Tapas-penance?

To know ‘what’, there is a need to know ‘how much’.

Tapas is basically of three types: ‘Kayena, Vaca, Manasa’.

Tapas through body; tapas through speech; tapas through mind.

Chasing the riches of knowledge is tapas through body.

Brahma tells: One should chase those who know the secret of Adhyatma - spirituality, should serve them with reverence. It should be a permanent practice in life. By that, the spiritual knowledge gets firmly rooted within us also. That is the power of blessing of wise ones. It is enough that they look at us with their wide eyes, soon the window of spirituality within us opens up. The light of Adhyatma spreads full, within.

It is enough that they fondly touch our heads. Then the darkness veiling our life vanishes.

They pour down all their powers for us. For that reason, let our body be dedicated to serve them.

Serving the knowers verily is serving knowledge.

With all these the secret of spirituality gets understood, and the understood things stay on: gurususrusaya vidya.

The second one is the tapas through speech, truthfulness in speaking.

It is not telling lies; not speaking that which would hurt others. Apart from being truth, the speech should be dear and pleasant for others. It is the speech which would nurture the goodness of the society, and becomes pleasant for good people.

Restraint in the speech; fineness and politeness.

Speech is an imprint of the personality.

Speech becomes the reflection of the cultivation and the personality of the speaker.

Good words can’t come forth from a person not cultivated well.

A person is self- restrained who has restrained speech.

A person is polite, in whose talk there is a natural politeness.

A person is gentle, who is full of gentle words.

Such a person won’t speak arrogantly, haughty with pride, he won’t boast about himself, won’t speak hurting others.

Such a person who know the technique of speech, master of speech come to know the secret of spirituality; and what is known, remains on.

This is the Vagmaya Tapas-penance through speech:

*anudvegakaram vakyam satyam priyahitam ca yat*

The third one is the tapas through mind; a continuous reflection.

It is reflecting again and again and meditating upon the thing, which was heard during the study.

It is telling the lesson to himself, which was heard by himself; self-discourse.

This internal reflection and self-discourse verily is the tapa through mind.

To tell it to others is Para-Pravacana, discourse for others. This is again Vang-maya tapas, the penance through speech.

*svadhyayapravacane eveti nako maudgiyah*

*taddi tapastadhhi tapah*

For this, the first requirement is the purity of the mind.

Pure things stay in a pure mind; they get perceived.

If we start the studies with an already polluted mind, the same dirt will reflect in the lessons.

To read the text is not to see the things which are already stuffed in our mind.

It is to fill up our mind with those things which really are present in the texts.

For that, we should empty ourselves first.

One cannot become full, if he can’t empty himself.

There is no use pouring milk into a dirty bowl. The milk gets foul, that is all.

This is the major fault with the people of today who wish to study things; the lack of tapas of mind.

Smearing the dirt of the mind onto the text.

They are like a spoon in a sweet dish.

The sweet doesn’t seep into the spoon; it can never enter into; it remains only smeared to the spoon.

There is a saying of Bhima in Mahabharata:

*srotriyasyeva te rajan mandakasyavipascitah*

*anuvakahata buddhirnaisa tatvarthadarsim*

(Your mind is dumb, like a fool, despite having read Vedas. It has turned inert, learning by rote and has not seen the essence of the lessons.)

This is the danger of a faulty study.

Such a study is like dirt on our skin.

A mental act washing such dust, verily is the tapas through mind. Think very deep about things.

Don’t get under an illusion that you understand things quickly, soon after you hear. Think about things in depth that is tapas: tapa alocane.

For that, one should reflect again and again. Of the things heard, how much is understood? How much remains to be known? What are the advantages and disadvantages of the thing understood? All that should be reflected upon continuously.

For that reason, the ancients used to go into forest, quitting the environs of the busy city life of noise.

Because, there is the right atmosphere for reflecting in concentration. A pure atmosphere helps pure reflections.

By reflection, one should understand. The understood thing should turn inside and deep. It should be churned and filtered in the depth of the heart. Breaking word with word, one should dive deep into its depth.

Through all these, the secrecy of the spirituality gets deciphered; the known remains on.

Thus it was about Tapas. Now secondly about ‘Dama’.

The earlier ‘Ḍṁðṁ’, becomes ‘Pata’ - fall, if it is reversed. Similarly ‘Dama’ becomes ‘Mada’ - arrogance, if reversed. Shedding ‘Mada’ verily is ‘Dama’: damo madaviparyayah.

If the insolence ‘what more do I have to know! ’ goes away, it gets known that ‘I don’t know anything ‘.That verily is ‘Dama’.

In another way, Dama is self -restraint; a control over the senses.

*dama indriya nigrahah*

It is the politeness after shedding pride. The polite self-restraint.

The third is Karma.

What is Karma?

It is allegiance to duty; being action oriented; performing right actions.

Yes, all these.

Gita too tells the same:

*kuru karmaiva tasmat tvam purvaihi purvataram krtam*

And the Upanisat too tells;

*kurvanneveha karmani jui visecchatam samah*

Man should not become non-functional. He is not a philosopher, one who remains passive, saying that ‘let it be so, as God wills’.

One should live duty bound all through life.

*kuru bhungksva ca karma nuam niyatam*

*haripadavinampradhiya satatam*

This is the message of Acarya Madhva, regarding life: Do your duty, accept whatever comes. May the awareness of God’s feet be never lost’.

Which Karma-action, is duty bound Karma? Which is right Karma? What we do in our self interest is not Karma. What we do in the interest of others is the real Karma; imperative Karma.

The Karma done for our own sake, to make our living, is self bound Karma. The real Karma is the one, done for the sake of others, to serve the mass.

Karma is action suitable for the self; it is Svadharma - one’s own duty.

*sve-sve karmanyabhiratah*

*sva karmana tamabhyarca siddhim vindati mahavah*

*svadharme nidhanam sreyah*

Such statements come up often in Gita.

What is Svakarma? What is Parakarma?

The Karma, natural to one’s own disposition, is Svakarma.

That which is unnatural is Parakarma.

According to one’s own disposition one should act. This verily was called Caturvarnya: caturvarnyam maya srstam.

Varna means one’s attributes, disposition, character.

When we get to know someone’s character, we comment, ‘he has come out with his true color’.

Varna division and character division are the same.

Everyone cannot have the same disposition.

Doing right actions according to one’s own given characteristics is Svadharama.

Therefore, Caturmukha says: ‘Don’t be inactive. Don’t get into an illusion that a Jnani - wise one, doesn’t need Karma. Be action oriented. But don’t be Karma-fanatic, blindly following somebody else’s way of doing things. Be work-oriented according to your own dispositions.

Karma takes to Jnana - knowledge. Do such Karma that carried us towards God.

So be Karma-oriented, therefore, be knowledge-oriented.

Do your Karma having knowledge. Do Karma to have knowledge: karmana jnanamatanoti.

Let Karma be the cause of the expansion of knowledge. Don’t be a Karmaslug. Don’t be languid lost in mere Veda-studies.

There won’t be knowledge, nor action in a passive person.

The studies too are a kind of internal action, aren’t they?

There is no Jnana without Karma.

There is no karma without Jnana.

When a work is done after understanding it, it becomes the real karma.

Therefore, do things after understanding, doing it on and on, understand more and more about It.

*tapo damah karmeti pratistha*

With all these coming together, the secrecy of Adhyatma, spirituality gets known. And the known remains on.

What remains should grow further.

It should flourish and fill our bowls.

What should be done for that?

*vedah sarvangani satyamayatanam*

Vedah = vedas, Sarvarigani = auxiliaries to vedas, (and) Satyam = truth investigating texts, (are) Ayatanam = shelter of this secret.

These three are the ayatanas of the spiritual knowledge, the living places.

Here we access to that secret.

Encircle that house, collect that secret.

For that break the hindering wall, break and rush in.

What are the Vedas?

They are the texts, that enable our mind to understand the things, the very things which our mind can’t understand, that which can’t be understood by perception, which can’t be hypothesized by logic.

They are the texts that activate the awareness of God.

Vedangas are the texts, that were created as supplementary works to understand the Veda-scriptures.

With all these around, if there is a confusion regarding the right meaning of the Vedas, the texts called ‘Satya’, Conclusively proclaim that such and such is the essence of the vedas, and that, this is the internal ‘Satya’ truth of the Vedas.

They verily are the Mimamsa Sastras - investigative philosophies.

Together, these are source of the knowledge; the science that familiarize us with the understanding of Brahman.

In that case, how many are there?

There are eight Vedas. Seven Vedangas. Three Satyas.

Thus, there are eighteen sources of wisdom.

We have heard that there are four vedas. What does eight Vedas mean?.

It is true there are four vedas. But it also true that there are eight Vedas.

The Vedas not composed by anybody are four: Rg-veda, Yajur veda, Sama-veda and Atharva-Veda.

The Vedas that are composed are four: Pancaratra, Mularamayana, Mahabharata and eighteen Puranas.

Thus, together there are eight Vedas.

Pancaratra, Mularamayana and Mahabharata are the three histories.

*itihasapuranah pancamo vedanam vedah*

Itihasapuranas are the fifth Veda. In that fifth, there are four divisions. Thus, the four became eight.

What are the seven Vedangas?

The well known Vedangas are six: Siksa, Vyakarana, Nirukta, chandas, Jyotisya and Kalpa.

These are the Sadangas - six auxiliaries.

Adding Smrtis which follow the Vedas, the Vedangas are seven. What is Siksa?

Siksa is phonetics.

Syllables originate from umbilical cords, spring from the throat and become discernible.

Siksa is the text that explains, as to from which site syllable should be pronounced and how it should be pronounced.

It is training in the word pronunciation, prior to the explanation of meanings.

Grammar is the science that explains, how the pronounced word was formed, which is the root, combining which suffix or prefix, it was formed.

It helps in deciding, which is a proper word, and which is improper.

While grammar puts on the armor of regulations to the words, Nirukta works on examining the history of the language.

Nirukta means etymology.

Language gains beauty due to etymological definitions.

There is a history of culture of the race, behind the definitions of the words. By Nirukta, each word opens up along with its cultural background.

Only by definitions, the study of the etymological meanings, deeper than in the common usage, is possible.

Especially in Vedic studies, the role of etymology is unique.

For instance, let us take the word ‘Agni’.

Its common meaning is fire.

But the meaning derived from etymology is: ‘the power that imparts movement to an inert thing’: agam nayati iti agnih.

That is why, Yaska, author of Nirukta, tells that, each word of the vedas is understood by etymological meaning:

*Aksarasamyat-nirbruyat. Nacaiva na nirbruyat.*

The fourth Vedanga is Chandas. Prosody.

It is related to Mantra in Verse form.

The knowledge of Chandas- meter, is necessary to know the move of the Verse.

It is also necessary to know, which Chandas should be sung in which fixed notes.

There is a relationship between the seven Chandas and seven musical notes.

Knowledge of the Chandas is important to recollect the Deities of the specific Chandas.

Fifth aspect is Jyotisa - Astronomy.

It is the science of the calculation of proper and precious time.

To perform rituals at the proper time, knowing the right lunar day, week and the stars, the help of this science is very essential.

The sixth is Kalpa, text on rituals. It is the science that explains the details of the various rituals to be performed at different times.

There are Kalpasutras - aphorisms on rituals, that explain rituals related to different schools.

Srautasutras, Grhyasutra, Sulbasutras, these are variations of Kalpasutras.

These six auxiliaries, and the Dharmasastras, Smrti texts written by sages like Manu, Yajnavalkya and others, combining together, became the seven Vedangas.

Dharmasastras are the constitutions of the respective periods.

The ancients don’t agree that these Smrti texts are valid for all times. Along with the changes of time, Dharmasastras too have been changing. Because they envelop the social Dharmas, codes, of the corresponding time.

As the society changes, the social Dharma too changes.

Only the Sanatana Dharma propounded by the Vedas is the eternal truth, which is above Time and Space.

That is why they are Sanatanas — perpetual everlasting.

One who understands these, the Vedas open p to him with ease. One who knows the parts, easily knows the whole.

So, there are eight Vedas; Seven Vedangas. Together it is fifteen. The next is Mimamsa Sastra: satyam.

*mimamsa satyamucyate*

Most explain that ‘Satya’ means telling truth.

Why did this ‘telling truth’ came in the list of the texts?

Along with the Vedas and Vedangas, this too must be text, deciding the meaning of Vedas, isn’t it?

This doubt gets the answer from these words of testimony: mimamsa satyamucyate.

The science that decides that this is the specific meaning of Veda,

according to the beginning and conclusion of the topic discussed, verily is Mimamsa Sastra.

The word ‘Satya’ is used here in the specific meaning.

It is the science that decides the true meaning of the Veda in accordance with logic.

There are three Mimamsas that expound the meaning of Veda. They are, Karma Mimamsa, Deva Mimamsa and Brahma Mimamsa.

The science that explains the Karma section is Karma Mimamsa. These are the Karmasutras of the sage Jaimini.

The science that explains the hierarchy of the Devas is Deva Mimamsa. It is not available now.

The science that integrates all the Vedas with God, is Brahma Mimamsa. They are the Brahma Sutras of Badarayana.

If these three are combined with the previous fifteen, it becomes eighteen Vidyas in total.

These are the living abodes of God. The source texts to be studied to gain the right awareness.

## Prose: nine

The secret lesson has been taught. The aspects of the worship that should be in the practitioner, worshipper have been explained. And the related source texts too have been mentioned.

The three faces of the Upanisats also have been referred. Caturmukha, who concludes after briefly telling about all the things to be told, ends the preaching with the concluding Mantra of Phalasruti — the word of reward:

*yo va etamevam vedapahatya papmanamanante*

*svarge loke jyeye prtitisthati pratitisthati \|\|9\|\|*

(The one who understands this Brahmavidya, shedding off evil, he in a blissful undestroyable world, in God who is pre eminent supreme amongst all, finds the abode, finds the abode.)

By this knowledge, the very Salvation is the reward, tells Caturmukha: yo va etamevam veda.

One who gets to know this knowledge, in this way gets God! What does it mean? ‘One who gets to know in this way’.

It means such a person, who knows Veda, Vedanga, Mimamsa along with the Tapas, self-restraint and right action.

To know does not mean to know by mere reading. To know is to see directly through the inner eye, progressing in the path of practice by constant reflection.

To know is to step into the stage of realization through worship.

The word ‘Vai’ in this sentence, does the job of stressing the comprehension: yo vai...

Only such a person who gains this vision can attain Salvation, is the intension here: ya eva veda; sa eva pratitisthati.

There is only one path for salvation; the path of God-awareness. In it there are no sub-lanes, no cross-roads. No easy inroads.

*tameva viditvatimrtyumeti*

Yah Vai = he who, Evam = in this way, Veda = realizes after understanding, Etam = this knowledge of Brahman...

What does he become?

Firstly, he sheds out the evil tilled in him.

Apahatya = shedding out, Papmanam = the evil...

First, unburdening the heavy evil which was dragging us down, and then climbing up.

That too, what an evil!

An evil huge like a hill; an evil collected and preserved over many past lives.

Under the heavy hill, we turn into atoms and get lost under their load.

The load is seen, but its carrier gets lost.

First, the job of us searching ourselves, should be undertaken.

For that, pushing aside all these mess and dirt, we should move inside.

Finally, the very hill of evil should be shattered.

Where is the Vajrayudha — diamond weapon, to shatter the hill? God’s awareness verily is that diamond weapon.

Otherwise there is no release from the mound of evil.

Evil goes on getting collected.

Hill grows bigger; we continue to remain smaller.

As we shrink smaller, the weight of the evil grows heavier.

As we grow bigger, the weight of the hill of evil, gets lighter.

There is only one tool to make it lighter. It is the knowledge of God.

Through knowledge the evil is brought down. What is the next move?

*anante svarge loke*

Anante = (in) endless, Svarge = blissful, Loke = divine place, (Pratitisthati = he stays permanently).

Hence a reference to svarga, heaven comes in...

But this is not the heaven where one keeps returning again and again. It is an eternal heaven.

That is why it is referred as anante loke.

It is not a heaven which can be reached and returned from.

It is the heaven to be stayed on for ever.

It is the Svarga - heaven, which won’t be lost, it is an unending heaven.

What does Svarga mean?

The happiness of the Soul is called ‘Svah’.

Svarga is such a world, that grants such a bliss for the Soul. When the door of the blissful Soul opens up, does one fall down into the ocean of mundane life? No.

Such a joy cannot be in a recurring Svarga.

Therefore, Salvation is very much the real heaven.

That is the real Loka too.

‘Loka’ means a state of being wisdom itself: lokr arsane.

How can this material heaven be a non-material knowledge itself?

Non-material Loka, is the real Loka.

Salvation verily is a Svarga. Salvation verily is Loka.

Only Moksa - salvation, is the endless Svargaloka.

Kathaka tells about this:

*svarge loke na bhayam kincanasti*

*na tatra tvam na jaraya bibheti*

Naciketa tells Yama: In heaven there is no fear. No trouble from you too. No problem of ageing also.

Salvation is the only fearless state, where there is no ageing, no frightening things.

Ignorance is fear. Knowledge verily is fearlessness.

Mundane life is fear. Salvation verily is fearlessness.

That is the real Svarga, the heaven that gives the self-bliss.

What happens in such a heaven?

*svarge loke jyeye prati+tisthati*

Here we see two types of word-division: loke+ajyeye and loke+jyeye.

In the printed text of Talavakara Brahmana, it has been divided as ‘ Ajyeye’, in the word-sub-text.

But in the texts of most of the commentators, it is ‘ Jyeye’.

Both are alright, the meaning is the same.

‘Jyeye’ means, that which is greater than all.

‘ Ajyeye’ means, that one, than which no other is greater than.

*na vidyate jyeyam yasmat*

Therefore, ‘Jyeya’ very much is ‘Ajyeya’.

*ajyeye pratitisthati*

A+jyeye = in God, whom no other is greater than, (he), Prati+tistati = stays for ever.

‘Ajeya’ is very much ‘Jyeya’.

*jyeye prati+tisthati*

Jyeye = in God, the greatest, (he) Prati+tistati = stays for ever.

The repetition appearing at the end, indicating the end of the Upanisat, justifies the things told by the Upanisat, by stressing them, pratitisthati. Pratitisthati.

(A)jyeye = in God, the greatest, (he) Prati+tistati= stays on, Prati+tistati= stays on.

Having a joyous form, with blissful God, he stays on forever.

Thus, with the Mantra telling about the fruits of this study, the fourth section ends. And the Upanisat seen by the Sages, Talavakaras, also ends.
